### [1] [CoVLM-Bench: A Real-World Benchmark for Cooperative Driving Question Answering and Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35823)
*Kang Yang,Shuai Liu,Hang Li,Yance Fang,Deying Li,Yongcai Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CoVLM-Bench is a new benchmark for evaluating vision-language models (VLMs) on cooperative driving question answering (CDQA) and cooperative planning (CP) using vehicle-infrastructure paired scenes. CoVLM-Drive, a unified VLM baseline, achieves lower planning error than V2X planners through CDQA adaptation and rationale supervision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current VLMs in autonomous driving are limited to ego-centric views. Infrastructure-side observations provide additional perspectives but are typically transformed into geometric representations rather than being directly integrated into VLMs. There's no unified benchmark for evaluating both question answering and trajectory planning on real-world vehicle-infrastructure cooperative scenes.

Method: CoVLM-Bench provides 2,196 paired frames with 35,136 CDQA annotations and CP tasks predicting six waypoints over a 3-second horizon. Annotations combine model-assisted drafting, record-based computation, and human verification. CoVLM-Drive is a unified VLM baseline that directly uses paired vehicle-infrastructure views for both CDQA and CP tasks, with three-part rationales as auxiliary supervision.

Result: CDQA adaptation improves answer accuracy. CoVLM-Drive achieves lower Final Displacement Error (FDE) compared to V2X planners. Both QA initialization and rationale supervision each reduce planning error.

Conclusion: CoVLM-Bench and CoVLM-Drive together provide a framework for training and comparing VLMs on cooperative scene understanding and trajectory planning in vehicle-infrastructure scenarios, demonstrating the value of integrating infrastructure-side views directly into VLMs.

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) have made substantial progress in autonomous driving, but their success has primarily been studied in ego-centric scenes. Infrastructure-side observations provide views beyond the ego vehicle's field of view, yet conventional cooperative-driving systems typically transform them into geometric representations for downstream perception and planning. Directly incorporating these views into VLMs offers an opportunity to improve cooperative scene understanding and trajectory planning. However, question answering and trajectory planning have not been jointly evaluated on the same real-world vehicle-infrastructure scenes. We present CoVLM-Bench, a benchmark for cooperative driving question answering (CDQA) and cooperative planning (CP) on vehicle-infrastructure paired scenes. CoVLM-Bench provides scene-grounded CDQA annotations, three-part rationales as auxiliary supervision, and future trajectory targets derived from recorded ego motion. It contains 2,196 paired frames with 35,136 CDQA annotations, while CP predicts six waypoints over a three-second horizon. The annotations combine model-assisted drafting, record-based computation, and human verification. Built upon CoVLM-Bench, we introduce CoVLM-Drive, a unified VLM baseline that directly uses paired views for both CDQA and CP. Experiments show that CDQA adaptation improves answer accuracy and that CoVLM-Drive reaches a lower FDE than the compared V2X planners; QA initialization and rationale supervision each reduce planning error. Together, CoVLM-Bench and CoVLM-Drive support the training and comparison of VLMs for cooperative scene understanding and planning.

</details>


### [2] [AerialDojo-200K: A Large-Scale Benchmark Suite for Open-World Aerial Object-Goal Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36066)
*Tongtong Feng,Xin Wang,Haoran Hou,Ren Wang,Weiran Wang,Shaokai Zhu,Ziqi Jia,Hao Wang,Yu-Wei Zhan,Zongyuan Wu,Jinghao Cui,Wenwu Zhu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: AerialDojo-200K is a large-scale benchmark suite for open-world aerial object-goal search, featuring 42 simulation scenes across 4 scene families, 205,732 task instances (semantic-goal and image-goal), and a unified evaluation framework with in-distribution and out-of-distribution scenes. Evaluation of multiple multimodal LLMs reveals significant limitations in current aerial agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Open-world aerial object-goal search lacks large-scale, standardized benchmarks. Existing benchmarks are small, environment-specific, and have heterogeneous action spaces/data formats, hindering large-scale training and cross-benchmark evaluation. This limits the scalability and generalizability of aerial agents.

Method: The authors constructed 42 simulation scenes across 4 scene families (urban, natural, infrastructure, disaster) and 21 scene types. Twelve annotators spent two months manually annotating 109 landmarks, 2099 target objects, and 2099 object anchors. They created 205,732 task instances across Base, Standard, and Long-Horizon settings, each with collision-free reference trajectories and multi-view video recordings. A unified evaluation framework with 21 in-distribution and 21 out-of-distribution scenes was developed.

Result: The benchmark is 3x larger in scenes and 18.7x larger in task instances than the largest existing benchmark. Evaluation of five open-source and four closed-source multimodal large language models showed that there is still a long way to go toward achieving general-purpose aerial agents.

Conclusion: AerialDojo-200K provides a comprehensive benchmark for advancing aerial object-goal search research, but current state-of-the-art multimodal LLMs still struggle significantly with this task, indicating substantial room for improvement in aerial agent capabilities.

Abstract: Open-world aerial object-goal search is a foundational yet challenging task, requiring aerial agents to autonomously explore large-scale, unstructured three-dimensional environments and reach target objects specified by semantic descriptions or reference images, rather than following route-specific instructions. However, research in this task remains at a nascent stage and relies on small, environment-specific benchmarks with heterogeneous action spaces and data formats. These limitations hinder large-scale training and cross-benchmark evaluation, constraining the scalability and generalizability of aerial agents. To address this problem, we propose AerialDojo-200K, a large-scale benchmark suite for open-world aerial object-goal search, with 3 times as many scenes and 18.7 times as many task instances as the largest existing benchmark for this task. Specifically, we construct 42 simulation scenes spanning four scene families and 21 scene types, including 18 urban, 12 natural, six infrastructure, and six disaster scenes. To ensure data quality, 12 annotators spent two months manually annotating 109 landmarks, 2099 target objects, and 2099 object anchors across these scenes. We further construct 205,732 task instances, comprising over 100K semantic-goal and over 100K image-goal instances across Base, Standard, and Long-Horizon settings. Each task instance includes a collision-free reference trajectory and corresponding multi-view video recordings. We also develop a unified evaluation framework with a scene partition comprising 21 in-distribution scenes and 21 out-of-distribution scenes. Finally, our evaluation of five open-source and four closed-source multimodal large language models reveals that there is still a long way to go toward achieving general-purpose aerial agents. All can be found at https://fengtt42.github.io/AerialDojo/.

</details>


### [3] [HERO: Histology Encoder for Robust Representation in Oncology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35943)
*Zhi Li,Eghbal Amidi,Yating Cheng,Tyson Dawson,Gorkem Can Ates,Shuzhen Kuang,Norsang Lama,Md Ashequr Rahman,Zhiying Lu,Elisabeth K. Kong,Milan Radovich,David Spetzler,Matthew Oberley,George W. Sledge,Ming Chen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: HERO (Histology Encoder for Robust Representation in Oncology) is a ViT-G/14 pathology foundation model trained with DINO and iBOT objectives on 500 million tiles from ~575,000 whole-slide images, designed to be robust to acquisition shift (different hospitals, scanners, staining protocols) while maintaining strong representation quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current pathology foundation models are not robust to acquisition shift - they embed acquisition factors (hospital, scanner, stain) alongside biological information, which can cause downstream errors and hinder safe clinical adoption. Existing benchmarks focus on representation quality but not robustness.

Method: ViT-G/14 architecture trained with DINO and iBOT self-supervised objectives, refined with high-resolution Gram anchoring on a morphology-balanced corpus of 500 million tiles from approximately 575,000 clinical whole-slide images.

Result: HERO shows the strongest robustness to center, scanner, and stain variation among state-of-the-art foundation models; performs comparably on tile-level classification, segmentation, and gene-expression prediction; ranks first on average across 39 slide-level clinical tasks; has the best average rank across six benchmark frameworks.

Conclusion: HERO successfully balances robustness to acquisition shift with representation quality, achieving both safe clinical adoption potential and strong performance across multiple downstream tasks.

Abstract: Foundation models trained on large pathology image corpora now provide strong, transferable representations for computational pathology. Over the past few years a series of such models has been released, each trained on more slides than the last; on standard classification and segmentation benchmarks, the leading models are now separated by small margins. In clinical use, however, the foundation model is applied to images from hospitals, scanners, and staining protocols outside its training data. Encoders generally embed these acquisition factors alongside biological information, which may introduce downstream errors and hinder safe clinical adoption. A pathology foundation model should therefore be robust to acquisition shift without giving up representation quality, yet robustness is seldom the axis along which models are compared. In this report, we introduce HERO (Histology Encoder for Robust Representation in Oncology), a ViT-G/14 pathology foundation model trained with the DINO and iBOT objectives and refined with high-resolution Gram anchoring on a morphology-balanced corpus of 500 million tiles from approximately 575,000 clinical whole-slide images. Across the evaluated public benchmarks, HERO shows the strongest robustness to center, scanner, and stain variation among the compared state-of-the-art foundation models, performs comparably on tile-level classification, segmentation, and gene-expression prediction, ranks first on average across 39 evaluated slide-level clinical tasks, and, under an equal-weighted framework-level analysis, has the best average rank across the six benchmark frameworks.

</details>


### [4] [HEIR: Learning Human-Entity Interactions with Functional Roles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35955)
*Di Wen,Wenhao Guo,Yuedong Tan,Yun Huang,Minheng Wu,Zhihang Chen,Haiwen Sun,Fei Teng,Zhiyuan Gao,Yufeng Zhang,Yuanhao Luo,Jingqi Zhang,Yufan Chen,Junwei Zheng,Ruiping Liu,Jiale Wei,Kailun Yang,Kunyu Peng*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: HEIR is a new image benchmark for complete grounded participant-role sets across object, interpersonal, and self-directed interactions, containing 18,730 images with 6 roles, 105 actions, and 437 nouns. CoRISP is a new method that uses shared entity identities to combine role-conditioned evidence and predict normalized participant-role sets, achieving state-of-the-art results on HEIR and V-COCO datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard HOI (Human-Object Interaction) metrics only score individual links, leaving complete event composition undermeasured. Understanding human-entity interactions requires recovering each person-action event's participants, roles, and shared identities to support embodied agents in anticipating and coordinating in shared environments.

Method: HEIR benchmark: 18,730 images with 6 roles, 105 actions, 437 nouns, featuring shared entities, role changes, and repeated fillers (51.6% multiple actors, 62.1% multiple actions). CoRISP uses shared entity identities to combine role-conditioned evidence and predict normalized participant-role sets, with cardinality and role-multiplicity potentials coupling assignments through event size and role composition with exact per-event normalization.

Result: CoRISP outperforms 16 baselines on HEIR: +2.87 Set mAP on repeated-role events and +3.82 Set mAP on shared-participant images. On V-COCO, CoRISP achieves 73.72/76.23 role AP and 61.06/68.59 complete-set AP under Scenarios 1/2 for two-slot actions.

Conclusion: Learning and evaluating event composition alongside individual relations provides significant value. The complete event structure matters beyond individual relation detection, as demonstrated by the performance gains on events with multiple actors, multiple actions, and shared participants.

Abstract: Understanding human-entity interactions requires recovering each person-action event's participants, roles, and shared identities. This structure can support embodied agents by clarifying who acts on which entities and how, informing anticipation and coordination in shared environments. Standard HOI metrics score individual links, leaving complete event composition undermeasured. We introduce HEIR (Human-Entity Interactions with Functional Roles), an image benchmark for complete grounded participant-role sets across object, interpersonal, and self-directed interactions. It contains 18,730 images, six roles, 105 actions, and 437 nouns, with shared entities, role changes, and repeated fillers; 51.6% of images contain multiple actors and 62.1% contain multiple actions. HEIR pairs relation AP with complete-set AP and structural evaluation. We also introduce CoRISP (Compositional Role-aware Interaction Set Prediction), which uses shared entity identities to combine role-conditioned evidence and predict normalized participant-role sets. Cardinality and role-multiplicity potentials couple assignments through event size and role composition, with exact per-event normalization. Across 16 baselines, relation and complete-event rankings diverge even after aligning action weights. CoRISP leads the evaluated systems on repeated-role events and shared-participant images in HEIR by 2.87 and 3.82 Set mAP points, respectively. On V-COCO, CoRISP achieves 73.72/76.23 role AP and 61.06/68.59 complete-set AP on two-slot actions under Scenarios 1/2. These results show the value of learning and evaluating event composition alongside individual relations. The code and dataset are publicly available at https://github.com/Kratos-Wen/HEIR.

</details>


### [5] [Systematic Multi-Agent Vision-and-Language Navigation: Formulation, Benchmark, and Method](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35965)
*Yunzhe Xu,Zhe Liu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper presents the first systematic formalization of Multi-Agent Vision-and-Language Navigation (MAVLN) as a constrained coordination problem, where teams of robots must complete missions with subtasks carrying dependency and resource constraints. The authors introduce TRISS, a coordination-ready navigation system with LLM-based scheduling, shared topological memory, and conflict-aware execution. Experiments on a dataset of 11,724 episodes across 145 scenes establish TRISS as a baseline while revealing substantial challenges in multi-agent coordination under task constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Vision-and-Language Navigation research focuses on single agent scenarios, while real-world applications require teams of robots to tackle tasks beyond individual capabilities. This work addresses the gap by formalizing multi-agent VLN as a constrained coordination problem with dependency and resource constraints (presence locks and holding chains).

Method: The authors propose TRISS, a coordination-ready navigation system with three key components: (1) LLM-based subtask scheduler for task decomposition and allocation, (2) Shared topological memory that aggregates each agent's exploration into team knowledge, and (3) Conflict-aware execution mechanism that realizes simultaneous intentions as collision-free routes. The task is instantiated as MAVLN dataset with 11,724 episodes across 145 scenes, teams of up to four agents, and three instruction regimes.

Result: TRISS is established as a comprehensive baseline for the MAVLN task. Experiments reveal substantial room for improvement across scheduling, planning, and execution dimensions, highlighting the significant challenges of coordinating multiple agents under task constraints.

Conclusion: This work provides the first systematic formalization of multi-agent VLN and demonstrates that coordination under dependency and resource constraints remains a challenging problem. TRISS serves as a foundational baseline, while the MAVLN dataset and constraint-aware metrics provide a benchmark for future research in multi-agent vision-and-language navigation.

Abstract: Vision-and-Language Navigation (VLN) has largely focused on a single agent following a single instruction, yet many real-world applications require teams of robots to tackle tasks beyond the capabilities of any individual agent. We present Systematic Multi-Agent Vision-and-Language Navigation, providing, to our knowledge, the first systematic formalization of multi-agent VLN as a constrained coordination problem: each mission consists of subtasks carrying dependency and resource constraints (presence locks and holding chains). A verified four-stage crafting pipeline instantiates the task as MAVLN, comprising 11,724 episodes across 145 scenes with teams of up to four agents under three instruction regimes, accompanied by tailored constraint-aware metrics. We further present TRISS, a coordination-ready navigation system coupling an LLM-based subtask scheduler, a shared topological memory that turns each agent's exploration into team knowledge, and a conflict-aware execution mechanism that realizes simultaneous intentions as collision-free routes. Extensive experiments establish TRISS as a comprehensive baseline and reveal substantial room for improvement across scheduling, planning, and execution, highlighting the challenges of coordinating under MAVLN task constraints. Project page: https://xyz9911.github.io/mavln.

</details>


### [6] [What Comes Next? Omni-StoryBench for Evaluating Story-Grounded Omnimodal Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37317)
*Sieun Hyeon,Yejoon Lee,Mintaek Lim,Woojin Kim,Jaeik Kim,Jaeyoung Do*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Omni-StoryBench is a benchmark evaluating omnimodal story generation coherence across image, narration, and speech. It contains 900 validated story transitions from children's books. Evaluation across 32 baseline configurations shows orchestration with strong VLM planning performs best, while image generation and visual continuity form the main bottleneck.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current multimodal evaluation focuses on isolated modalities (text, image, speech) independently, but fails to assess whether outputs from different modalities form a coherent shared event. The authors argue that individually plausible outputs may not express coherence when combined, which is critical for applications like story generation.

Method: The benchmark provides current storybook pages and structured next-page conditions, requiring models to generate the next illustration, narration, and spoken character utterance. Systems are evaluated using modality-specific metrics plus LLM-as-a-judge rubrics assessing: context preservation, condition following, reference consistency, and cross-modal coherence. 32 baseline configurations were tested across orchestration, semi-orchestration, and native any-to-any paradigms.

Result: Orchestration with strong VLM planning proves most reliable. Current native omnimodal models struggle with output completeness and controllability. Text-side performance correlates with image and speech quality, but image generation and visual continuity is the clearest bottleneck.

Conclusion: Omni-StoryBench serves as a system-level benchmark measuring coherent omnimodal generation beyond isolated modality quality. The key bottleneck identified is image generation and visual continuity, suggesting future work should focus on improving visual coherence in multimodal story generation systems.

Abstract: Omnimodal evaluation should go beyond independent text, image, and speech production: individually plausible outputs may not express a coherent shared event. We introduce Omni-StoryBench, a story-grounded omnimodal benchmark evaluating whether models can coherently continue stories across image, narration, and speech. Each instance provides a current storybook page and structured next-page conditions, requiring models to generate the next illustration, narration, and spoken character utterance. Omni-StoryBench contains 900 rigorously validated story transitions from openly licensed children's books, with ground-truth next-page references and speech metadata. We evaluate systems with modality-specific metrics and consistency-centered LLM-as-a-judge rubrics for context preservation, condition following, reference consistency, and cross-modal coherence. Across 32 baseline configurations spanning orchestration, semi-orchestration, and native any-to-any paradigms, we find orchestration with strong VLM planning most reliable, while current native omnimodal models often struggle with output completeness and controllability. Our analysis shows text-side performance is associated with image and speech quality, but image generation and visual continuity form the clearest observed bottleneck among the evaluated configurations. These results position Omni-StoryBench as a system-level benchmark measuring coherent omnimodal generation beyond isolated modality quality.

</details>


### [7] [Persistence Forcing: Exploiting Feature Specialization in Pixel-Space Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36014)
*Chong Wang,Zixuan Fu,Shiqi Huang,Siyuan Yang,Hao Cheng,Bihan Wen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper introduces heterogeneous refinement in pixel-space diffusion Transformers, where different feature groups receive distinct refinement budgets across depth. This creates two specialized groups: persistent features (encoding global structure with sparse refinement) and active features (encoding local details with frequent refinement). The proposed Persistence Forcing (PerF) method allows persistent features to continuously condition active features during generation, improving coherence of global structure while complementing classifier-free guidance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current pixel-space DiTs uniformly refine hidden representations across depth, but natural images have inherent multi-level granularity - global structure can be compact while local textures need richer representations. This mismatch leads to inefficient use of computation and representation capacity.

Method: 1) Heterogeneous refinement: assign different feature groups distinct refinement budgets across transformer depth
2) Emergent feature specialization: sparse refinement → global structure (persistent features), frequent refinement → local details (active features)
3) Persistence Forcing (PerF): persistent features continuously condition actively refined features, allowing stable global information to guide fine detail refinement
4) The conditioning interaction induces meaningful guidance direction promoting coherent global structure

Result: - PerF-L achieves FID 1.91 on ImageNet 256×256, approaching JIT-H's 1.86 with only half the parameters
- PerF-H achieves FID 1.63 on ImageNet 256×256
- PerF-H achieves FID 1.76 on ImageNet 512×512

Conclusion: Heterogeneous refinement in pixel-space DiTs enables emergent feature specialization between global structure and local details. PerF successfully exploits this organization to achieve state-of-the-art or competitive FID scores with fewer parameters, demonstrating the effectiveness of conditioning persistent features to guide active feature refinement during generation.

Abstract: Pixel-space diffusion Transformers (DiTs) directly operate on high-dimensional visual data, yet their hidden representations typically undergo uniform refinement across depth. Natural images, however, are inherently organized at different levels of granularity. Global structure can often be represented compactly, whereas local textures and fine details require richer representations. Motivated by this, we introduce heterogeneous refinement in pixel-space DiTs, assigning different feature groups distinct refinement budgets across depth. Consequently, an ordered feature specialization emerges: sparsely refined features predominantly encode global visual structure, whereas more frequently refined features increasingly specialize toward localized, high-frequency details. We refer to these two groups as persistent and active features, respectively. Building on this emergent specialization, we introduce Persistence Forcing (PerF), which explicitly exploits this persistent--active feature organization for pixel-space image generation. This enables persistent features to continuously condition actively refined features, allowing stable global information to guide the ongoing refinement of finer visual details. During generative sampling, this interaction further induces a meaningful guidance direction that promotes coherent global structure and naturally complements classifier-free guidance. On ImageNet $256\times256$, PerF-L achieves FID of $1.91$, approaching $1.86$ of JiT-H with only half the parameters, while PerF-H further achieves FID of $1.63$ and $1.76$ on ImageNet $256\times256$ and $512\times512$, respectively.

</details>


### [8] [CoDimRecon: Agentic Reconstruction of Sim-Ready 3D Scenes with Deformable Curves, Surfaces, and Volumes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36024)
*Shuzhao Xie,Lelin Wang,Guying Lin,Zhi Wang,Minchen Li*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CoDimRecon is an agentic framework that reconstructs editable 3D scenes containing rigid, articulated, and deformable objects from multi-view RGB observations, producing simulation-ready assets with behavioral testing for robotic applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing 3D reconstruction methods largely assume rigid objects, leaving a gap for deformables whose simulation-ready geometry depends on dimensionality (curves, surfaces, volumes) and require models beyond elasticity. This limits robotics, gaming, and immersive applications that need realistic deformable object simulation.

Method: CoDimRecon uses an agentic framework with scene-level geometric priors for scale/layout and object-level generated meshes for detailed geometry. Articulated rigid objects are decomposed into movable parts with explicit joints. For deformables, category-wise agent sessions reconstruct: curves as centerlines with radii, surfaces as manifold shells with thickness, and volumes as watertight solids. Reusable simulator skills initialize physical models, while agent-guided behavioral tests expose mismatches and trigger targeted revisions.

Result: On Replica and ScanNet++ scenes, CoDimRecon achieves competitive compositional reconstruction accuracy while additionally producing deformable assets for rod, shell, and solid simulation. Robot interactions are demonstrated across all three representations, including controlled paper-folding where behavioral testing motivates plastic bending modeling.

Conclusion: CoDimRecon successfully extends simulation-ready 3D reconstruction to handle deformable objects across multiple representations (curves, surfaces, volumes), with integrated behavioral testing that enables physically accurate robotic interactions. The agentic approach and reusable simulator skills provide a promising framework for comprehensive scene reconstruction in robotics and simulation applications.

Abstract: Reconstructing simulation-ready 3D scenes from real-world observations enables robotics, gaming, and immersive applications, yet existing methods largely assume rigid objects. This leaves an important gap for deformables, whose simulation-ready geometry depends on dimensionality (curves, surfaces, or volumes) and whose behavior may require models beyond elasticity. We present CoDimRecon, an agentic framework that reconstructs editable scenes containing rigid, articulated, and deformable objects from multi-view RGB observations. Scene-level geometric priors ground scale and layout, while object-level generated meshes guide the agent toward detailed, compact geometry; articulated rigid objects are decomposed into movable parts with explicit joints. For deformables, category-wise agent sessions reconstruct curves as centerlines with radii, surfaces as manifold shells with thickness, and volumes as watertight solids for volumetric meshing. Reusable simulator skills initialize compatible physical models and parameters, while agent-guided behavioral tests expose mismatches and trigger targeted revisions of motion, geometry, numerics, or material modeling. On evaluated Replica and ScanNet++ scenes, CoDimRecon achieves competitive compositional reconstruction accuracy while additionally producing deformable assets for rod, shell, and solid simulation. We further demonstrate robot interactions across all three representations, including a controlled paper-folding case in which behavioral testing motivates plastic bending.

</details>


### [9] [MG-Thinker: Bi-Axial Self-Reflection for Multi-Image Reasoning Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37374)
*Heyu Huang,Chi Chen,Zonghao Guo,Yuhua Li,Maosong Sun,Ruixuan Li*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: MG-Thinker is a post-training RL framework for multi-image reasoning grounding (MRG) that introduces hierarchical reasoning and addresses heterogeneous task-sample difficulties through BiA-DAPO, achieving state-of-the-art performance on MRG benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RL-based approaches for multi-image reasoning grounding overlook two intrinsic characteristics: (1) a coarse-to-fine hierarchical reasoning pattern, and (2) heterogeneously distributed task-sample difficulties. This limits the ability to perform pixel-precise localization in real-world multi-image contexts.

Method: The paper proposes MG-Thinker, a post-training RL framework featuring: (1) A curated 25K MRG dataset with task-adaptive Chain-of-Thought (CoT) annotations that elicit multi-perspective evidence before conclusion; (2) Bi-Axial DAPO (BiA-DAPO), which decomposes rollout advantages along intra-group signal axis and inter-group competence axis through two complementary mechanisms, using a defined candidate pool for stable group-level statistics.

Result: MG-Thinker achieves state-of-the-art performance on multi-image reasoning grounding while consistently improving generalization across multi-image understanding and diverse multimodal benchmarks.

Conclusion: The hierarchical reasoning paradigm with task-adaptive CoT annotations, combined with BiA-DAPO's handling of heterogeneous difficulties, effectively advances multi-image reasoning grounding capabilities and generalization.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) has recently delivered substantial gains in multimodal reasoning, opening a promising route for fine-grained visual perception. Yet for multi-image reasoning grounding (MRG), reasoning over real-world multi-image contexts toward pixel-precise localization, existing RL-based approaches overlook two characteristics intrinsic to this paradigm: a coarse-to-fine hierarchical reasoning pattern, and heterogeneously distributed task--sample difficulties. In this work, we present MG-Thinker, a post-training RL framework that advances a new MRG paradigm featuring such hierarchical reasoning, supported by a curated 25K MRG dataset with task-adaptive Chain-of-Thought (CoT) annotations that elicit multi-perspective evidence before conclusion. To remedy the heterogeneous task--sample difficulties, we further propose Bi-Axial DAPO (BiA-DAPO), which decomposes rollout advantages along an intra-group signal axis and an inter-group competence axis through two complementary mechanisms, both grounded on our defined candidate pool for stable group-level statistics. Extensive experiments show that MG-Thinker achieves state-of-the-art performance on multi-image reasoning grounding while consistently improving generalization across multi-image understanding and diverse multimodal benchmarks.

</details>


### [10] [NRF-GS: Neural Residual Fields for Expressive and Compact Gaussian Splatting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37115)
*Pratik Singh Bisht,Andreas Kolb*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: NRF-GS proposes a hybrid representation for 3D Gaussian Splatting that replaces per-splat spherical harmonics with a shared neural residual field, enabling better view-dependent reflectance modeling while reducing Gaussian count by up to 50%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard 3DGS uses low-order SH for view-dependent reflectance, which cannot model high-frequency directional effects effectively. This limitation is compensated by increasing splat count, causing representation redundancy. The authors aim to enhance expressiveness while reducing geometric redundancy.

Method: NRF-GS uses a hybrid approach: (1) Each Gaussian encodes compact appearance features and a Lambertian base color; (2) A lightweight global scene-level MLP predicts view-dependent residuals conditioned on viewing direction, distance, and per-splat features. This combines diffuse per-splat reflectance with shared global function for high-frequency details.

Result: NRF-GS achieves comparable or better rendering quality while reducing Gaussian count by up to 50%, with visibly improved specular and high-frequency details.

Conclusion: Accurate high-frequency directional reflectance modeling (especially in specular regions) makes the GS-representation more expressive, reducing the need for geometrically redundant splats. The shared neural residual field enables both higher expressiveness and parameter sharing across splats.

Abstract: We revisit the role of appearance modeling in 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) and show that limited expressiveness in view-dependent reflectance is a key driver of representation redundancy. In standard 3DGS, low-order spherical harmonics (SH) are used, restricting the splats' ability to model high-frequency directional effects, which is typically compensated by increasing the number of splats. We propose \emph{NRF-GS: Neural Residual Fields for Gaussian Splatting}, a hybrid representation that replaces per-splat SH-bases with a shared neural residual field. Each Gaussian encodes a compact set of appearance features and a lambertian base color, while a lightweight \emph{global scene-level MLP} predicts view-dependent residuals conditioned on viewing direction, distance, and per-splat features. This formulation enhances directional reflectance modeling by combining diffuse per-splat reflectance representations with a shared global function for high-frequency details, enabling both higher expressiveness and parameter sharing across splats. Our key insight is that by accurately capturing high-frequency directional reflectance, especially in specular regions, the GS-representation becomes more expressive, reducing the need for geometrically redundant splats. As a result, NRF-GS achieves comparable or better rendering quality while reducing the number of Gaussians by up to 50\%, and produces visibly improved specular and high-frequency details.

</details>


### [11] [MSTypography: Multi-character Semantic Typography via Balancing Word Legibility and Object Recognizability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37141)
*Xinye Yang,Xinding Zhu,Kai Fang,Xinyi Ren,Mengjian Li,Bin Cao,Jiazhou Chen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes a global-to-local framework for multi-character semantic typography, where visual representations of words convey semantic meaning while maintaining legibility. The method combines mask-driven silhouette approximation at the global level with semantic-guided refinement at the local level, using structural losses and OCR constraints to preserve readability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing digital typography methods mainly focus on single-character scenarios and suffer from lack of legibility constraints and insufficient local deformation when extended to multi-character words, as intricate structures among multiple characters are hardly preserved during the typography process.

Method: The proposed global-to-local framework includes: (1) mask-driven silhouette approximation at the global level; (2) a culling step for efficiency; (3) semantic-guided refinement at the local level using diffusion priors; (4) structural losses including explicit collision constraints and implicit Jacobian singular value constraints; (5) OCR constraint for character-level readability.

Result: This is the first multi-character semantic typography method that effectively balances word legibility and object recognizability. Evaluations on five representative languages (English, Chinese, Japanese, Korean, Arabic) demonstrate superiority over SOTA methods.

Conclusion: The proposed framework successfully handles multi-character semantic typography by preserving word legibility through structural and OCR constraints while enhancing object recognizability via semantic guidance with diffusion priors.

Abstract: Semantic typography is a design technique where the visual representation of a word conveys its semantic meaning, while maintaining its legibility. Existing digital typography methods mainly focus on single-character scenarios. They suffer from a lack of legibility constraints and insufficient local deformation when extended to multi-character words, as the intricate structures among multiple characters are hardly preserved during the typography process. In this paper, we propose a global-to-local typography framework for multi-character scenarios. It performs mask-driven silhouette approximation at the global level, while semantic-guided refinement at the local level, with a culling step in between to improve efficiency. To preserve word legibility, we designed structural losses (including explicit collision constraints and implicit Jacobian singular value constraints) and an OCR constraint for character-level readability. To enhance the object recognizability, we leverage semantic guidance with diffusion priors, which drives the character glyph toward the target concept while preserving its structural integrity. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first multi-character semantic typography method that effectively balances word legibility and object recognizability. Evaluations on five representative languages (English, Chinese, Japanese, Korean, Arabic) demonstrate superiority over SOTA methods. Codes will be open-sourced.

</details>


### [12] [One Geometry, Different Outcomes: Readout-Dependent Effects of the Modality Gap in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36101)
*Aditya Sharma,Divya Saxena*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper provides a unified geometric explanation for why modifying the modality gap in contrastive vision-language models (CLIP, SigLIP) has divergent effects across different downstream tasks. The authors discover that a single dominant direction captures 94.4-99.9% of the image-text mean separation, enabling task-specific analysis of gap modification impacts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work showed conflicting results: reducing the modality gap improves zero-shot classification but degrades image-text retrieval. This inconsistency lacked a unified theoretical explanation, motivating the authors to investigate the geometric structure of the gap and its task-dependent effects.

Method: The authors analyzed CLIP and SigLIP encoders by decomposing the similarity score to identify three task-specific roles of the gap direction. They conducted experiments across zero-shot classification, standard cross-modal retrieval, and mixed-modal retrieval tasks, using geometric analysis to derive an exponent that tracks the grid-search optimum.

Result: The modality gap is approximately rank-one (94.4-99.9% of squared norm captured by a single dominant direction). Key findings: (1) Gap-offset subtraction in zero-shot classification is equivalent to additive class bias. (2) Projecting out the gap direction in retrieval induces multiplicative ranking distortion; a geometry-derived exponent achieves Spearman rho = 0.93 vs grid-search. (3) In mixed-modal retrieval, the gap direction sorts candidates by modality, and its removal can improve cross-modal ranking. The rank-one account has limits due to residual semantic structure.

Conclusion: This unified geometric framework explains why gap modification can improve, degrade, or restore performance across downstream tasks. It provides a principled basis for selecting gap interventions in similarity-based vision-language systems, clarifying when and why such modifications change model behavior.

Abstract: Contrastive vision-language models learn shared embedding spaces by aligning matched image-text pairs, yet their representations remain separated by a modality gap. Prior work reports divergent effects of modifying this gap: reducing it can improve zero-shot classification and cross-modal alignment, whereas removing gap-related structure can degrade image-text retrieval. In this paper, we provide a unified geometric explanation for these task-dependent effects. Across CLIP and SigLIP encoders, we find that a single dominant direction captures 94.4-99.9% of the squared norm of the image-text mean separation, revealing that the mean-separation component is approximately rank-one. A decomposition of the similarity score then identifies three task-specific roles. In zero-shot classification, query-side fixed gap-offset subtraction is exactly equivalent to an additive class bias. In standard cross-modal retrieval, projecting out the gap direction and renormalising residuals discards candidate-specific norm information, inducing a multiplicative ranking distortion; a geometry-derived exponent tracks the grid-search optimum (Spearman rho = 0.93) and restores performance in some settings, although the gains transfer unevenly. In mixed-modal retrieval, the gap direction sorts candidates by modality; its removal can improve cross-modal ranking, unlike random or non-gap controls. Residual semantic structure after removal defines the limits of the rank-one account. Together, these results explain why gap modification can improve, degrade, or restore performance across downstream settings. By clarifying when and why gap modification changes model behavior, this account provides a principled basis for selecting gap interventions in similarity-based vision-language systems across evaluated downstream tasks.

</details>


### [13] [The Domain Is a Residue: Adapting Self-Supervised Features, Not Generators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37330)
*Thomas Deixelberger,Markus Steinberger*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper proposes Representation Feature Adapter (RFA), a lightweight 2.9M-parameter network that removes weather effects (fog, rain, snow) from footage or converts renders to photographs while preserving scene content. It uses DINO feature maps to separate scene structure (fixed) from weather/lighting/rendering style (residue to be moved). RFA outperforms CycleGAN-Turbo and other methods with 160x fewer parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current unpaired translators preserve source appearance because their generator sees the source directly. The authors propose using DINO feature maps where the scene is fixed and weather/lighting/rendering style appears as a residue (13-14% of feature norm), which can be moved to achieve domain translation while preserving scene content.

Method: The Representation Feature Adapter (RFA) is a 2.9M-parameter network that moves the residue in DINO feature maps. Only the adapter and its discriminators are trained; the encoder and feature-conditioned decoder (trained once for all conditions) remain frozen. The approach leverages DINO features where scene content is fixed and weather/lighting/rendering style exists as a residual component.

Result: RFA outperforms CycleGAN-Turbo on fog (both metrics) and night (KID), with competitive results on snow, rain, and haze. On sim-to-real translation, it leads REGEN and HyPER-GAN on both metrics. Only RFA removes rain while preserving scene structure. It has ~160x fewer trainable parameters than CycleGAN-Turbo and under 1/5 of its per-condition training time.

Conclusion: The RFA demonstrates that DINO feature maps can effectively separate scene content from appearance (weather, lighting, rendering style). This approach enables efficient domain translation with significantly fewer parameters and training time than existing methods, while achieving superior or competitive performance across various weather conditions and sim-to-real translation tasks.

Abstract: Clearing fog, rain or snow from footage, or turning renders into photographs, must remove the source domain and keep the scene. Unpaired translators carry it through because their generator sees the source appearance (pixels, a near-invertible latent or a control map) and keeps it. A DINO feature map fixes what is in the scene and carries weather, lighting and rendering style as a residue of 13 to 14% of the feature norm. We propose the Representation Feature Adapter (RFA), a 2.9M-parameter network that moves this residue. We train only the adapter and its discriminators; the encoder and a feature-conditioned decoder, trained once for all conditions, stay frozen. Against CycleGAN-Turbo it is ahead on both metrics on fog and on KID on night, and level within noise on snow, rain and haze. On sim-to-real it leads REGEN and HyPER-GAN on both metrics. Only the RFA removes the rain while keeping the scene. The removal costs scene structure: CycleGAN-Turbo keeps more on every condition but fog. On VAE latents the identical adapter collapses to the identity, and decoders from other groups that never saw it render its output. The RFA has about 160 times fewer trainable parameters than CycleGAN-Turbo and under a fifth of its per-condition training time.

</details>


### [14] [Hardware-Aware Functional Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks for Efficient Medical Image Enhancement and Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36134)
*Mohammad Sadegh Sirjani*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: FunKANLite is a two-stage hardware-aware compression of FunKAN (Functional Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks) for edge medical devices. It achieves 5.6x parameter reduction and 3.7x FLOPs reduction while maintaining accuracy within 1.4% IoU and reducing energy consumption by up to 68% on edge devices.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: FunKAN achieves state-of-the-art accuracy on MRI Gibbs artifact removal and anatomical segmentation, but its 11.6M parameters and 8.7 GFLOPs are too large for edge medical devices, necessitating model compression for point-of-care deployment.

Method: Two-stage compression: (1) FunKANLite-TR reduces spatial prior and replaces ResBlock offset predictor with depthwise-separable block; (2) FunKANLite-ST lowers Hermite basis rank, factorizes spatial prior into low-rank form, and halves filter widths through knowledge distillation.

Result: FunKANLite-ST has 5.6x fewer parameters and 3.7x fewer GFLOPs than FunKAN. It stays within 1.4 percentage points IoU on BUSI, GlaS, and CVC-ClinicDB, reaches 33.95 dB PSNR on IXI, and reduces energy per inference by up to 68% while raising throughput by 2.9x on NVIDIA Jetson Orin Nano and Raspberry Pi 5.

Conclusion: FunKANLite successfully compresses FunKAN for edge medical device deployment, achieving significant computational savings with minimal accuracy loss, enabling practical point-of-care MRI and segmentation tasks.

Abstract: Functional Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks (FunKAN) achieve state-of-the-art accuracy on MRI Gibbs artifact removal and anatomical segmentation, but their 11.6 M parameters and 8.7 GFLOPs are too large for edge medical devices. We present FunKANLite, a two-stage, hardware-aware compression of FunKAN for point-of-care use. FunKANLite-TR reduces the spatial prior and replaces the ResBlock offset predictor with a depthwise-separable block. It has 1.9x fewer parameters than FunKAN and no loss in accuracy. We then distill FunKANLite-TR into FunKANLite-ST, which lowers the Hermite basis rank, factorizes the spatial prior into a low-rank form, and halves the filter widths. FunKANLite-ST has 5.6x fewer parameters and 3.7x fewer GFLOPs than FunKAN. It stays within 1.4 percentage points IoU of FunKAN on BUSI, GlaS, and CVC-ClinicDB, and reaches 33.95 dB PSNR on IXI. On an NVIDIA Jetson Orin Nano and a Raspberry Pi 5, FunKANLite-ST reduces energy per inference by up to 68% and raises throughput by 2.9x.

</details>


### [15] [Texture Space Material Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37654)
*Jacob Munkberg,Peter Kocsis,Jon Hasselgren*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A method for generating high-quality 3D materials entirely in texture space by fine-tuning a video diffusion transformer, enabling text-guided material generation, multi-view material generation, and material upscaling. The approach leverages the projection from image space to texture space to generalize across arbitrary geometries and avoids view consistency issues.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing video and multi-view diffusion models suffer from view consistency issues when generating materials for 3D objects. The goal is to develop a method that can generate high-quality materials without these limitations while leveraging powerful pretrained video diffusion priors.

Method: Fine-tune a video diffusion transformer for text-guided material generation, multi-view material generation, and material upscaling. The key insight is using the known projection from image space to texture space, which enables generalization across arbitrary geometries and texture parameterizations. Since texture space is 2D, pretrained video diffusion model priors can be effectively reused.

Result: The method achieves state-of-the-art results for material generation and reconstruction in both quantitative and qualitative evaluations. It can scale to high resolutions (8K), 100+ input views, and neural material representations.

Conclusion: The proposed texture-space approach successfully addresses view consistency issues while leveraging strong pretrained video diffusion priors, enabling high-quality material generation and reconstruction across various applications including material reconstruction from posed photos, text-guided generation, and image-guided generation.

Abstract: We present a method for generating high quality materials for 3D objects entirely in texture space. We finetune a video diffusion transformer for text-guided material generation, multi-view material generation, and material upscaling. Our key insight is to use the known projection from image space to texture space, enabling the diffusion process to generalize across arbitrary geometries and texture parameterizations. This approach also avoids the view consistency issues inherent in video and multi-view diffusion models. Because texture space is two dimensional, we can reuse the strong priors of pretrained video diffusion models. We apply our method to high quality material reconstruction from posed photos captured under unknown lighting, as well as to text- and image guided material generation. Our method can scale to high resolutions (8K), 100+ input views, and neural material representations. In quantitative and qualitative evaluations we show state-of-the-art results for material generation and reconstruction.

</details>


### [16] [Xiaomi-OCR-0 Technical Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36136)
*Xin Chen,Anan Du,Feng Feng,Pei Fu,Jian Luan,Longwei Xu,Shaojie Zhang,Hang Li,Heng Qu,Cheng Tan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Xiaomi-OCR-0 is a compact 0.8B vision-language model for document parsing and OCR-centric understanding, built on Qwen3.5-0.8B with an approximately 170M-sample OCR-centric corpus. It uses progressive training combining Q-Mask-based text anchoring, continued pretraining, and mixed-task reinforcement learning (Mix-RL). The model achieves state-of-the-art results on multiple document parsing benchmarks (95.24 on Real5-OmniDocBench, 96.83 on OmniDocBench v1.6, 87.94 on Wild-OmniDocBench) and an average score of 83.2 across five OCR-oriented VQA benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing compact OCR-specific vision-language models rely on costly supervision and focus primarily on visual-text reconstruction, lacking OCR-centric understanding capabilities. This work aims to develop a unified, efficient model that excels at both document parsing and OCR-centric understanding tasks.

Method: The model uses a progressive training recipe: (1) Q-Mask-based text anchoring for precise text localization, (2) continued pretraining on a 170M-sample OCR-centric corpus built via an automated data engine combining expert consensus, render-based verification, and targeted synthesis, and (3) mixed-task reinforcement learning (Mix-RL) for joint optimization.

Result: Xiaomi-OCR-0 achieves 95.24 on Real5-OmniDocBench, 96.83 on OmniDocBench v1.6, and 87.94 on Wild-OmniDocBench, with an average score of 83.2 across five OCR-oriented VQA benchmarks. Ablations show that OCR-centric understanding supervision provides additional gains for document parsing when sufficient parsing training is applied.

Conclusion: A compact 0.8B model can achieve strong document parsing and OCR-centric understanding performance through a well-designed progressive training recipe and large-scale OCR-centric data. The combination of document parsing and OCR-centric understanding tasks is synergistic, with OCR-centric supervision providing additional gains for parsing tasks.

Abstract: Compact OCR-specific vision-language models achieve strong document parsing performance, but often rely on costly supervision and focus primarily on visual-text reconstruction. We introduce Xiaomi-OCR-0, a unified 0.8B model for document parsing and OCR-centric understanding. We build an approximately 170M-sample OCR-centric corpus using an automated data engine that combines expert consensus, render-based verification, and targeted synthesis. Starting from Qwen3.5-0.8B, our progressive training recipe combines Q-Mask-based text anchoring, continued pretraining, and mixed-task reinforcement learning (Mix-RL). Xiaomi-OCR-0 achieves 95.24 on Real5-OmniDocBench, 96.83 on OmniDocBench v1.6, and 87.94 on Wild-OmniDocBench, while reaching an average score of 83.2 across five OCR-oriented VQA benchmarks. Ablations further show that, with sufficient parsing training, OCR-centric understanding supervision provides additional gains for document parsing.
  Homepage: https://huggingface.co/spaces/SeerRay-Lab/Xiaomi-OCR-0.

</details>


### [17] [From Sharp Eyes to Expert Mind: Internalizing Expert Knowledge in MLLMs for Tampered Text Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36145)
*Kaiqing Lin,Songze Li,Shen Chen,Yunfei Guo,Xiaoye Qiu,Haodong Li,Taiping Yao,Bo Wang,Youchang Xiao,Bin Li,Shouhong Ding*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes Expert Knowledge Internalization (EKI), a two-stage framework that transfers forensic expertise into a Multimodal Large Language Model (MLLM) for Tampered Text Detection (TTD). The method addresses a "Double Mismatch" problem (spatial precision and perceptual granularity) through text/image-focused strategies and a Forensic-General Representation Alignment (FGRA) loss. EKI achieves state-of-the-art performance with cross-domain generalization while maintaining inference efficiency comparable to vanilla MLLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing expert models for tampered text detection generalize poorly across domains, while MLLMs lack sensitivity to fine-grained forensic artifacts. The complementary strengths of both approaches motivate the internalization of expert forensic perception into MLLMs rather than using external modules.

Method: The proposed EKI framework has two stages: (1) Stage 1 uses Text-Focused and Image-Focused strategies to establish precise spatial focus on small text regions; (2) Stage 2 employs Forensic-General Representation Alignment (FGRA) loss to align shallow LLM representations with a pre-trained forensic expert, enabling fine-grained artifact perception before semantic abstraction dilutes it.

Result: Extensive experiments on multiple in-domain and cross-domain benchmarks demonstrate that EKI achieves state-of-the-art performance and stronger generalization than expert-model-based and MLLM-based methods. The resulting MLLM maintains inference efficiency nearly identical to the vanilla model without requiring any external expert at inference time.

Conclusion: EKI successfully internalizes forensic expertise into MLLMs, addressing the Double Mismatch problem. The method achieves superior performance and generalization while eliminating the need for external expert modules during inference, offering both accuracy and efficiency.

Abstract: Tampered Text Detection (TTD) is essential for safeguarding document authenticity in security-critical workflows. Existing expert models are effective at capturing subtle manipulation traces but often generalize poorly across diverse document domains, while Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) offer stronger semantic understanding and transferability yet remain insensitive to fine-grained forensic artifacts. This complementarity motivates us to investigate how expert forensic perception can be internalized into an MLLM rather than merely accessed through an external module. We identify a fundamental Double Mismatch that hinders this goal: a Spatial Precision Mismatch between coarse visual tokens and tiny tampered regions, and a Perceptual Granularity Mismatch between semantics-oriented pre-training and low-level forensic perception. To address these challenges, we propose Expert Knowledge Internalization (EKI), a progressive two-stage framework that transfers forensic expertise into the MLLM itself. In Stage 1, Text-Focused and Image-Focused strategies establish precise spatial focus on small text regions. In Stage 2, the proposed Forensic-General Representation Alignment (FGRA) loss aligns shallow LLM representations with those of a pre-trained forensic expert, enabling the model to acquire fine-grained artifact perception before such cues are diluted by deeper semantic abstraction. Extensive experiments on multiple in-domain and cross-domain benchmarks demonstrate that EKI achieves state-of-the-art performance and stronger generalization than existing expert-model-based and MLLM-based methods. Moreover, the expert is required only during training, allowing the resulting MLLM to maintain inference efficiency nearly identical to the vanilla model without relying on any external expert at inference.

</details>


### [18] [Boosting Metric Depth Completion via Training-Free Adaptive Response Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36168)
*Mia Zhang,Jizong Peng*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper introduces "adaptive response geometry" for depth completion, which treats the choice between depth, log depth, or disparity as an image-level unknown rather than a fixed assumption. The method uses a continuous response family with depth-dependent gain, derives response-gradient relations, and applies Hard-Dirichlet residual reconstruction. The training-free pipeline achieves macro AbsRel 0.0301 and macro NMed 14.04°, outperforming PriorDA, LDCM, and Any2Full.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current depth completion methods leverage visual foundation models as geometric priors but rely on rigid affine assumptions in predefined coordinate systems to align priors to true metric scale. This leaves systematic calibration errors unaddressed, as linearity in depth calibration depends on the response coordinate (depth, log depth, or disparity).

Method: The paper proposes adaptive response geometry which: (1) makes the fixed choice of depth, log depth, or disparity an image-level unknown; (2) uses a continuous response family that unifies these coordinates and defines an explicit depth-dependent gain; (3) derives the response-gradient relation to estimate response parameters in metric space; (4) applies Hard-Dirichlet residual reconstruction to complete the calibrated prior.

Result: The training-free pipeline achieves macro AbsRel 0.0301 and macro NMed 14.04°, improving both aggregate measures over PriorDA, LDCM, and Any2Full under deliberately incomplete metric observations.

Conclusion: The proposed adaptive response geometry successfully addresses systematic calibration errors in depth completion by treating response coordinate selection as an unknown. The method outperforms prior works (PriorDA, LDCM, Any2Full) without requiring training. Linearity diagnostics are provided to examine how selected responses change depth relations and metric errors.

Abstract: Depth completion aims to recover dense metric depth from sparse sensor measurements, increasingly leveraging visual foundation models as geometric priors. However, aligning these priors to true metric scale typically relies on rigid affine assumptions in predefined coordinate systems, leaving systematic calibration errors. Linearity in depth calibration depends on the response coordinate. We introduce adaptive response geometry, which makes the fixed choice of depth, log depth, or disparity an image-level unknown. A continuous response family unifies these coordinates and defines an explicit depth-dependent gain. We derive the response-gradient relation and estimate the response parameters in metric space. Hard-Dirichlet residual reconstruction completes the calibrated prior. Under deliberately incomplete metric observations, the training-free pipeline achieves macro AbsRel 0.0301 and macro NMed 14.04°, improving both aggregate measures over PriorDA, LDCM, and Any2Full. Linearity diagnostics examine how the selected response changes the depth relation and its metric error.

</details>


### [19] [Exploring Learning Models for Topological Relationship Recognition from Image Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36172)
*Saptak Das,Monidipa Das*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A new dataset of over 11,000 labeled images was created to address the lack of datasets for detecting topological relationships (touch, overlap, containment, separation) between objects in images. Testing traditional ML models (Naive Bayes, KNN, Random Forest, SVM, ANN) and deep learning models (VGG16, InceptionResNetV2) with features extracted via segmentation, contour detection, and grayscale normalization, VGG16 achieved 89.55% validation accuracy, demonstrating the power of transfer learning for spatial reasoning and topological classification tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Topological relationship detection in images is important for GIS, biomedical imaging, and robotics, but has been underexplored due to two main roadblocks: insufficient datasets and lack of evaluation metrics.

Method: A dataset of 11,000+ labeled images was created. Features were extracted using segmentation, contour detection, and grayscale normalization. Multiple models were tested: Naive Bayes, KNN, Random Forest, SVM, ANN (traditional ML), and VGG16, InceptionResNetV2 (deep learning with transfer learning).

Result: Deep learning methods, particularly VGG16, significantly outperformed traditional machine learning models, achieving 89.55% validation accuracy.

Conclusion: Transfer learning is highly effective for analyzing topological relationships in images. VGG16's performance establishes a new benchmark for spatial reasoning and topological classification research.

Abstract: Figuring out how objects relate to each other, like whether they touch, overlap, stay completely separate or one sits inside another, matters a lot in fields like GIS, biomedical imaging, and robotics. Even though machine learning has come a long way, people haven't really focused on spotting these topological relationships in images. The main roadblocks? Not enough good datasets and no clear way to measure results. So, we rolled up our sleeves and built a new dataset. It's pretty sizable: over 11,000 labelled images showing all those essential relationships. We ran tests with some classic machine learning models, Naive Bayes, KNN, Random Forest, SVM, and Artificial Neural Networks, and threw in some deep learning stars like VGG16 and InceptionResNetV2. For the dataset itself, we used segmentation, contour detection, and grayscale normalization to tease out solid feature vectors. The results? Deep learning methods, especially VGG16, pulled ahead, with validation accuracy hitting 89.55%. That's a big jump compared to the traditional models. This shows how powerful transfer learning is for analyzing topological relationships in images, and it gives researchers a new standard to aim for in future work on spatial reasoning and topological classification.

</details>


### [20] [FD-AA: A Lightweight Focal-Diffuse And Attenuation-Aware Head for Incidental Abdominal Abnormality Detection in Chest CT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36189)
*Haoyan Ding,Kritika Iyer,Halid Yerebakan,Zhenyu Bu,Chushu Shen,Peiyu Duan,Xinyuan Zheng,Sepehr Farhand,Xueqi Guo,Chaowei Wu,Yoshihisa Shinagawa,Gerardo Hermosillo Valadez*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: FD-AA is a lightweight organ-aware classification head for detecting incidental abdominal abnormalities in routine chest CT scans. It combines an attenuation-aware module for sparse focal evidence with masked generalized-mean pooling for diffuse anomaly patterns. The method achieves SOTA performance across multiple frozen 3-D CT encoders on CT-RATE and RAD-ChestCT datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Routine chest CT captures upper-abdominal structures containing clinically relevant incidental abnormalities, but detecting these findings is challenging due to different spatial extents and attenuation patterns across organs. Existing methods using frozen encoders lack organ-specific adaptation for capturing both focal and diffuse anomaly patterns.

Method: Proposed FD-AA, a lightweight organ-aware classification head with two key components: (1) Attenuation-aware module that preserves sparse focal evidence using Hounsfield Unit (HU) information, and (2) Masked generalized-mean pooling that captures diffuse anomaly patterns. FD-AA is adaptable for frozen 3-D CT encoders and processes features within each organ separately.

Result: FD-AA with Pillar-0 achieved SOTA performance: CT-RATE test set AUC = 0.798, external RAD-ChestCT AUC = 0.713. Improved macro AUC/AP from 0.763/0.346 to 0.798/0.405 over direct classification (p = 0.034/0.016). Performance gains generalize across encoders: MedicalNet +9.8%, CT-CLIP +14.7%, ResNet +3.7%.

Conclusion: Integrating focal-diffuse aggregation with explicit HU evidence is effective for incidental abdominal abnormality detection in chest CT. The lightweight design maintains compatibility with frozen encoders while achieving consistent improvements across multiple feature representations.

Abstract: Routine chest CT captures upper-abdominal structures that may contain clinically relevant incidental abnormalities. Detecting these findings requires feature extraction from organs with different spatial extents and attenuation patterns. We propose FD-AA, a lightweight organ-aware classification head adaptable for frozen 3-D CT encoders. Within each organ, an attenuation-aware module preserves sparse focal evidence, while masked generalized-mean pooling captures diffuse anomaly patterns. In seven abdominal organs, FD-AA with Pillar-0 achieved state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance in both the CT-RATE test set (AUC = 0.798) and the external RAD-ChestCT dataset (AUC = 0.713). More specifically, FD-AA improved macro AUC/AP from 0.763/0.346 to 0.798/0.405 over direct classification using frozen Pillar-0 only (p = 0.034/0.016). Such performance gain generalizes across multiple frozen encoders (AUC improvement on MedicalNet +9.8%, CT-CLIP +14.7%, ResNet +3.7%), demonstrating the effectiveness of FD-AA across different feature representations. These results support the effectiveness of integrating focal-diffuse aggregation with explicit HU evidence for incidental abdominal abnormality detection.

</details>


### [21] [PreviewDiff: Multimodal Critic-Guided Search over Diffusion Latents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36199)
*Vighnesh Subramaniam,Boris Katz,Brian Cheung,Chun-Liang Li,Tomas Pfister,Yale Song*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: PreviewDiff is a training-free test-time search method that improves diffusion model outputs by using multimodal feedback during the denoising process. It decodes partial previews at intermediate steps, gets scoring and critique from a multimodal judge, and uses natural-language feedback to guide semantic edits and re-noising branches, improving over Best-of-N and scalar-search baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion models struggle with compositional details (object counts, attribute binding, spatial relations, temporal actions). Existing methods like Best-of-N sampling can only select among completed outputs and cannot repair promising trajectories before they fail. There is a need for methods that can actively guide generation while the sample is still editable.

Method: PreviewDiff introduces a training-free test-time search method that: 1) At selected denoising checkpoints, decodes partial previews; 2) Uses a multimodal judge to score and critique the preview; 3) Generates natural-language feedback to branch over semantic prompt edits and locally re-noised latent continuations; 4) Scores and selectively rolls forward these branches, using verifier compute to guide generation during the editable phase.

Result: PreviewDiff consistently improves over budget-matched Best-of-N selection and strong scalar-search baselines across image and video generation benchmarks. Ablations show that earlier interventions and increased search width provide the largest gains, while deeper search and additional semantic variants offer complementary improvements.

Conclusion: Multimodal feedback is most useful not only as a final verifier, but as an active controller inside the denoising process. This approach transforms diffusion sampling from scalar search into a multimodal critic-guided search over intermediate latents.

Abstract: Diffusion models can produce striking images and videos, but they still struggle with the compositional details that make a generation faithful to a prompt, such as object counts, attribute binding, spatial relations, and temporally grounded actions. A common way to improve prompt satisfaction is to spend more compute at test time through Best-of-N sampling, but final-sample selection is fixed. Best-of-N can only choose among completed outputs and cannot repair a promising trajectory before it fails. We introduce PreviewDiff, a training-free test-time search method that turns diffusion sampling from scalar search into a multimodal critic-guided search over intermediate latents. At selected denoising checkpoints, PreviewDiff decodes a partial preview, asks a multimodal judge to score and critique it, and uses the resulting natural-language feedback to branch over semantic prompt edits and locally re-noised latent continuations. These branches are then scored and selectively rolled forward, allowing verifier compute to guide generation while the sample is still editable. Across image and video generation benchmarks, PreviewDiff consistently improves over budget-matched Best-of-N selection and strong scalar-search baselines. Ablations show that earlier interventions and increased search width provide the largest gains, while deeper search and additional semantic variants offer complementary improvements. PreviewDiff demonstrates that multimodal feedback is most useful not only as a final verifier, but as an active controller inside the denoising process.

</details>


### [22] [Sparse-View Interpretable 3D Animal Behavior Representations for Neural Encoding and Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36217)
*Xinming Dai,Qihang Jin,Tianshu Tan,Baiyuan Chen,Hanrui Lyu,Lenny Aharon,Kyle Daruwalla,Xun Helen Hou,Matthew R. Whiteway,Liam Paninski,Yizi Zhang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SABLE is a self-supervised framework that learns 3D behavior representations from sparse video views by leveraging geometric priors from monocular depth and pose estimation. It reconstructs 3D animal behavior from two-view videos without ground-truth 3D labels, outperforming SOTA methods in neural encoding/decoding and enabling zero-shot generalization to unseen animals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current methods for behavioral representation from video have limitations: pose tracking loses rich information beyond keypoints, and nonlinear video embeddings lack interpretability. There was a need for a method that can reconstruct 3D behavior from sparse views while learning explicit, interpretable 3D latent structure.

Method: SABLE uses a multi-view transformer augmented with priors from monocular depth and pose estimation. It is a self-supervised framework that learns behavior representations by reconstructing 3D animal behavior from extremely sparse views (two-view videos) while learning explicit 3D latent structure, without requiring ground-truth 3D labels.

Result: SABLE reliably recovers 3D behavior from two-view videos where SOTA methods fail or yield degenerate solutions. It matches or exceeds prior SOTA performance in neural encoding and decoding across the International Brain Lab and Cheese3D datasets. Once pretrained, it generalizes zero-shot to unseen animals without animal-specific calibration or retraining.

Conclusion: SABLE establishes 3D-aware video embeddings that capture complex behavior, opening new avenues for studying brain-behavior relationships. It successfully addresses the limitation of prior methods by providing interpretable 3D representations that work across different animals without retraining.

Abstract: A deeper understanding of brain function requires a precise, structured characterization of behavior.Yet, extracting behavioral representations from video in a form suitable for scientific analysis remains a fundamental challenge. Many prior studies represent behavior via pose estimation or nonlinear video embeddings. However, pose tracking discards rich information beyond predefined keypoints, while nonlinear video embeddings lack interpretability. We address this limitation with SABLE (Sparse-view Animal Behavior Latent Embeddings), a self-supervised framework that leverages a geometric inductive bias to learn behavior representations.By augmenting a multi-view transformer with priors from monocular depth and pose estimation, SABLE reconstructs 3D animal behavior from extremely sparse views while learning explicit 3D latent structure. Without ground-truth 3D labels, it reliably recovers 3D behavior from two-view videos, whereas state-of-the-art (SOTA) methods fail or yield degenerate solutions. Across the International Brain Lab and Cheese3D datasets, we demonstrate that SABLE learns 3D representations that match or exceed prior SOTA performance in neural encoding and decoding. Once pretrained across animals, SABLE serves as an off-the-shelf model that generalizes zero-shot to unseen animals without animal-specific calibration or retraining. Our method establishes 3D-aware video embeddings that capture complex behavior, opening new avenues for studying brain-behavior relationships.

</details>


### [23] [LeRF: Learning Reference Coordinate Frames for Perspective Taking Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36219)
*Bang Xiao,Wenqi Jia,Ozgur Kara,Tiancheng Shen,Yibo Yang,Bolin Lai,Junho Kim,James Matthew Rehg*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: LeRF is a framework that trains Vision-Language Models (VLMs) to perform perspective taking by constructing explicit reference coordinate frames. It combines supervised fine-tuning for frame prediction with reinforcement learning for improved reasoning, achieving state-of-the-art performance on perspective-taking benchmarks without external perception models or 3D reconstruction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-Language Models struggle with perspective taking - they default to camera viewpoint when queries require reasoning from different viewpoints (e.g., another entity's perspective). This is a fundamental limitation in spatial intelligence that needs to be addressed.

Method: LeRF uses a three-stage approach: (1) decides whether a coordinate frame is necessary for the query; (2) if so, grounds the reference entity and predicts the frame's origin and entity-centered reference frame; (3) a lightweight renderer overlays the frame onto the image for subsequent reasoning. Training involves supervised fine-tuning for tool invocation and frame prediction, followed by reinforcement learning on spatial VQA pairs.

Result: LeRF consistently improves over its backbone model and achieves strong performance against existing open-source methods across diverse perspective-taking benchmarks. Further evaluations show improved reference-frame grounding and orientation estimation.

Conclusion: The proposed LeRF framework demonstrates that learning explicit reference coordinate frames enables VLMs to perform viewpoint-dependent reasoning more effectively. The combination of supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning, along with visual frame overlay, provides a novel approach to improving perspective-taking capabilities in Vision-Language Models without requiring external perception models or explicit 3D reconstruction.

Abstract: Perspective taking is a fundamental component of spatial intelligence, requiring models interpret spatial relations from a specified viewpoint, such as that of another entity or an imagined observer. Despite the increasing spatial reasoning capabilities of Vision-Language Models (VLMs), they still struggle with perspective taking, often defaulting to the camera viewpoint when a query requires reasoning from a different perspective. We introduce Learning Reference Coordinate Frames for Perspective Taking (LeRF), a framework that trains VLMs to construct and use explicit reference frames for viewpoint-dependent reasoning. Given an image and a query, LeRF decides whether a coordinate frame is necessary. If so, it grounds the reference entity and predicts the frame's origin and entity-centered reference frame.
