A lightweight renderer overlays the frame onto the image, enabling subsequent reasoning over these visual cues without external perception models or explicit 3D reconstruction. To learn this process, we first perform supervised fine-tuning to teach selective tool invocation and reference coordinate frame prediction, followed by reinforcement learning on spatial VQA pairs to improve frame-guided reasoning. Across diverse perspective-taking benchmarks, LeRF consistently improves over its backbone and achieves strong performance against existing open-source methods. Further evaluations also show improved reference-frame grounding and orientation estimation, supporting the effectiveness of learned reference frames for viewpoint-dependent reasoning.

</details>


### [24] [Mutually Adversarial Self-Training with Evolving Data for Unified Multimodal Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36224)
*Wentao Zhou,Weijie Gan,Jiayun Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: MATE is a reinforcement-learning-based post-training framework for Unified Multimodal Models where the generation and understanding branches take turns challenging each other. Candidates that defeat one branch become challenges for the other in the next epoch, creating self-play in data space. On Janus-Pro-1B, it improves GenEval by 2.4 points, DPG-Bench by 1.7 points, and understanding benchmarks by 0.7 points.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Unified Multimodal Models (UMMs) combine image generation and visual understanding in a shared backbone. Since these are inverse tasks, prior work lets the two branches cooperatively supervise each other. However, this cooperative approach may not fully exploit the adversarial relationship between generation and understanding. This work proposes that having the branches challenge each other rather than cooperate could lead to stronger models.

Method: MATE uses a mutually adversarial self-training approach where generation and understanding take turns being challenger and solver. Given an image, the understanding branch proposes candidate descriptions that the generation branch must turn back into similar images, and vice versa. Candidates are screened for consistency with the original image or prompt. The solver is trained on the candidate it handles worst. Crucially, candidates that defeat one branch become the sources of next challenges to the other in the next epoch, keeping challenges evolving with the model.

Result: On Janus-Pro-1B, MATE improves GenEval by 2.4 points, DPG-Bench by 1.7 points, and the average over nine understanding benchmarks by 0.7 points, while strengthening consistency across repeated image-text cycles.

Conclusion: MATE demonstrates that adversarial self-play between generation and understanding branches, with evolving challenges, can improve both tasks simultaneously. The key innovation is using the model's own outputs as adversaries rather than training separate adversaries, and keeping challenges evolving with the model through epoch-based data evolution.

Abstract: Unified multimodal models (UMMs) combine image generation and visual understanding in a shared backbone. Since generation and understanding are inverse tasks, recent studies self-train UMMs by letting the two branches cooperatively supervise each other. We introduce MATE (Mutually Adversarial self-Training with Evolving data), a reinforcement-learning-based post-training framework in which the two branches instead challenge each other, and the challenges evolve as the model trains. MATE lets generation and understanding take turns to be challenger and solver. Given an image, the understanding branch proposes several candidate descriptions that the generation branch must turn back into similar images, and vice versa. The candidates are screened for consistency with the image or prompt they were proposed from, and the solver is trained on the candidate it handles worst. The adversary thus comes from the model's own outputs, and no separate adversary is trained. Moreover, the candidates that defeat one branch become the sources of the next challenges to the other in the next epoch, which keeps the challenges evolving with the model and turns the training into self-play in data space. On Janus-Pro-1B, MATE improves GenEval by 2.4 points, DPG-Bench by 1.7 points, and the average over nine understanding benchmarks by 0.7 points, while strengthening consistency across repeated image-text cycles.

</details>


### [25] [Think Before You Restore: Risk-Aware Manchu Manuscript Restoration with Stroke-Guided Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36243)
*Mingqiu Liang,Dongdong Wang,Siyang Lu,Ting Huang,Yingjun Qi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SAGE-Restore is a selective restoration framework for historical Manchu manuscripts that addresses over-restoration by first assessing where restoration is needed, then using this assessment to guide restoration candidate generation and pixel-level selection through a stroke-aware gating mechanism.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Full-page blind restoration of historical Manchu manuscripts is challenging due to scarce annotations, unknown degradation regions, and fragile connected strokes. Generic restoration models may improve visual quality but often modify intact content, leading to over-restoration.

Method: SAGE-Restore uses an encoder to predict patch-level repair probabilities from complementary appearance and stroke-structural cues, conditioning restoration candidate generation. The repair logits are refined into a pixel-level soft gate that selectively controls where the restoration candidate is applied. A fidelity-aware evaluation protocol jointly measures degraded-region recovery, intact-content preservation, and their balance.

Result: SAGE-Restore achieves the highest R-Recovery (0.463) and RFS (0.626), while maintaining high U-Fidelity (0.968), demonstrating an effective balance between restoration and content preservation.

Conclusion: SAGE-Restore successfully addresses the over-restoration problem in historical Manchu manuscript restoration by implementing a selective, stroke-aware gating mechanism that preserves intact content while effectively recovering degraded regions.

Abstract: Full-page blind restoration of historical Manchu manuscripts is challenging due to scarce annotations, unknown degradation regions, and fragile connected strokes. Generic restoration models may improve visual quality but often modify intact content, leading to over-restoration. We propose SAGE-Restore (Stroke-Aware Gated rEstoration), a selective restoration framework that first assesses where restoration is needed and then uses this assessment to guide restoration candidate generation and pixel-level selection. Its encoder predicts patch-level repair probabilities from complementary appearance and stroke-structural cues to condition restoration candidate generation, while the corresponding repair logits are refined into a pixel-level soft gate that selectively controls where the restoration candidate is applied. We further introduce a fidelity-aware evaluation protocol that jointly measures degraded-region recovery, intact-content preservation, and their balance. SAGE-Restore achieves the highest R-Recovery (0.463) and RFS (0.626), while maintaining high U-Fidelity (0.968), demonstrating an effective balance between restoration and content preservation.

</details>


### [26] [Representation by Design in Generation: Cross-View Class-Token Alignment in Diffusion Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36348)
*Xiaoyu Wu,Yifei Wang,Chen Wei*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes a method to train diffusion models to learn strong semantic representations while maintaining generation quality, by introducing cross-view class-token alignment into the flow matching framework.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors observe that while semantic representations can improve diffusion generation, the models' own representations are often treated as a by-product. They aim to train diffusion models to learn substantially stronger semantic representations as a primary objective, not just as a tool for improving generation.

Method: The method extends SelfFlow (which introduces self-supervised patch alignment into flow matching) with cross-view class-token alignment inspired by DINO and iBOT. Two independently noised, dual-timestep observations of each image are formed. The student class-token representation is aligned with the stop-gradient EMA-teacher target from the other observation. This objective is jointly optimized with flow-matching and local patch objectives.

Result: ImageNet linear-probing accuracy improves by 9.4% (class token) and 10.1% (mean-pooled patch tokens) compared to a matched two-view baseline. Frozen-backbone VOC2012 segmentation improves by 3.6 mIoU. Text-to-image training FID improves from 2.52 to 2.37. All these gains are achieved while maintaining comparable ImageNet generation FID.

Conclusion: Representation learning need not remain a by-product of generation in diffusion models—it can be directly optimized as a first-class capability alongside generation quality during diffusion pretraining.

Abstract: Generative and representation learning remain asymmetrically connected: semantic representations are used to improve diffusion generation, whereas the models' own representations are often treated as a by-product of synthesis. We ask whether diffusion models can instead be trained to learn substantially stronger semantic representations without sacrificing generation quality. SelfFlow takes a step in this direction by introducing self-supervised patch alignment into flow matching, but its main gains remain in faster convergence and improved generation. Inspired by DINO and iBOT, we extend this framework with cross-view class-token alignment to further strengthen semantic representations. Specifically, we form two independently noised, dual-timestep observations of each image and align each student class-token representation with the stop-gradient EMA-teacher target from the other observation. This objective is optimized jointly with the inherited flow-matching and local patch objectives. Notably, although the additional objective acts only on the class token, it strengthens both class-token and patch representations. Compared with a matched two-view baseline, ImageNet linear-probing accuracy improves by 9.4\% using the class token and 10.1\% using mean-pooled patch tokens, while frozen-backbone VOC2012 segmentation improves by 3.6 mIoU. These representation gains are achieved while maintaining comparable ImageNet generation FID. In text-to-image training, the same objective also improves generation FID, reducing it from 2.52 to 2.37 at matched checkpoints. Our results show that representation need not remain a by-product of generation or merely a tool for improving it: it can be directly optimized as a first-class capability of diffusion pretraining alongside generation.

</details>


### [27] [StructRL: Online Structured Reinforcement Learning for Long-Horizon Vision-Language-Action Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36352)
*Ziyi Yin,Sangmin Woo,Kang Zhou,Sungyeon Kim,Aosong Feng,Haibo Ding,Jun Huan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: StructRL is an online RL framework that addresses long-horizon manipulation tasks for VLA models by constructing structured intermediate supervision from verifiable subtask completions, outperforming baselines on RoboCasa365 and LIBERO-Long.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-language-action models struggle with long-horizon tasks requiring multiple dependent manipulations. Existing online RL methods provide only sparse terminal rewards after task success, which fail to distinguish early failures from rollouts making substantial partial progress.

Method: StructRL decomposes each task into verifiable subtasks, grants intermediate rewards only after prerequisite subtasks have been completed, and scales each reward according to completion pace.

Result: Across RoboCasa365 and LIBERO-Long benchmarks with GR00T-N1.5 and pi 0.5 models, StructRL consistently outperforms evaluated online RL baselines.

Conclusion: Verifiable, structured intermediate rewards improve long-horizon VLA post-training by providing meaningful learning signals during task execution rather than only at task completion.

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models perform well on shorter-horizon manipulation tasks but still struggle with long-horizon tasks that require multiple dependent manipulations from a single command. Online reinforcement learning (RL) can improve these policies through environment interaction, yet many existing methods provide reward only after the complete task succeeds. However, such terminal supervision is sparse and does not distinguish early failures from rollouts that make substantial partial progress. We propose StructRL, an online RL framework that constructs structured intermediate supervision from verifiable subtask completions. StructRL decomposes each task into verifiable subtasks, grants intermediate rewards only after the prerequisite subtasks have been completed, and scales each reward according to completion pace. Across RoboCasa365 and LIBERO-Long with GR00T-N1.5 and pi 0.5, StructRL consistently outperforms evaluated online RL baselines. These results show that verifiable, structured intermediate rewards improve long-horizon VLA post-training. Code is available at https://github.com/amazon-science/StructRL.

</details>


### [28] [Compress to Remember: Learning Compact Memory via On-Policy Distillation for Long Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36364)
*Xiaoyu Wu,Weihang Guo,Yifei Wang,Xinze Feng,Lydia E. Kavraki,Zhiwei Steven Wu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: PACC (Prediction-Aligned Context Compaction) addresses long-context degradation in video generation by training a learned compressor to aggregate past frames into compact memory tokens through on-policy distillation, using a frozen video generator as both teacher and student. The method outperforms baselines on MBench (Causal-rCM +6.63, Causal Forcing +3.19) and generates minute-long videos competitively on VBench-Long.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard video generators struggle with long-context degradation as historical context grows, making it difficult to retain information from earlier frames. Key-frame-based approaches can discard necessary information. The authors investigate whether a frozen video generator can provide supervision to learn a compact representation of history without modifying the underlying generator.

Method: PACC uses on-policy distillation with a frozen video generator in dual roles: as a student (conditioned on compressed memory) and as a teacher (conditioned on full history). A learned compressor aggregates information across past frames into compact memory tokens. Only the compressor is updated to align the student's predictions with teacher targets at each denoising step.

Result: PACC outperforms the strongest baseline by 6.63 points on Causal-rCM and 3.19 points on Causal Forcing on MBench. On VBench-Long using MovieGen prompts, PACC generates minute-long videos with generation quality competitive with baselines.

Conclusion: Learning to compact historical context can improve long-video memory without modifying the underlying generator. PACC successfully addresses long-context degradation by compressing historical information into reusable memory tokens while preserving necessary information for future generation.

Abstract: Standard video generators do not natively compact historical context into reusable memory tokens. As generation continues, the growing history makes it increasingly difficult to retain information from earlier frames due to long-context degradation. Key-frame-based approaches address this challenge by retaining selected past frames, but can discard information needed for future generation. Rather than relying on frame selection alone, we study whether a frozen video generator can supply the supervision needed to learn a compact representation of the history. We propose Prediction-Aligned Context Compaction (PACC), which uses a learned compressor to aggregate information across past frames into compact memory tokens. We train the compressor through on-policy distillation, using the same frozen generator both as a student when conditioned on compressed memory and as a teacher when conditioned on the full history. The student generates continuations, while the teacher provides targets for the same noisy inputs at each denoising step. Only the compressor is updated to align the student's predictions with these targets. We evaluate PACC on MBench, which jointly measures memory-event coverage and consistency. PACC outperforms the strongest baseline by 6.63 points on Causal-rCM and 3.19 points on Causal Forcing. Evaluation on VBench-Long using MovieGen prompts further shows that PACC produces minute-long videos with generation quality competitive with baselines. Together, these results show that learning to compact historical context can improve long-video memory without modifying the underlying generator.

</details>


### [29] [OTT3R: Multi-View 3D Reconstruction and Fast Dataset Generation at 1% Compute](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36374)
*Brandon Leblanc,Charalambos Poullis*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: OTT3R is a knowledge distillation framework that compresses large 3D reconstruction models (π³) into a 102M-parameter student (9.4× compression), while also providing a pseudo-label pipeline that replaces COLMAP for generating 3D supervision, running on 2 commodity GPUs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address two critical problems in 3D reconstruction: (1) High computational cost of large feed-forward models excludes most research groups and prevents edge deployment; (2) Lack of a COLMAP-like system for neural 3D pseudo-label generation, as Structure-from-Motion is slow and unreliable.

Method: Knowledge distillation framework on a single workstation with 2 GPUs. Distills π³ (959M params) into a 102M-parameter student. Includes an integrated pseudo-label pipeline generating dense per-pixel point maps and SE(3) camera poses. Domain specialization at 0.2% compute for deployable artifact.

Result: 9.4× compression with up to 7× faster inference, trained at 1.6% of VGGT's training compute. Pseudo-label pipeline processes 667K images in 3.5 hours on 2 GPUs, succeeding on sequences where COLMAP fails. General student matches teacher on in-distribution monocular depth and zero-shot outperforms COLMAP on 7-Scenes and DTU completion. Domain-specialized student: 4× more accurate than COLMAP on 7-Scenes at 980× throughput, with near-teacher completion.

Conclusion: OTT3R successfully addresses both compute accessibility and pseudo-label generation problems. The domain-specialized student achieves 4× higher accuracy than COLMAP at 980× throughput while maintaining near-teacher performance on 3D completion tasks.

Abstract: Feed-forward 3D reconstruction models have achieved impressive performance by scaling model and dataset size, but their cost excludes most research groups and precludes edge deployment. Additionally, generating 3D supervision without sensors still relies on slow, unreliable Structure-from-Motion, as the community lacks a COLMAP-like system for neural 3D pseudo-label generation. We present OTT3R (RGB-Only Tiny Transformer for 3D Reconstruction), a knowledge distillation framework that addresses both problems on a single workstation equipped with 2 GPUs. Distilling $π^3$ (959M parameters) into a 102M-parameter student yields 9.4$\times$ compression and up to 7$\times$ faster inference, trained at 1.6% of VGGT's training compute. An integrated pseudo-label pipeline offers a reliable, high-throughput alternative to COLMAP, generating dense per-pixel point maps and SE(3) camera poses for a 667K-image corpus in 3.5 hours on two commodity GPUs and succeeding on every sequence we tested, including those where COLMAP fails. The general student tracks the teacher on in-distribution monocular depth and, zero-shot, outperforms COLMAP on 7-Scenes and on DTU completion, but it does not replace the teacher on out-of-distribution multi-view geometry. The deployable artifact is the domain-specialized student: after specialization at 0.2% compute, it is 4$\times$ more accurate than COLMAP on 7-Scenes at 980$\times$ throughput, with near-teacher completion. Code is available at https://github.com/TheFourthKaramazov/OTT3R

</details>


### [30] [LEGO-Anything: Coding Agents for 3D Scene Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36380)
*Xirui Li,Peng Shi,Mingwen Dong,Sheng Zhang,Zhuoyan Xu,Dongkyu Lee,Shuaichen Chang,Yi Xiang,Lin Pan,Jiarong Jiang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: LEGO-Anything is an Image-to-Code framework that reconstructs 3D scenes from single images by iteratively writing and executing Blender code. The system uses a coding agent that inspects scenes, renders outputs, and revises programs. They introduce LEGO-Bench, a simulator-grounded benchmark with 208 images, where GPT-6-astra achieves best results (53.4% indoor, 39.6% outdoor). Analysis reveals three recurring issues: weak scene initialization, regressive edits, and unreliable self-evaluation. LEGO-Plugin improves all six evaluated models with up to 62.7% relative gains. In LEGO-World, reconstructed scenes support object detection, instance masks, and depth estimation but fall short of specialized vision models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current 3D scene reconstruction from single images produces fixed outputs that cannot be easily inspected, edited, or queried. The authors argue that reconstructing scenes as explicit scene programs would be more useful, enabling manipulation and querying of the 3D representation.

Method: The authors propose an Image-to-Code framework where a coding agent iteratively: 1) writes Blender Python code, 2) executes the code to generate scenes, 3) inspects rendered outputs, and 4) revises the program based on feedback. They introduce LEGO-Bench, a simulator-grounded benchmark with 208 images from 104 diverse indoor and outdoor scenes that scores artifact validity, geometry, and appearance. They also develop LEGO-Plugin to address construction issues and test downstream vision tasks in LEGO-World.

Result: GPT-6-astra achieves the strongest results on LEGO-Bench with 53.4% indoor and 39.6% outdoor scores. Three major issues were identified: weak scene initialization, regressive edits during iteration, and unreliable self-evaluation. LEGO-Plugin improves all six evaluated models with relative gains up to 62.7%. In LEGO-World, reconstructed scenes support object detection, instance segmentation, and depth estimation with non-trivial but not yet competitive performance compared to specialized vision models.

Conclusion: Program-constructed scenes from current coding agents are a promising but not yet sufficiently precise representation of natural images. While the approach enables editing and querying of 3D scenes, substantial gaps remain between valid artifacts and faithful recovery of scene geometry and appearance. Further improvements are needed before reconstructed scenes can reliably support downstream vision tasks.

Abstract: A 3D scene reconstructed from a single image is most useful when represented not as a rendering or a fixed 3D output, but as an explicit scene program whose execution yields a scene that can be inspected, edited, and queried. We present LEGO-Anything, an Image-to-Code framework in which a coding agent iteratively writes and executes Blender code, inspects scenes and renderings, and revises the program. To evaluate end-to-end scene recovery, we introduce LEGO-Bench, a simulator-grounded benchmark with 208 images from 104 diverse indoor and outdoor scenes. LEGO-Bench separately scores artifact validity, visible-surface geometry, and rendered appearance. Its simulator-grounded design enables extensibility and precise automatic evaluation. Among evaluated agents, GPT-6-astra achieves the strongest overall results, with 53.4% indoor and 39.6% outdoor scores, yet substantial gaps remain between delivering valid scene artifacts and faithfully recovering scene geometry and appearance. Analysis of agent construction trajectories reveals three recurring issues: weak scene initialization, regressive edits during iteration, and unreliable self-evaluation. These findings motivate LEGO-Plugin, a training-free harness plugin for more controlled iterative scene construction, which improves all six evaluated models, with relative gains of up to 62.7% in overall score. Finally, we test whether reconstructed scenes can represent natural images and support vision tasks. In LEGO-World, we derive object detections, instance masks, and relative depth as deterministic queries on scenes reconstructed by GPT-6-astra. These readouts show non-trivial performance across all three tasks but fall well short of specialized vision models, suggesting that program-constructed scenes from current coding agents are a promising but not yet sufficiently precise representation of natural images.

</details>

### [31] [Hybrid Reconstruction of Admissible Spline Spaces from Locally Modified Unclamped Patches for Isogeometric Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35788)
*C. G. Provatidis,I. Dimitriou*

Main category: cs.GR

TL;DR: This paper presents a decoupled reconstruction framework for NURBS representations that enables arbitrary local modifications (knot insertion, degree elevation, basis modifications) while preserving exact CAD geometry. The method uses a Hybrid Reconstruction Operator with QR pivoting, linear programming, and non-negative least squares to recover global admissibility and enforce inter-patch continuity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Local modifications to NURBS representations (knot insertion, degree elevation, basis changes) inevitably violate inter-patch continuity. Existing methods couple local spline editing with continuity enforcement, making the process inefficient. The authors aim to decouple these operations while maintaining mathematical properties like non-negativity, partition of unity, and exact geometry reproduction.

Method: The proposed method involves: (1) Decomposing NURBS into independent local Active Sections; (2) Identifying anchor degrees of freedom via QR pivoting; (3) Solving sequential linear programming problems with distance-based regularization to generate non-negative nullspace vectors; (4) Using non-negative least squares to enforce partition of unity; (5) Implementing hierarchical pairwise condensation to preserve computational efficiency and locality by freezing columns that satisfy interface constraints.

Result: The resulting hybrid basis is strictly non-negative, spans the exact constrained nullspace, preserves partition of unity to machine precision, and reproduces the original geometry exactly. Numerical benchmarks on multi-patch curves with heterogeneous local polynomial degrees demonstrate optimal convergence rates for a nonlinear diffusion problem.

Conclusion: The methodology provides a highly efficient, mathematically principled tool for adaptive Isogeometric Analysis by entirely decoupling local spline editing from continuity enforcement. It successfully combines local geometric flexibility with globally consistent approximation spaces.

Abstract: We propose a decoupled reconstruction framework that temporarily decomposes a NURBS representation into independent local Active Sections, enabling arbitrary local knot insertion, degree elevation, and basis modifications while preserving exact CAD geometry. However, these independent modifications inevitably violate inter-patch continuity, requiring a robust algebraic recovery of global admissibility. We introduce a novel reconstruction methodology that constructs a positive Hybrid Reconstruction Operator: anchor degrees of freedom are identified via QR pivoting, after which a sequence of linear programming problems with distance-based regularization generates strictly non-negative nullspace vectors, while a subsequent non-negative least-squares solve enforces a precise partition of unity. Crucially, to ensure computational efficiency and preserve locality, we implement a hierarchical pairwise condensation strategy that freezes columns already satisfying new interface constraints, confining the optimization exclusively to the active degrees of freedom at each merge step. The resulting hybrid basis is, by construction, strictly non-negative, spans the exact constrained nullspace, preserves partition of unity to machine precision, and reproduces the original geometry exactly. Numerical benchmarks, including a nonlinear diffusion problem on multi-patch curves with heterogeneous local polynomial degrees, confirm optimal convergence rates and demonstrate the framework's ability to seamlessly combine local geometric flexibility with globally consistent approximation spaces. By entirely decoupling local spline editing from the enforcement of continuity, this methodology provides a highly efficient, mathematically principled, and universally applicable tool for adaptive Isogeometric Analysis.

</details>


### [32] [Providing Rapid Design Feedback for 3D Obstacle Course Games Using Constrained Solvability Queries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36225)
*Zander Majercik,Sharon Zhang,William Wang,Tejan Karmali,Fangjun Zhou,Yucheng Yuan,Jean-Peïc Chou,Maneesh Agrawala,Kayvon Fatahalian*

Main category: cs.GR

TL;DR: An interactive system for 3D obstacle course game design that provides rapid feedback on obstacle solvability by querying solution sequences under designer-specified constraints, using a GPU-accelerated simulator and GoExplore algorithm guided by RL-trained agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Designers of 3D obstacle course games lack tools for rapid feedback on how obstacles can be solved, making it difficult to understand whether obstacles are solvable, identify undesirable solutions, or assess difficulty during the design process.

Method: Implement high-performance GoExplore algorithm for exploratory search, guided by an offline RL-trained obstacle-solving agent; accelerate search using a custom GPU-accelerated simulator achieving ~14,000× real-time speed at 60fps; enable constrained solvability queries with designer-specified constraints (e.g., waypoints, jump limits, region avoidance).

Result: The system enables designers to understand obstacle solvability, identify undesirable solution paths, assess difficulty, and explore new design directions. Human playtesting confirms players engage with obstacles as designers intended.

Conclusion: Constrained solvability queries in a rapid design loop are sufficiently expressive to aid obstacle course design, allowing designers to answer high-level questions about solution paths and difficulty, leading to better-designed obstacles verified through human playtesting.

Abstract: We present a system that aids the design of 3D obstacle course games by providing designers with rapid feedback on how obstacles can be solved. Our core contribution is a system for querying for solutions (sequences of player actions) to an obstacle that adhere to designer-specified constraints (e.g., avoid a region, travel through a given waypoint, only perform two jumps, etc.). To solve a wide range of obstacle designs quickly, we author a high-performance implementation of the GoExplore algorithm for exploratory search, and guide search with an obstacle solving agent trained offline using reinforcement learning (RL). To further accelerate search, the system carries out exploration using a custom GPU-accelerated obstacle course game simulator that generates playthrough experience at nearly 14,000$\times$ real time, 60-fps gameplay. Through design studies, we demonstrate that the use of constrained solvability queries in a rapid design loop is sufficiently expressive to help designers understand ways an obstacle can be solved or why it cannot be solved. We also show how the tool can let designers answer higher-level questions such as identifying undesirable solution paths and assessing the difficulty of solutions. This allows them to pursue new design directions they did not originally anticipate. Human playtesting of obstacles designed using our system confirms that human players indeed play the obstacles in the manner the designers intended. We release code for our interactive tool, simulator, training setup, and procedural level generation system at https://zandermajercik.github.io/interactive-obstacle-course-feedback/.

</details>


### [33] [Text2Sim: Agentic Physics-Based Simulation Generation with Distilled Expertise](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36593)
*Xiaoyu Xiong,Tsun-Hsuan Wang,Yi-Ling Qiao,Tao Du,Minchen Li*

Main category: cs.GR

TL;DR: Text2Sim is an AI agentic pipeline that automatically creates physical simulations from text descriptions. It uses a hierarchical agent system with specialized components (Planner, Writers, Critic) and a novel "Debug Card" mechanism for error repair. The system achieves superior quality over 4 state-of-the-art baselines on both automatic metrics and human preference studies across 42 test cases covering rigid, articulated, deformable, and cloth physics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Creating diverse physical simulations is currently labor-intensive because multiple interconnected components (assets, layout, physical parameters, motion, control, and rendering) must be designed and debugged jointly. This complexity makes simulation creation a bottleneck for applications requiring large-scale physical scenarios.

Method: Text2Sim employs a hierarchical agentic architecture built on the Genesis simulation framework. The system integrates: (1) a Planner for high-level task decomposition, (2) Specialized Writers for generating simulation code, (3) asset-generation tools, and (4) an independent Critic for quality assessment. A key innovation is the "Debug Cards" - compact skills distilled from graphics demonstrations that provide role-specific physical guidance for execution-based repair when simulations fail.

Result: Text2Sim outperforms all four state-of-the-art baselines on both automatic physical quality and visual quality scorers. In blinded user studies, significantly more participants prefer Text2Sim's outputs compared to baselines. The system handles diverse physics phenomena including rigid bodies, articulated objects, deformable materials, and cloth.

Conclusion: Text2Sim demonstrates that an agentic pipeline with specialized components and learned repair skills can effectively automate physical simulation creation from text prompts. The approach enables downstream applications such as dataset construction and multimodal input extension. The planned release of code, Debug Card library, and generated case datasets will support further research in this direction.

Abstract: Creating diverse physical simulations remains labor-intensive because assets, layout, physical parameters, motion, control, and rendering must be designed and debugged jointly. We present Text2Sim, a simulation-specialized agentic pipeline that converts a text-only request into an executable, editable dynamic case. Built on Genesis, Text2Sim uses a hierarchical agentic structure that combines a Planner with specialized Writers, asset-generation tools, and an independent Critic. Compact skills (Debug Cards) distilled from graphics demonstrations provide role-specific physical guidance for execution-based repair. We evaluate physical quality, visual quality, and human preference on 42 held-out prompts spanning rigid, articulated, deformable, and cloth phenomena, with a paper-level split between experience construction and evaluation. We design automatic physical and visual scorers to evaluate the quality of the results, and Text2Sim achieves higher scores than all four state-of-the-art baselines on both metrics. In blinded user studies with these baselines, significantly more participants prefer Text2Sim than prefer the baselines, which is consistent with the results from our automatic scorers. The pipeline also supports a broad range of downstream applications; we select dataset construction and extension to multimodal input as two representative examples. We will release the code, the Debug Card library, and a dataset of generated cases, each pairing the text prompt and rendered video with the executable program, assets, physical parameters, controls, and recorded states.

</details>


### [34] [Volcanite: Commodity-Hardware Segmentation Volume Visualization for Connectomics and Beyond](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36898)
*Max Piochowiak,Reiner Dolp,Julian Herold,Eric Behle,Carsten Dachsbacher,Alexander Schug*

Main category: cs.GR

TL;DR: Volcanite is an open-source framework that enables direct, interactive exploration of terabyte-scale 3D segmentation volumes on commodity hardware, without requiring meshing or distributed infrastructure. It uses compression-aware data handling and a Vulkan GPU backend to render up to 500 billion voxels at 100 FPS, preserving voxel labels while adding visual effects like shadows and global illumination.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern imaging produces massive segmentation volumes (terabyte-scale) with categorical, label-rich data. However, the scale forces researchers to use workarounds like slice-based interpretation, approximate meshes, or distributed workflows that obscure spatial context and voxel-level details. There was a need for a tool that enables direct exploration of these volumes on commodity hardware without lengthy preprocessing.

Method: The paper presents Volcanite, which combines: (1) compression-aware data handling, (2) a Vulkan GPU backend, and (3) segmentation-specific rendering techniques. This approach enables low-latency exploration without meshing or distributed infrastructure.

Result: Volcanite can render up to half a trillion voxels (500 billion) at 100 frames per second on commodity hardware. It preserves voxel labels, adds visual enhancements (shadows, global illumination), and works across multi-domain datasets including unpublished, sensitive or proprietary data.

Conclusion: Volcanite decouples hypothesis generation from data preparation by replacing lengthy preprocessing with direct inspection. It transforms teravoxel label fields into interactive evidence for discovery, validation, and cross-domain spatial analysis, making large-scale segmentation data more accessible for exploration and analysis.

Abstract: Modern imaging produces terabyte-scale segmentation volumes, assigning each voxel an object label. These categorical, boundary-sensitive and label-rich data underpin connectomics and other imaging-driven fields, yet their scale often forces interpretation through slices, approximate meshes or distributed workflows that obscure spatial context and voxel-level defects. Here we show that such volumes can be explored directly on commodity hardware with Volcanite, an open-source framework for dense-segmentation rendering. Combining compression-aware data handling, a Vulkan GPU backend and segmentation-specific rendering, Volcanite enables low-latency exploration without meshing or distributed infrastructure, including for unpublished, sensitive or proprietary data. Across multi-domain datasets, it preserves voxel labels, adds shadows and global illumination, and renders up to half a trillion voxels at 100 frames per second. By replacing lengthy preprocessing with direct inspection, Volcanite decouples hypothesis generation from data preparation and turns teravoxel label fields into interactive evidence for discovery, validation and cross-domain spatial analysis

</details>


### [35] [Length-varying Neural Motion Stitching via Cluster Transition Graph](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37167)
*Haemin Kim,Junghyun Nam,Seokhyeon Hong,Vanessa Tan,Junyong Noh*

Main category: cs.GR

TL;DR: A length-varying neural motion stitching method using a cluster transition graph that adaptively generates transitions of varying lengths between distinct motion sequences through motion clustering, cluster pathfinding, and Transformer-based motion generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing motion stitching approaches require manual selection of transition range or assume fixed transition length, which restricts the diversity of motions that can be connected. When input motions differ significantly, the character needs sufficient time to adapt its pose, necessitating transitions of varying lengths.

Method: The proposed method uses a cluster transition graph with three stages: 1) Motion clustering - maps input motions to discrete clusters; 2) Cluster pathfinding - identifies corresponding clusters in the graph and searches for a connecting path where nodes represent motion clusters and directed edges indicate valid transitions; 3) Motion generation - uses a Transformer encoder-based motion generator with the path and input motions to produce final transition poses.

Result: The method can adaptively adjust motion length and successfully generate plausible transitions between distinct motions such as crawling, basketball shooting, and slow locomotion. Using a graph structure effectively estimates transition durations compared to fixed-length methods.

Conclusion: The cluster transition graph approach effectively estimates transition durations and produces high-fidelity results compared to methods with fixed transition lengths. The framework successfully handles diverse motions like crawling, basketball shooting, and slow locomotion.

Abstract: Motion stitching aims to create new character animations by seamlessly combining existing motion sequences. Existing approaches often require manual selection of transition range or assume fixed transition length, restricting the types of motions that can be connected. To broaden the diversity of motions that can be synthesized, it is essential to generate transitions of varying lengths, allowing the character sufficient time to adapt its pose when the input motions differ significantly. To this end, we propose a length-varying neural motion stitching method based on a cluster transition graph, which produces naturally connected motion sequences given two distinct input motions. Our framework consists of three stages: motion clustering, cluster pathfinding, and motion generation. First, motion clustering maps input motions to discrete clusters. Next, we identify the corresponding clusters in the cluster transition graph and search for a connecting path. In this graph, nodes represent motion clusters, and directed edges indicate valid transitions between them. The resulting path determines both the transition length and a guide sequence that informs motion generation. Finally, the path and input motions are provided to a Transformer encoder-based motion generator to produce the final transition poses. Experimental results demonstrate that our method adaptively adjusts the motion length and successfully generates plausible transitions between distinct motions, such as crawling, basketball shooting, and slow locomotion. We also show that using a graph structure effectively estimates transition durations and produces high-fidelity results compared to methods that assume a fixed transition length, or directly compute the time.

</details>


<div id='cs.SE'></div>

# cs.SE [[Back]](#toc)

### [36] [Agent-Callable Feature Coverage: Measuring Software Readiness for AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35789)
*Zedong Peng*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper addresses how well software systems support AI agents through structured interfaces, introducing the GUI-API parity principle. Two key contributions are proposed: Agent-Callable Feature Coverage (ACFC) as a readiness metric and Agent Readiness Conformance (ARC) as a scoring framework evaluating Accessibility, Discoverability, and Controllability on 0-3 scales. Empirical study of 30 systems and controlled testing of 10 systems reveal that most systems achieve moderate API coverage but lack agent-oriented documentation and safety governance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI agents can already operate software through screenshot-based computer use, but the critical question is how well software supports them through structured, controllable channels. The need is formalized as the GUI-API parity principle: every capability available to human users through GUI should be accessible to agents through structured, callable interfaces with safety metadata.

Method: The authors introduce two contributions: (1) Agent-Callable Feature Coverage (ACFC), a product-level readiness metric quantifying how much of a system's human-facing functionality agents can access and use; (2) Agent Readiness Conformance (ARC), a framework scoring each capability on three axes (Accessibility, Discoverability, Controllability) each on a 0-3 scale, yielding a composite 0-9 score. The methodology includes an empirical study of 30 software systems across five categories and a controlled testbed of ten deployed systems tested with three agent models including Google's Gemma4.

Result: ARC-based ACFC scores are substantially lower than Accessibility-only metrics would suggest. Most systems achieve moderate API coverage but lag on agent-oriented documentation and safety governance. In controlled testing, API-only agents completed 56 of 58 tasks when target capabilities were exposed through structured interfaces; a documentation ablation reduced this to 50 of 58, demonstrating that Discoverability affects execution even among accessible capabilities. Inaccessible capabilities (negative controls) were not solved.

Conclusion: The GUI-API parity principle provides a framework for evaluating software readiness for AI agents. The ARC framework reveals that structured interface exposure alone is insufficient—documentation and safety governance are critical for agent task completion. The findings hold across multiple agent models including local deployment of Gemma4, suggesting broad implications for software design to support AI agents.

Abstract: AI agents already operate graphical software through screenshot-based computer use, so the pressing question is not whether agents can operate software, but how well software supports them through structured, controllable channels. We formalize this need as the GUI-API parity principle: every capability available to human users through a graphical interface should also be accessible to agents through a structured, callable interface with appropriate safety metadata. To operationalize this principle, we introduce two contributions. First, Agent-Callable Feature Coverage (ACFC), a product-level readiness metric quantifying how much of a system's human-facing functionality agents can access and use. Second, Agent Readiness Conformance (ARC), a framework that scores each capability on three axes: Accessibility (can an agent call it), Discoverability (can an agent find and understand it), and Controllability (can an agent use it safely), each on a 0-3 scale, yielding a composite 0-9 readiness score. Drawing on the Richardson Maturity Model, ARC provides finer-grained assessment than binary coverage. In an empirical study of 30 software systems across five categories, ARC-based ACFC scores are substantially lower than Accessibility-only metrics would suggest: most systems achieve moderate API coverage but lag on agent-oriented documentation and safety governance. In a controlled testbed of ten deployed systems, API-only agents completed 56 of 58 tasks whose target capabilities were exposed through structured interfaces; a documentation ablation reduced this to 50 of 58, showing that Discoverability affects execution even among accessible capabilities, while inaccessible capabilities, included as negative controls, were not solved. These findings hold across three agent models, including Google's open-weights Gemma4 run locally.

</details>


### [37] [A Leakage-Safe, Cost-Aware Regression Testing Methodology for the Quantum Transpiler](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35834)
*Furqan Nasir,Muhammad Arif Shah,Ifitkhar Alam*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper presents a leakage-safe, cost-aware regression-test-selection methodology for the Qiskit quantum transpiler. The study finds that a transparent risk_score selector underperforms simple baselines, with diversity-only selection achieving the best AUC (0.874 vs 0.721). A decomposed, diversity-first selector subsequently closes this gap. The study is notable for reporting a negative result as a pre-registered finding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum SDKs like Qiskit require continuous revision, where a single transpiler-pass change can introduce software regressions. Continuous Integration (CI) systems must select and prioritize tests from a large, cost-heterogeneous test suite under fixed budget constraints. The challenge is to develop an effective test selection methodology that avoids data leakage while optimizing detection of regressions.

Method: The study uses a pre-registered, budget-binding evaluation methodology to avoid data leakage. They used a cost-heterogeneous corpus of 116 test units with a per-unit cost spread of 9,945X (T_full = 69.17s). Budgets were fixed based on measured cost before any test-oracle labels were read. They evaluated a risk_score selector against multiple baselines (diversity-only, cost-only, history-only, change-stage, random) and performed component ablation to identify effective signals. A second independent evaluation used 19 mutation-testing events (9 original + 10 new, verified operators) to test the decomposed, diversity-first selector.

Result: The transparent risk_score selector did not beat simple test case-prioritization baselines. Mean detection-vs-budget AUC was 0.721 [95% CI 0.44, 0.94] for risk_score versus 0.874 [0.65, 1.00] for diversity-only (Cliff's δ = -0.64, large effect). Other baselines: cost/history/change-stage achieved 0.840, random achieved 0.821. Component ablation revealed that diversity and novelty are the effective signals, not severity or cost. The second evaluation with the decomposed, diversity-first selector closed the gap to the strongest baseline (0.880 vs 0.880, δ = -0.08, negligible effect) while decisively beating the original composite (0.880 vs 0.768, δ = +0.57). Two verified forward-regression events from real Qiskit CI history corroborated these findings.

Conclusion: The study reports a negative result honestly as a pre-registered finding with its mechanism explained. The key finding is that diversity and novelty are the effective signals for test selection, not severity or cost. A decomposed, diversity-first selector outperforms the original composite risk_score approach. The study emphasizes the importance of pre-registration and transparent reporting of negative results in empirical software engineering. All code, data, and artifacts are released for reproducibility.

Abstract: Quantum SDKs such as Qiskit are revised continually, and a single transpiler-pass change can introduce a software regression, so continuous integration (CI) must select and prioritize tests from a large, cost-heterogeneous suite under a fixed budget. We present a leakage-safe, cost-aware regression-test-selection methodology for the Qiskit quantum transpiler, with a pre-registered, budget binding evaluation that avoids data leakage. The evaluation uses a cost-heterogeneous corpus (116 units, per-unit cost spread 9,945X, T_full = 69.17 s) whose budgets were fixed from measured cost before any test-oracle label was read. A transparent risk_score selector does not beat simple test case-prioritization baselines: mean detection-vs-budget AUC is 0.721 [95% CI 0.44, 0.94] versus 0.874 [0.65, 1.00] for diversity-only (Cliff's δ = -0.64, large), with cost/history/change-stage baselines at 0.840 and random at 0.821. A component ablation shows the composite underperforms its own best signal: diversity and novelty, not severity or cost, are the effective signal. A second, independently pre-registered evaluation (19 mutation-testing events: nine original plus ten new, verified operators) tests the decomposed, diversity-first selector this mechanism implies: it closes the gap to the strongest baseline to a negligible effect size (0.880 vs 0.880, δ = -0.08) while decisively beating the original composite (0.880 vs 0.768, δ = +0.57). Two verified forward-regression events from real Qiskit CI history corroborate it, reported per event, not pooled, under a pre-declared claim-scope rule. We report the negative result as an honest, pre-registered finding with its mechanism. All code, data, and artifacts from this empirical software engineering study are released for reproducibility.

</details>


### [38] [Beyond Rule-Based Mutation Testing: Test-Aware Mutant Generation Using Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35841)
*Nils Kiele,Zainab Saad,Zirui Wang,Steve Drew,Samira Ebrahimi Kahou*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper proposes "test-aware mutant generation" where an LLM is given problem statements, canonical solutions, and base tests in a single prompt to generate non-trivial mutants that pass base tests but represent genuine bugs. This approach achieves 87.7% and 79.1% verified fault rates on HumanEval and MBPP benchmarks, significantly outperforming test-blind prompting (12.2%, 23.0%) and traditional rule-based tools like mutmut (4.4%, 5.7%).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional mutation testing generates many trivial, redundant, or equivalent mutants that limit practical use. While LLM-based approaches generate more realistic faults, they remain test-blind—they cannot reason about what existing tests already cover, leading to inefficient mutant generation that wastes effort on bugs tests already catch.

Method: The authors propose test-aware mutant generation: an LLM receives the problem statement, canonical solution, and base tests in a single prompt, and must generate a non-trivial mutant that passes all base tests. They evaluated across 5 LLMs (Gemini 3.1 Pro, Gemini 3 Flash, GPT 5.1 Codex Mini, GPT 4.1 Mini, Qwen3-32B) on HumanEval and MBPP benchmarks, using EvalPlus extended test suites as automated oracle to verify whether surviving mutants represent genuine bugs.

Result: Test-aware prompting yields verified fault rates of 87.7% (HumanEval) and 79.1% (MBPP)—meaning these mutants pass all base tests but are caught by the oracle. This vastly outperforms matched test-blind prompting (12.2% and 23.0%) and traditional rule-based tool mutmut (4.4% and 5.7%). Fault subtlety (fraction of extended tests a mutant fails) remains comparable across methods, but test-awareness minimizes computational cost per verified fault.

Conclusion: Exposing an LLM to existing unit tests shifts mutant generation from untargeted bug injection toward effective discovery of weaknesses in an existing test suite. This approach provides a concrete foundation for scaling test-aware mutant generation to production-level environments for improved test suite quality assessment.

Abstract: Mutation testing evaluates test-suite adequacy by injecting synthetic faults into program code. However, traditional rule-based tools often generate large numbers of trivial, redundant, or equivalent mutants that limit their practical use for identifying gaps in a test suite. While recent large language model (LLM)-based approaches generate more realistic faults, most remain test-blind: The model sees only the source code and cannot reason about what existing tests already cover. We propose test-aware mutant generation, in which an LLM receives the problem statement, canonical solution and base tests in a single prompt, and must generate a nontrivial mutant that passes the base unit tests. We evaluate this approach across a set of five LLMs -- Gemini 3.1 Pro, Gemini 3 Flash, GPT 5.1 Codex Mini, GPT 4.1 Mini, Qwen3-32B -- on the HumanEval and MBPP benchmarks. The extended EvalPlus test suites serve as an automated oracle to verify whether surviving mutants represent genuine bugs. Test-aware prompting yields verified fault rates of 87.7% (HumanEval) and 79.1% (MBPP), meaning these mutants pass all base tests but are caught by the oracle. This vastly outperforms the matched test-blind prompting (which yields only 12.2% and 23.0%, respectively) and the traditional rule-based tool mutmut (4.4% and 5.7%). While fault subtlety (the fraction of extended tests a mutant fails) remains comparable across all three methods, test-awareness minimizes the computational cost per verified fault, compared to test-blind prompting. Exposing an LLM to existing unit tests shifts mutant generation from untargeted bug injection toward effective discovery of weaknesses in an existing test suite. Our work establishes a concrete foundation for future research to scale test-aware mutant generation to production-level environments.

</details>


### [39] [PoliVEM: a Python-driven virtual element framework for computational solid mechanics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35878)
*Paulo Akira F. Enabe,Rodrigo Provasi*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: PoliVEM is a comprehensive software framework implementing the Virtual Element Method (VEM) for computational solid and structural mechanics. It provides a unified C++17 core with Python interface to handle 1D beams, 2D/3D elasticity, axisymmetric elasticity, transient diffusion, and finite-strain hyperelasticity in a single implementation architecture.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to create a unified software framework that places various VEM formulations (beams, elasticity, diffusion, hyperelasticity) in a common implementation, avoiding separate codebases for each problem type and maximizing code reuse across different physical problems.

Method: The framework uses a hierarchical storage of degrees of freedom (vertices, edges, faces, cells), constructs projections from common polynomial data, and maintains the consistency-stabilization split at the element level. It employs composition to separate mesh, material, element, assembler, and solver responsibilities. Key features include polygonal/polyhedral mesh input, higher-order entity numbering, cached projection operators, static condensation, colored sparse assembly, and incremental Newton method with line search and regularization.

Result: The numerical experiments verify the shared implementation through polynomial reproduction, recovery after static condensation, higher-order DOF handling, polygonal/polyhedral meshes, patch tests, stiffness spectra, and comparisons with independent FEM calculations. The framework successfully handles beam and 2D/3D elasticity formulations. However, two main limitations were identified: (1) scaling issues of high-order polynomial bases on anisotropic cells, and (2) memory requirements of sparse direct factorizations.

Conclusion: PoliVEM provides a well-designed, reusable VEM framework with broad applicability across multiple physics problems. The common core architecture enables efficient code reuse. The main limitations are computational: high-order polynomial basis scaling on anisotropic meshes and memory consumption for sparse direct solvers, which should be addressed in future work.

Abstract: This work presents PoliVEM, a software framework for the Virtual Element Method (VEM) in computational solid and structural mechanics. A C++17 computational core and a Python interface place one-dimensional beams, two- and three-dimensional elasticity, axisymmetric elasticity, transient diffusion, and finite-strain hyperelasticity in a common implementation. The framework stores vertex, edge, face, and cell degrees of freedom in one hierarchy, constructs the energy, strain, and $L^2$ projections from common polynomial data, and retains the consistency--stabilization split at the element level. The core separates the mesh, material, element, assembler, and solver responsibilities and combines them by composition. A new formulation supplies its projection, discrete form, and stabilization while reusing the mesh representation, degree-of-freedom numbering, boundary-condition treatment, sparse assembly, algebraic solvers, and Python binding pattern. The numerical infrastructure includes polygonal and polyhedral mesh input, higher-order entity numbering, cached projection operators, static condensation, coloured sparse assembly, linear solver selection based on the matrix structure, an incremental Newton method with line search and regularization, and explicit and implicit time integration. Three numerical experiments examine the shared implementation through polynomial reproduction, recovery after static condensation, higher-order degree-of-freedom handling, polygonal and polyhedral meshes, patch tests, stiffness spectra, and comparisons with independent finite element calculations. The results verify the common core for the beam and two- and three-dimensional elasticity formulations. They also identify the scaling of high-order polynomial bases on anisotropic cells and the memory required by sparse direct factorizations as the main limitations of the current implementation.

</details>


### [40] [The Artifact Promotion Control Model: An Implementation Case Study. Build on Target Machines vs. Build Once and Promote Artifacts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35891)
*Vasili Gavrilov,Enkli Ylli*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper presents a control model for artifact promotion (building once and moving the same build through test/production) in cloud deployment, with a case study demonstrating a one-command release process on AWS achieving 31 production rollouts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To present artifact promotion as a control model for cloud deployment that meets various regulatory requirements (FedRAMP, SOX 404, FFIEC, DO-178C, FDA 21 CFR Part 11, HIPAA, DoD IL) more directly than build-on-target approaches, and to demonstrate its practical implementation.

Method: Part I: Restates the model in stricter form including release objects, control domains, artifact-versus-environment identity distinction, source-control compromise analysis, and regulatory control mapping. Part II: Anonymized case study of a production web application on AWS with 31 rollouts analyzed.

Result: Part II reports 31 production rollouts (36s per server, six-minute total rollout), 22 releases in first four weeks, with a control table showing implementation has certain controls but lacks signature, scan, approval record, and deploy verification. Two release units were not covered by the pipeline.

Conclusion: The paper assembles existing concepts (build-once principle, build/release/run split, binary authorization, separation as supply-chain property, NIST SP 800-204D) into one model, establishes that secrets injected at build time break artifact identity, and derives that the releasing role needs no production password.

Abstract: Artifact promotion means building the software once and moving that same built copy through the test and production environments, instead of building it again on each server. A previous treatment by the first author presented it as a control model for cloud deployment in accessible engineering prose. Part I of this paper restates the model in stricter form: the release object, the control domains a deployment crosses, the artifact-versus-environment identity distinction, source-control compromise as a control-domain question, and the regulatory controls (FedRAMP/SI-7, SOX 404, FFIEC, DO-178C, FDA 21 CFR Part 11, HIPAA, DoD IL) whose integrity and change-control requirements the model's properties meet more directly than build-on-target does. The parts of the model are each in the literature already: the build-once principle, the build/release/run split, binary authorization, separation as a supply-chain property, NIST SP 800-204D. The paper assembles them as one model, whatever form the built copy takes; states that a secret injected at build time breaks artifact identity; restates why downloading libraries at deployment time is an attack surface that grows with their number; and derives that the releasing role needs no production password. Part II is an anonymized case study of a production web application on AWS in which a release is one command and the artifact's path from upload to running fleet is autonomous. It reports 31 production rollouts timed stage by stage from the platform's records (36 s per server inside a six-minute rollout), 22 releases in the first four weeks, the application's service levels, a control table stating which controls the implementation has and which it lacks (no signature, no scan, no approval record, no verification at deploy), and the two release units the pipeline did not cover. A closing table checks each claim of Part I against that record.

</details>


### [41] [UNBIND: UNlearning By INference-time Directional Steering for Code LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35913)
*Zhengyang Shan,Jiayun Xin,Yanjun Lin,Xu Qian,Zhiang Liu,Minghui Xu,Yue Zhang,Qin Hu,Kun Li,Xiuzhen Cheng*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: UNBIND is a code unlearning framework that enables selective forgetting of specific code implementations in large language models while preserving general programming capabilities. It addresses the tension between removing copyrighted or sensitive code and maintaining useful programming knowledge by separately identifying hidden states corresponding to target code and suppressing its reproduction at inference time without modifying model weights.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Code large language models can memorize implementations from training data that may have copyright or security concerns. When such code needs to be removed, there's a fundamental tension: targeted code and retained code share computational patterns, making it difficult to forget specific implementations while preserving general programming abilities. Current methods struggle to achieve both selective forgetting and utility preservation simultaneously.

Method: UNBIND constructs separate directions for two objectives: (1) identifying which hidden states correspond to target code, and (2) suppressing its reproduction. The framework operates at inference time with fixed model weights, allowing selective unlearning without retraining. Evaluation was conducted across 14 baselines using two code models (likely CodeLlama and similar) and two corpora.

Result: UNBIND achieves the highest joint forgetting and utility score in every setting. It reduces target code reproduction by 97.3% to 99.1% (measured by F-BLEU), with minimal utility loss (at most 2 fewer HumanEval+ and 6 fewer MBPP+ problems solved). In repeated extraction tests, exact spans of 50+ tokens dropped from 188-262 to 0-2 out of 300. No extracted span reached 100 tokens, and mean best recovery ratio ranged from 0.43% to 6.45%.

Conclusion: UNBIND presents a practical approach to selective code unlearning that effectively removes target code reproduction while maintaining useful programming capabilities. The framework shows effectiveness across multilingual and related-code evaluations, demonstrating its applicability to real-world copyright and security concerns in code generation models.

Abstract: Code large language models acquire programming capabilities from large code corpora, but can also memorize implementations that later require removal. Code unlearning is needed to control their continued reproduction when copyright or security concerns arise. However, targeted and retained code share computational patterns, creating a tension between forgetting specific implementations and preserving general programming ability. We propose \textbf{UNBIND}, a code unlearning framework that separately considers which hidden states correspond to the target code and how to suppress its reproduction. By constructing separate directions for these objectives, UNBIND achieves selective unlearning at inference time while keeping model weights fixed. Our evaluation covers fourteen baselines across two code models and two corpora. UNBIND achieves the highest joint forgetting and utility score in every setting. It reduces target code reproduction by 97.3\% to 99.1\% as measured by F-BLEU, with at most two fewer HumanEval+ and six fewer MBPP+ problems solved than the original models. In repeated extraction tests under a fixed budget, the number of targets yielding exact spans of at least 50 tokens falls from 188--262 to 0--2 out of 300 per setting. No extracted span reaches 100 tokens, and the mean best recovery ratio ranges from 0.43\% to 6.45\%. Multilingual and related-code evaluations further show effective forgetting with limited impact on useful programming capabilities, supporting UNBIND as a practical approach to selective code unlearning.

</details>


### [42] [Evaluating Name-Only Directory Routing for One-Shot Code Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35918)
*Manoj Bajaj*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper evaluates whether language models can use directory and file names to find annotated code files that are missed by fixed lexical queries. Across 82 issues from 11 repositories, name-only directory routing recovered 46.5% of gold files within eight candidates, outperforming FTS5 (35.2%) and fixed rg queries (24.5%).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Finding the right files is an early challenge for coding agents. The study tests whether a language model can follow directory and file names to find annotated code files that are missed by fixed lexical queries.

Method: The researchers compared three methods across 82 audited issues from 11 repositories at pinned pre-fix commits: (1) name-only directory routing, (2) FTS5 full-text search, and (3) fixed full-issue rg query. They evaluated under an 8-file limit and a shared 16K-token context budget, and also tested combining routing with FTS5 and an exploratory flat path control.

Result: Directory routing recovered 0.465 of gold files within eight candidates, outperforming FTS5 (0.352) and fixed rg query (0.245). The paired gain over FTS5 was 0.113 (95% CI: 0.053 to 0.168). Under the 16K-token budget, routing delivered 0.443 of annotated lines versus 0.246 for FTS5. Combining routing with FTS5 reached 0.491 file recall. The exploratory flat path control achieved 0.572 recall but required 24.6 model calls per issue versus 8.9 for routing. Routing averaged 8.9 seconds per issue; FTS5 took 7 milliseconds after a 0.9-second build.

Conclusion: Directory routing added relevant file candidates to one-shot lexical search, but the study cannot attribute the gain to hierarchy or demonstrate that it improves issue resolution.

Abstract: Finding the right files is an early challenge for coding agents. We test whether a language model can follow directory and file names to find annotated code files missed by fixed lexical queries. Across 82 audited issues from 11 repositories at pinned pre-fix commits, name-only directory routing recovered 0.465 of gold files within eight candidates, compared with 0.352 for FTS5 and 0.245 for a fixed full-issue rg query. The paired gain over FTS5 was 0.113 (95% repository-cluster bootstrap interval, 0.053 to 0.168). Under a shared 16K-token context budget, routing delivered 0.443 of annotated lines versus 0.246 for FTS5 on 55 cases with fully aligned annotations. At the same eight-file limit, combining routing with FTS5 reached 0.491 file recall, but its gain over routing alone was uncertain. An exploratory flat path control reached 0.572 recall while using 24.6 model calls per issue, compared with 8.9 for routing. Routing averaged 8.9 seconds per issue; FTS5 took 7 milliseconds per query after a 0.9-second build. On this cohort, directory routing added relevant file candidates to one-shot lexical search, but the study cannot attribute the gain to hierarchy or show that it improves issue resolution.

</details>


### [43] [The Uneven Decline of Collective Knowledge Production: Evidence from Stack Overflow After Generative AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36069)
*Myokyung Han,Taegyoon Kim,Jinhyuk Yun,Lanu Kim*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This study examines how the release of ChatGPT-3.5 has affected collective knowledge on Stack Overflow, one of the largest software engineering knowledge-sharing platforms. Analyzing over 2 million questions from 2020-2025, the research finds that Gen AI is eroding easy, accessible knowledge first while more complex, less common knowledge persists. Easy questions declined sharply while difficult questions increased; data-rich topics lost share while data-scarce ones gained. The impact is uneven across programming languages, with more prevalent languages showing sharper shifts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work documented an aggregate decline in participation on knowledge-sharing platforms after Gen AI's release, but it remained unclear which specific kinds of knowledge are being lost first. The researchers aimed to understand which dimensions of collective knowledge are most affected by Gen AI.

Method: The study treats the release of ChatGPT-3.5 as a natural shock and analyzes over 2 million questions posted between 2020-2025 on Stack Overflow. They track two dimensions of collective knowledge: difficulty and data availability. They use diverse methods and robust checks to validate findings, examining patterns across programming languages (especially Python) and analyzing code complexity as a corroborating measure.

Result: 1) Easy questions decline sharply while difficult questions become more common, with rising code complexity corroborating this pattern
2) Data-rich topics and tags lose share of questions while data-scarce ones gain ground
3) The two dimensions interact: decline in easy questions is concentrated in data-rich domains, while difficult questions increase regardless of data availability
4) The pattern extends across programming languages, with more prevalent languages showing sharper shifts

Conclusion: Gen AI's impact on collective knowledge is uneven—it erodes easy, accessible knowledge first while more complex, less common knowledge persists. This suggests a concerning trajectory where the accessible, foundational knowledge base may degrade over time, potentially making it harder for newcomers to learn and for the community to maintain shared knowledge resources.

Abstract: Generative AI (Gen AI) is reshaping how individuals learn and work, but its consequences for collective knowledge, the shared body of knowledge that online communities produce together, remain poorly understood. Prior work has documented an aggregate decline in participation on knowledge-sharing platforms, but it remains unclear which specific kinds of knowledge are being lost first. We study this question using Stack Overflow, one of the largest online communities for software engineering, treating the release of ChatGPT-3.5 as a natural shock. Analyzing over two million questions posted between 2020 and 2025, we track how two dimensions of collective knowledge, difficulty and data availability, change following Gen AI's release. Using diverse methods and robust checks, we find consistent patterns. Easy questions decline sharply while difficult questions become more common, a pattern corroborated by rising code complexity. Data-rich topics and tags lose share of questions, while data-scarce ones gain ground. The two dimensions also interact: the decline in easy questions is concentrated specifically within data-rich domains, while difficult questions increase regardless of data availability. This pattern extends beyond Python across programming languages, with more prevalent languages showing sharper shifts. Together, our findings reveal that Gen AI's impact on collective knowledge is uneven, eroding easy, accessible knowledge first while more complex, less common knowledge persists.

</details>


### [44] [The Invisible Scheduler: Dragging a Sprite Can Change What a Scratch Program Does](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36147)
*Xinyue Feng,Hanyuan Shi,Yuan Si*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper addresses a critical concurrency bug in Scratch programming where sprite stacking order (determined by z-index) affects program execution but is invisible to code. Since Scratch allows any sprite to start scripts when the green flag is clicked, and the order they execute is determined by their front-to-back stacking order (which can be changed by dragging), programs can behave differently on different computers despite having identical blocks. The authors developed StackSwap to systematically test all possible stacking orders and identify scheduling-dependent bugs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The core problem is that Scratch programs are inherently concurrent - multiple sprite scripts run simultaneously when the green flag is clicked. However, the initial execution order is determined by visual stacking order (which sprite appears in front), which is not accessible through any Scratch block. This creates non-deterministic behavior: a program that works perfectly for a student may fail on a teacher's computer simply because the sprites were arranged differently. This is a fundamental fairness and reproducibility issue in educational programming.

Method: The authors developed StackSwap, a tool that: (1) Exploits the observation that with fixed project files, rearranging sprite positions produces all permutations of initial stacking order; (2) Runs a project under all possible stacking orders - exhaustively for up to 5 sprites, and using conflict graph analysis for more; (3) Compares runs under four observation lenses (likely program output, variable values, broadcast messages, and sprite positions); (4) Returns either a two-run witness showing which adjacent sprite pair swap changed the outcome, or a robustness certificate if all orders produce the same result, or an unresolved verdict; (5) Analyzes 767 real programs from a course, an online judge, and random public samples.

Result: Key findings: (1) 26.4% of course programs and 18.2% of public programs with scheduling choices behave differently under some stacking order; (2) Among sensitive programs where all orders executed, the saved order's outcome recurs in only 17% (course) and 50% (public) of orders; (3) Under scripted play testing, 5 out of 427 student submissions with scheduling choices had requirement failures under one order but not another; (4) For half of the sensitive programs, the random number stream is the highest-priority shared resource causing the race condition.

Conclusion: The authors conclude that Scratch's implicit scheduling based on stacking order is a significant source of non-deterministic behavior that affects real-world programs. They provide recommendations for: (1) Learners - to be aware of this issue and avoid race conditions; (2) Graders - to test programs under multiple stacking orders; (3) The Scratch platform - to either expose stacking order to blocks, provide deterministic scheduling options, or at minimum warn users about this behavior.
