The paper demonstrates this is not a theoretical concern but a practical issue affecting 18-26% of programs with concurrent scripts.

Abstract: Tens of millions of children program in Scratch, whose programs are concurrent: when the green flag is clicked, every sprite's scripts start together and share the project's state. Which script starts first is decided by the sprites' front-to-back stacking order, which no block reads. Dragging a sprite brings it to the front, and the order is saved with the project. Hence, a program can work on the author's screen and fail on the teacher's, with the same blocks. Our key observation is that, with the rest of the file fixed, rearranging the sprites that start scripts among their positions produces exactly the permutations of their initial stacking order. Under a fixed input and seed the unmodified virtual machine reproduces every one of them. StackSwap runs a project under these orders, all of them for up to five sprites, one per class of a conflict graph beyond, and a sample where neither is feasible, and compares the runs under four observation lenses. It returns a two-run witness naming an adjacent pair of sprites whose swap changes the outcome, with the resources they share as candidate causes; otherwise an exhaustive or graph-conditional robustness certificate, or an unresolved verdict. On 767 real programs from a course, an online judge, and a random public sample, 26.4% (course) and 18.2% (public) of those with a scheduling choice behave differently under some stacking order. Among the sensitive programs whose every order ran, the saved order's outcome recurs under a median 17% (course) and 50% (public) of the orders. Under scripted play, a predicate written from a stated requirement holds under one order and fails under another for 5 of 427 student submissions with a scheduling choice. For half of the sensitive programs the random stream is the racing pair's highest-priority shared resource. We close with recommendations for learners, graders, and the Scratch platform.

</details>


### [45] [Live Architecture Models for Cloud-Native Architecture-as-Code: Early Results from Kubernetes Conformance Checking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36148)
*Denis Mamatin,Andrey Salov*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper presents Archer, a VS Code prototype that maintains live architecture models for Kubernetes systems by connecting editable KDL (Kubernetes Deployment Language) representations to runtime facts through explicit correspondences and recurring conformance checks. The approach enables snapshot recovery of Kubernetes facts into KDL and detects model-cluster inconsistencies without enforcing or repairing deployment state.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Infrastructure drift can separate a running Kubernetes system from its documented architectural intent, creating a need for synchronized architectural representations that can track and detect runtime deviations from the intended architecture.

Method: The approach uses an Archer-specific subset of Kubernetes Deployment Language (KDL) with a VS Code prototype featuring synchronized textual and graphical views. Snapshot recovery extracts selected Kubernetes facts into KDL; periodic and on-demand read-only checks report model-cluster inconsistencies. Evaluation uses three feature-selected Kubernetes example applications with precision/recall metrics for snapshot recovery and inconsistency detection, using a documented manual perturbation protocol.

Result: Results support feasibility within the evaluated KDL scope. Recovery scores are reproducible from saved ground-truth and recovered models. However, the paper does not establish comparative superiority, detection of arbitrary production drift, scalability, or developer benefit. Limitations include storage coverage and ingress-host representation/default handling.

Conclusion: The approach demonstrates feasibility for live architecture models with explicit correspondences between architectural representations and Kubernetes runtime facts within the evaluated scope. However, further work is needed to address scalability, broader drift detection, and demonstrate developer benefits.

Abstract: Infrastructure drift can separate a running Kubernetes system from its documented architectural intent. This paper investigates live architecture models: editable architectural representations connected to selected runtime facts through explicit correspondences and recurring conformance checks. The approach is instantiated through an Archer-specific subset of Kubernetes Deployment Language (KDL) and Archer, a VS Code prototype with synchronized textual and graphical views. Snapshot recovery extracts selected Kubernetes facts into KDL; periodic and on-demand read-only checks report model-cluster inconsistencies without enforcing or repairing deployment state.
  We assess feasibility on three feature-selected Kubernetes example applications under an author-defined protocol, reporting precision and recall for snapshot recovery and selected inconsistency detection. Recovery scores can be reproduced from saved ground-truth and recovered models; detection uses a documented manual perturbation protocol. The results support feasibility within the evaluated KDL scope. They do not establish comparative superiority, detection of arbitrary production drift, scalability, or developer benefit. Storage coverage and ingress-host representation/default handling remain limitations.

</details>


### [46] [The Invisible Throttle: Running on Borrowed Time in Scratch](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36152)
*Xinyue Feng,Hanyuan Shi,Yuan Si*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper reveals a hidden performance bug in Scratch's virtual machine where non-drawing loops run dramatically faster (up to 77,000x) than drawing loops due to an undocumented "redraw gate" mechanism. The study analyzes 500 popular projects and proposes fixes for graders and the platform.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scratch has 135 million users (mostly children), but the undocumented behavior of its virtual machine's frame-based execution creates inconsistent and unpredictable loop speeds. The authors discovered that loops without visual output run at vastly different speeds depending on what else is visible, with no official documentation explaining this rule.

Method: The researchers implemented ThrottleCheck to emulate a budgeted semantics on the unmodified VM, tested 500 popular public games under various conditions (muting sprites, hiding sprites, running without renderer), and analyzed test suites from Whisker (a testing tool) and tutorials.

Result: 59% of 500 popular games contain loops that never draw and never wait. Muting drawing sprites affects game state after 10 seconds in 24% of games; hiding them affects 18%; running without renderer affects 61%. Time-sensitive tests fail/pass reversely once throttle is released in 17% of Whisker's tests and 19% of tutorial checks on 224 remixes.

Conclusion: The authors propose that graders should run without a renderer but emulate the redraw gate and state a budget. They provide specific recommendations for the Scratch platform and conclude with a simple sentence suitable for teaching children about the actual behavior.

Abstract: Scratch has 135 million registered users, most of them children, and 164 million shared projects. What they are taught about the speed of a script fits in one sentence: a loop iterates once per frame. Unfortunately, that sentence describes the exception. In the public virtual machine a frame repeats the scripts until something visible asks the screen to redraw, no script is left running, or three quarters of the frame's wall-clock time are spent. Hence a loop that does not draw is paced by whatever else is visible and by the machine. Hide the moving sprite of a two-sprite project, and the other's counting loop runs 77,000 times faster on a laptop. No documentation states the rule. Our key observation is that the redraw gate is one flag for the whole runtime, so a loop's speed can be changed without touching its code: hide the sprite that draws, change the machine's budget, or run the program in a tool with no renderer. ThrottleCheck implements a budgeted semantics (a stated budget of rounds per frame, the redraw gate emulated) on the unmodified virtual machine, runs a project under each knob, and reports a rate-sensitive project with a witness. On 500 popular public games, 59% contain a loop that never draws and never waits. Muting the requests of the sprites that draw changes the state of a played game after ten seconds in 24% of the games that have one; hiding them changes 18%; running the game without a renderer and without the gate changes 61%. Rules that read a position, a score or a clock after a fixed time reverse between pass and fail once the throttle is released, in 17% of Whisker's own example tests and 19% of a tutorial's checks on 224 of its remixes. A grader without a renderer grades a program the editor never runs, unless it emulates the gate and states a budget; we say what graders and the platform should do, and close with the sentence a child could be taught.

</details>


### [47] [From Dead Code and Static Requirements to Working Engines: Software Revival with Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36161)
*Tianyu Liu,Dingyuan Dai,Yufan Du,Zhen Yang*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: ReviveBench is a benchmark evaluating whether coding agents can restore non-functional software while preserving methods, and reconstruct industrial software engines from open specifications. It contains two task families (revival: 10 tasks; reconstruction: 13 tasks) evaluated by hidden verifiers. Key findings: strongest models pass all revival tasks; identifier obfuscation reduces line similarity without reducing pass rates; 28 verifier defects were uncovered, highlighting measurement error in executable verification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The research addresses whether coding agents can restore software that no longer runs while preserving its underlying methods, and reconstruct industrial software engines from open specifications. This is motivated by the need to evaluate coding agents' capabilities in software maintenance and reconstruction tasks that are critical in industrial settings.

Method: ReviveBench benchmark with two task families: (1) Revival family - 10 tasks involving dependency incompatibilities, deleted core modules, legacy builds, and GPU-based foundation models; (2) Reconstruction family - 13 tasks spanning numerical, geometric, hardware, and transactional systems (CAD, CRM). Evaluated by hidden verifiers calibrated against native execution environments, established engineering tools, or purpose-built reference implementations. Contamination-control experiments tested identifier obfuscation effects.

Result: Strongest model passes all 10 revival tasks. Identifier obfuscation reduces line similarity from 0.51-0.96 to 0.03-0.44 without reducing pass rates. On post knowledge-cutoff repositories, strongest model passes 8/9 runs. Two models meet pass criteria on all 13 reconstruction tasks. Audit revealed 28 verifier defects: 24 false negatives and 2 false positives.

Conclusion: Executable verification can introduce substantial measurement error. Three practical checks are proposed: (1) test whether prescribed methods can reach grading thresholds, (2) investigate agreement among independently generated candidates, and (3) recompute diagnostics from submitted artifacts. ReviveBench provides both evaluation of software revival and engine reconstruction capabilities, and demonstrates cases for validating verifiers used to measure coding agents.

Abstract: Can coding agents restore software that no longer runs while preserving its underlying methods, and reconstruct industrial software engines from open specifications? Here we introduce ReviveBench, a benchmark with two task families evaluated by hidden verifiers calibrated against native execution environments, established engineering tools, or purpose-built reference implementations. The revival family comprises ten tasks involving dependency incompatibilities, deleted core modules, legacy builds, and a GPU-based foundation model. Every starting workspace fails verification, and the strongest evaluated model passes all ten tasks in at least one run each. In contamination-control experiments, identifier obfuscation reduces line similarity to the original implementations from 0.51--0.96 to 0.03--0.44 without reducing the observed pass rate of any evaluated model. On repositories created after the stated knowledge cutoffs, the strongest model passes eight of nine runs. The reconstruction family comprises thirteen tasks spanning numerical, geometric, hardware, and transactional systems (e.g. CAD and CRM). Two models meet the benchmark's pass criteria on all thirteen, although our audit shows that the CFD task cannot establish numerical-solver capability. Benchmark construction and auditing uncover 28 verifier defects, including 24 false negatives and two false positives. These findings show that executable verification can itself introduce substantial measurement error. We present three practical checks: test whether prescribed methods can reach the grading thresholds, investigate agreement among independently generated candidates, and recompute diagnostics from submitted artifacts. ReviveBench thus provides both an evaluation of software revival and engine reconstruction, and cases in validating the verifiers used to measure coding agents for software design.

</details>


### [48] [Assay: Claims That Decay With the Code. Content-Addressed Evidence Graphs for Accountable AI-Assisted Software Delivery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36170)
*Om Shankar Tiwari,Tangi Vass,Gagan Deep Singh*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Assay is a tool that addresses AI coding agent failures by binding agent claims to Merkle hashes of code dependency cones, creating a traceability system that invalidates stale claims when dependencies change. It implements a merge gate with multiple verification criteria and achieves significant efficiency gains while blocking adversarial behaviors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI coding agents fail in two coupled ways: (1) they waste context rediscovering code locations, and (2) they falsely assert success without evidence when tasks become difficult. Existing solutions (repository indexes for context, adversarial review for accountability) describe the same object—the codebase structure—at different timescales, but this connection was not made operational.

Method: Assay binds every agent claim (tests pass, no secrets, behavior preserved) to the Merkle hash of the dependency cone of the code it covers. Claims become stale when the covered code or its dependencies change. The system implements a merge gate checking: coverage, freshness, signatures, exit codes, plausibility, evidence monotonicity, and review status. The approach uses dependency-free Python with an MCP server.

Result: - 600-token brief costs 14x to 114x less than exploration proxy
- Warm rebuilds are up to 5x faster than cold ones
- Cone binding re-verifies 7.9% to 81.9% of claims (vs repository binding re-verifies all, while per-module binding misses 23% to 68% of required invalidations)
- Merge gate blocks 9 of 9 scripted adversarial behaviors while admitting honest ones

Conclusion: Assay successfully operationalizes the connection between codebase structure and verification state. The dependency cone binding approach provides a sound, minimal, and efficient mechanism for tracking claim validity. The merge gate effectively blocks adversarial behaviors while maintaining throughput for legitimate contributions.

Abstract: AI coding agents fail in two coupled ways. They spend most of their context window rediscovering where things live, and they assert success without evidence when the work gets hard. Repository indexes address the first with cheap context, and orchestration frameworks with adversarial review address the second with accountability. Both describe the same object, the structure of the codebase, at two timescales: what is true of the code now, and what was verified to be true, at which revision, by whom. Assay makes that observation operational. Every claim an agent makes (tests pass, no secrets, behavior preserved) is bound to the Merkle hash of the dependency cone of the code it covers, so the claim is stale exactly when that code or anything it depends on changes. We show the binding is sound and minimal, and that the blast radius of a change is precisely the set of claims it invalidates. On the graph we place a risk-proportional evidence obligation, a bounded review protocol with separation of duties, and a merge gate that consults no model: coverage, freshness, signatures, exit codes, plausibility, evidence monotonicity (the mechanical form of "do not delete the failing test"), and review status. Assay is a dependency-free Python tool with an MCP server. On five public repositories a 600-token brief costs 14x to 114x less than an exploration proxy, warm rebuilds are up to 5x faster than cold ones, cone binding re-verifies 7.9% to 81.9% of claims where repository binding re-verifies all of them while per-module binding misses 23% to 68% of required invalidations, and the gate blocks 9 of 9 scripted adversarial behaviours while admitting the honest ones. Every number in this paper is generated by the released scripts.

</details>


### [49] [How Much Prompt Is Enough? A Blackbox Minimization of Few-Shots in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36289)
*Ali Alfageeh,Rahul Gopinath,Amin Alipour*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper presents a blackbox prompt-minimization framework that reduces few-shot prompts to their minimal necessary subset, achieving 65.3% character reduction while preserving output fidelity. The framework reveals that models preferentially retain logical identifiers and constraint declarations while discarding natural language prose.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The internal structure and causal hierarchy of prompts remain poorly understood - which parts are causally necessary and which are redundant is unclear. This opacity causes issues: subtle prompt variations can silently shift model outputs in critical software systems, and engineers lack techniques to reason about prompt reliability.

Method: The authors present a blackbox prompt-minimization framework that reduces few-shot prompts to their necessary minimal subset. They apply this framework to a few-shot learning system through a case study approach.

Result: Few-shot exemplars can be reduced by a mean of 65.3% (±15.8%) in character count while fully preserving propositional output fidelity. Models preferentially retain logical identifiers and constraint declarations while discarding natural language prose and cross-prompt relational annotations. Some models function as "universal encoders" producing highly legible minimized prompts, while others are "universal decoders" able to interpret minimized prompts from most other models.

Conclusion: By identifying which components are indispensable, this framework provides a principled basis for prompt compression and structural analysis of few-shot exemplars, enabling more reliable and efficient prompt engineering.

Abstract: Prompts are the primary mechanism for directing the behavior of large language models (LLMs). Yet the internal structure and causal hierarchy of prompts remain poorly understood: which parts are causally necessary and which are redundant is an open question. This opacity can have severe consequences. Subtle prompt variations can silently shift model outputs in critical software systems, and engineers lack techniques to reason about prompt reliability.
  We present \framework, a blackbox prompt-minimization framework that reduces few-shot prompts to their necessary minimal subset. We use a case study to apply \framework to a few-shot learning system and demonstrate the insights that this framework can provide.
  Our experiments show that few-shot exemplars can be reduced by a mean of 65.3\%~$\pm$~15.8\% in character count while fully preserving propositional output fidelity. The models preferentially retain logical identifiers and constraint declarations while discarding natural language prose and cross-prompt relational annotations.
  Our analysis also shows that some models are universal encoders, able to produce highly legible yet minimized prompts, while others are universal decoders, able to interpret minimized prompts from most other models.
  By identifying which components are indispensable, \framework provides a principled basis for prompt compression and structural analysis of few-shot exemplars.

</details>


### [50] [Strategies for Deploying AI Agents in Production at Scientific User Facilities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36362)
*Ming Du,Xiangyu Yin,Michael Prince,Yi Jiang,Rajat Sainju,Tekin Bicer,Yanqi Luo,Eric Codrea,Peco Myint,Nina Andrejevic,Juanjuan Huang,Trupti Mohanty,Pawan Tripathi,Dishant Beniwal,Hemant Sharma,Doga Gursoy,Aileen Luo,Tao Zhou,Chenran Xu,Jan Ilavsky,Matthew T. Dearing,Ryan Chard,Hoon Seo,Dariusz Jarosz,Elaine Chandler,Thomas Fors,Hairong Shang,Pete Jemian,Xuli Wu,Madeline Miller,Ryan Aydelott,Nicholas Schwarz,Francesco de Carlo,Byeongdu Lee,Yine Sun,Moises Smart,Philippe Piot,Joe Sullivan,Mathew J. Cherukara,Alec Sandy,Stefan Vogt,Jonathan Lang,Laurent C. Chapon*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This perspective paper discusses the transition of agentic AI from research demonstrations to production use at scientific user facilities (light sources, neutron sources, nanoscience centers, and autonomous laboratories). It presents practical strategies for deploying LLM-driven agents at the Advanced Photon Source (APS), focusing on reusable elements across instruments and facilities, including beamline control, knowledge retrieval, and data analysis frameworks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to address the gap between AI research demonstrations and practical production deployment at scientific facilities. The authors aim to provide reusable strategies and design principles that can be applied across different instruments and facilities, enabling scientists to focus on hypothesis-driven work while agents handle repeatable tasks like calibration, measurement execution, quality control, and initial data analysis.

Method: The paper draws on actual deployments of LLM-driven agents at the APS (Advanced Photon Source). It presents practical strategies with an emphasis on reusable elements, covering agent harnesses for beamline control, facility knowledge retrieval, and data analysis. The underlying design principles are kept implementation-independent, addressing inference endpoints, tool-server architectures, non-text data handling, computationally intensive services, reusable skills, and governed learning throughout instrument lifecycles.

Result: The paper provides a comprehensive set of recommendations and design principles for deploying agentic AI at scientific facilities. These include: agent harnesses for beamline control, facility knowledge retrieval, and data analysis; principles for inference endpoints, tool-server architectures, non-text data, computationally intensive services, reusable skills, and governed learning; as well as considerations for network/Linux operations, governed shared memory, and deterministic orchestration across facility services.

Conclusion: The recommendations represent a snapshot of LLM technology as of the publication date, acknowledging that the rapidly evolving nature of LLM capabilities means these principles will need continuous updating. The goal is to enable scientific facilities to leverage agentic AI for repeatable tasks while allowing scientists to focus on higher-level scientific interpretation and hypothesis generation.

Abstract: Agentic artificial intelligence (AI) is moving beyond research demonstrations toward production use at scientific user facilities, including light sources, neutron sources, nanoscience centers, and autonomous laboratories. Its scientific value extends beyond increasing throughput. Agents can perform repeatable tasks in calibration, measurement execution, and quality control, as well as initial analyses that turn data into reviewable evidence, allowing scientists to focus on hypotheses, unexpected observations, and interpretation. Drawing on deployments of LLM-driven agents at the APS, this perspective distills practical strategies with an emphasis on elements that can be reused across instruments and facilities. We discuss agent harnesses for beamline control, facility knowledge retrieval, and data analysis while keeping the underlying design principles independent of any specific implementation. These principles cover inference endpoints, tool-server architectures, non-text data, computationally intensive services, reusable skills, and governed learning throughout an instrument's lifecycle. We also consider how network and Linux operations, governed shared memory, and deterministic orchestration can extend these patterns across facility services. Because LLM capabilities continue to evolve, these recommendations represent a snapshot of the technology as of the date on the cover.

</details>


### [51] [LatentSift: Policy-State Filtering for Token-Efficient Verification of Software Engineering Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36371)
*Yuning Han,Yangchenchen Jin,Tyler Jandreau,Jingwei Sun*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: LatentSift is a token-free, execution-free filter that replaces LLM-based execution-free verifiers in software engineering agent workflows. It uses hidden states from policy generation to compare candidates with positive/negative state banks from training, reducing verification tokens by 49.1-62.1% while maintaining or improving performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Test-time scaling for software engineering agents generates multiple candidate trajectories requiring verification. Existing hybrid workflows use LLM-based execution-free (EF) verifiers to filter candidates before running tests, but this adds another model pass over every trajectory, consuming as many tokens as generation itself. The goal is to reduce token consumption while maintaining or improving agent performance.

Method: LatentSift represents each candidate trajectory through its reasoning, observation, and function-call states (hidden states already produced during generation). It compares these states with positive and negative banks collected from successful and unsuccessful trajectories during policy training. The resulting distance scores are fused with a learned linear score to retain promising candidates for execution-based stages.

Result: On SWE-bench Verified, across three agents and two policy sizes: (1) EF-verifier tokens reduced by 66.6-81.0%, (2) Total verification tokens (including test generation) reduced by 49.1-62.1% at K=16, (3) Hybrid Best@16 matches or improves on reference workflow, rising from 59.26% to 60.06% on DeepSWE-Preview.

Conclusion: LatentSift effectively replaces LLM-based execution-free verifiers with a token-free approach using hidden states. It significantly reduces verification overhead while maintaining or improving software engineering agent performance, making test-time scaling more computationally efficient.

Abstract: Test-time scaling improves software engineering agents by generating multiple candidate trajectories and selecting the best one. Verifying and selecting among these long interactions can consume as many tokens as generation itself. Existing hybrid workflows first apply an LLM-based execution-free (EF) verifier to filter candidates before running tests, which adds another model pass over every trajectory. We introduce LatentSift, a token-free and execution-free filter that replaces this first stage with hidden states the policy already produces while generating the candidates. It represents each candidate through its reasoning, observation, and function-call states, compares them with positive and negative banks of such states collected from successful and unsuccessful trajectories during policy training, and fuses the resulting distance scores with a learned linear score to retain promising candidates for the execution-based stages. On SWE-bench Verified, across three agents and two policy sizes, LatentSift cuts EF-verifier tokens by 66.6--81.0% and total verification tokens, which include test generation, by 49.1--62.1% at K=16, while hybrid Best@16 matches or improves on each agent's reference workflow, rising from 59.26% to 60.06% on DeepSWE-Preview.

</details>


### [52] [WitnessGym: Benchmarking Coding Agents on the Construction of Bug Witnesses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36635)
*Haomin Qi,Xiangzhe Xu,Yiming Huang,Jingbo Shang,Chengpeng Wang*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: WitnessGym is an automated framework that constructs bug-validation benchmarks by injecting bugs into real Java projects' test-reached paths, rebuilding projects, and retaining cases exposed by construction-time witnesses. It automatically generated 1,300 benchmark cases, and evaluation shows witness construction remains difficult even when bug patterns are known.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bug validation requires producing executable witnesses (concrete input + testing harness) to make audit findings actionable, but benchmark evaluation is difficult when cases reuse public historical bugs or require manual construction.

Method: 1) Inject bugs into test-reached paths of real projects with test suites
2) Rebuild each project and retain cases exposed by construction-time witness
3) Use bug specifications and execution adapters for extensibility to additional bug types and languages
4) Apply bug-preserving transformations to vary surrounding structure while preserving witness behavior
5) Evaluate four coding agent frameworks in six framework/model pairings across bug types, execution contexts, and transformation depths

Result: Automatically constructed 1,300 benchmark cases from real-world Java projects with test suites. Injected patches resemble historical bug patches and are difficult for evaluated models to distinguish in blinded comparisons. Witness construction remains difficult even when the bug pattern is known.

Conclusion: WitnessGym provides a scalable solution for creating bug-validation benchmarks through automated bug injection. The framework, benchmark cases, and evaluation scripts are publicly available, enabling reproducible research on bug validation and coding agents.

Abstract: Bug validation asks a coding agent to produce an executable witness for a reported bug. The witness combines a concrete input with a testing harness and exposes faulty behavior during execution. Such evidence makes audit findings actionable, yet benchmark evaluation is difficult when cases reuse public historical bugs and witnesses or require manual construction. We present WitnessGym, an automated framework for constructing bug-validation benchmarks through bug injection. It injects bugs into test-reached paths of real projects, rebuilds each project, and retains cases exposed by a construction-time witness. Bug specifications and execution adapters allow extension to additional bug types and languages. Bug-preserving transformations vary the surrounding structure while preserving the witness behavior. Based on real-world Java projects with test suites, WitnessGym automatically constructs 1,300 benchmark cases. The injected patches resemble historical bug patches and are difficult for the two evaluated models to distinguish in blinded comparisons. We evaluate four coding agent frameworks in six framework/model pairings across bug types, execution contexts, and transformation depths. Witness construction remains difficult even when the bug pattern is known. Our framework, benchmark cases, and evaluation scripts are available.

</details>


### [53] [CTE-Bench: Counterfactual Trace Evaluation for Stateful Software Simulators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36647)
*Xinran Zhang*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: CTE-Bench is a benchmark that evaluates whether AI models can predict how code interventions (patches or state changes) affect a stateful service's future behavior. It provides Python service code, pre-intervention call/response history, the intervention itself, and 40 future calls to predict. The benchmark reveals that current models achieve 54-61% accuracy with correct feedback but only 23-33% without it, and errors compound over multiple steps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks have gaps: function-level code-execution benchmarks omit persistent service state, while agent benchmarks score actions taken rather than the ability to predict future behavior. A wrong expectation by a coding agent may surface only several calls later, making prediction capability crucial for reliable software engineering agents.

Method: CTE-Bench presents models with Python service code, observed calls/responses before intervention, the intervention (source edit or state overwrite), and 40 fixed future calls. Models must predict every future response, which are then verified by executing the service. Three memory protocols test different feedback conditions: correct earlier responses, no earlier responses, or self-generated predictions.

Result: With correct earlier responses revealed, four API-hosted models (DeepSeek V4-Flash, Kimi K2.5, Qwen3.6-35B-A3B, Claude Sonnet 4.6) achieve 54.3%-61.5% effect-step VM. Hiding responses lowers VM to 23.2%-28.9%. Using self-generated predictions yields 24.8%-33.2%. At most 1.2% of scenarios are predicted exactly end-to-end. The dataset contains 255 scenarios over 6 deterministic Python services (10,200 predictions per model), with 2,476 future calls whose response the intervention actually changes.

Conclusion: Current models can track intervention effects mainly when correct feedback is supplied, but their errors compound significantly over a rollout. This highlights a fundamental limitation in AI models' ability to predict stateful service behavior without reliable feedback, which has important implications for developing reliable coding agents.

Abstract: Coding agents change running software: they patch a service's code or overwrite its stored state, and then act on their own expectation of how the service will respond afterwards. A wrong expectation may surface only several calls later. Function-level code-execution benchmarks omit persistent service state, and agent benchmarks score the actions an agent takes or the final state it reaches. We introduce CTE-Bench, which measures whether a model can predict how an intervention changes a stateful service's future behavior, without asking it to choose actions. Each scenario gives the model Python service code, the calls and responses observed before the intervention, the intervention itself (a source edit or a state overwrite), and 40 fixed future calls; the model predicts every future response, and predictions are checked by executing the service. Three memory protocols control whether the model sees the correct earlier responses, none of them, or its own earlier predictions. CTE-Bench-Core-v1 contains 255 scenarios over six deterministic Python services, giving 10,200 predictions per model. The main score is effect-step value match (VM): exact response equality on the 2,476 future calls whose response the intervention changes. With correct earlier responses revealed, four API-hosted models (DeepSeek V4-Flash, Kimi K2.5, Qwen3.6-35B-A3B, and Claude Sonnet 4.6) reach 54.3%-61.5% effect-step VM. Hiding those responses lowers effect-step VM to 23.2%-28.9%; conditioning on self-generated predictions gives 24.8%-33.2%, and at most 1.2% of scenarios are predicted exactly end to end. Current models thus track intervention effects mainly when correct feedback is supplied, and their errors compound over a rollout. We release CTE-Bench-Core-v1 with its executable oracle, evaluation scripts, and an evaluation card mapping each claim to its protocol.

</details>


### [54] [The Editor Has Read-Only Access: Correctness Signals in Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36783)
*Angad Miglani,Samrath Singh Chadha,Kevin Li,Manas Venkata Sai Ravulapalli*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether diffusion language models encode code correctness in their internal activations and whether this signal can be used to improve code generation. Using linear probes across six models, researchers found that models do encode correctness information (especially in later layers), but this signal cannot be reliably leveraged to improve generation through steering interventions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The researchers aimed to determine if diffusion language models internally "know" whether generated code is correct, and whether this knowledge can be extracted to improve code generation quality.

Method: Linear probes trained on model activations from six diffusion models, with controls using semantic mutations; comparisons with model confidence; steering experiments adding probe-derived directions to residual stream

Result: Linear probes can distinguish passing from failing code attempts, with strongest signals in later layers. However, probe-derived steering does not improve generation quality, while negative steering degrades performance.

Conclusion: Although diffusion models encode correctness information in their representations, this signal cannot be reliably exploited for generation improvement through current steering methods.

Abstract: Diffusion language models generate code by repeatedly updating a partially masked sequence. We ask whether their internal activations encode code correctness and whether that information can improve generation. Across six diffusion models, linear probes distinguish passing from failing attempts, with the strongest reads generally appearing beyond the early layers. Controls using small semantic mutations support a connection to correctness rather than surface style alone. In comparisons with model confidence, probe point estimates offer no consistent advantage. Adding a probe-derived direction to the residual stream does not yield a dependable improvement in the tested steering settings, while the opposite direction degrades performance. We distinguish these observations from claims about statistical significance or a general inability to steer. Supplementary methods, archived results, and code document the tested interventions and the limits of their statistical calibration and reproducibility.

</details>


### [55] [XRepoSkill: Learning Transferable Skills for Software Engineering Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36807)
*Yaoqi Guo,Haoyang Zhou,Jiayi Zhang,Yiran Zhang,Yang Liu,Qiuyuan Chen,Qiang Lin,Hande Dong,Jie M. Zhang,Zhenpeng Chen*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: XRepoSkill addresses the challenge of transferring software engineering skills across repositories by learning transferable rules from successful trajectories. It contrasts successful and failed trajectories on the same issue, derives candidate rules from divergent execution paths, verifies rules based on their association with successful outcomes, and retains only rules that recur across multiple repositories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Software engineering agents struggle to transfer learned skills across repositories because behaviors appearing in successful trajectories may not actually be responsible for success—they could be incidental or habitual. Current approaches fail to distinguish genuinely useful behaviors from coincidental ones.

Method: XRepoSkill represents skills as collections of rules (specifying what action to take and when). It derives candidate rules by contrasting successful vs failed trajectories on the same issue, identifying where execution paths diverge. Each rule is paired with an executable predicate for systematic evaluation. Rules are verified based on association with successful issue resolution and retained only when prescribed behaviors recur across multiple repositories. Repository-specific variants are consolidated into transferable rules.

Result: Against three recent skill learning methods, XRepoSkill achieves the highest issue resolution rate in all six benchmark-LLM combinations. On the challenging long-horizon DeepSWE benchmark, XRepoSkill improves issue resolution by 10.3 percentage points over the baseline agent without learned skills and by 5.0 points over the strongest skill-learning baseline.

Conclusion: XRepoSkill effectively learns transferable skills that generalize across repositories by filtering out repository-specific behaviors and retaining only rules that consistently lead to successful outcomes across multiple repositories. This approach significantly outperforms existing skill learning methods on SWE-bench Pro and DeepSWE benchmarks.

Abstract: Software engineering agents increasingly use reusable skills distilled from prior experience to resolve repository-level issues, yet such skills often fail to transfer across repositories. A central challenge is that a behavior appearing in a successful trajectory is not necessarily responsible for the successful outcome: it may be genuinely useful, merely incidental, or simply a recurring habit of the model. We introduce XRepoSkill, a trajectory-based approach for learning transferable skills. We represent a skill as a collection of rules, each specifying what action to take and when to take it during issue resolution. XRepoSkill first contrasts successful and failed trajectories of the same agent on the same issue and derives candidate rules from where their execution paths diverge. Each rule is paired with an executable predicate that enables its prescribed behavior to be evaluated systematically on other trajectories. A rule is verified based on its association with successful issue resolution and retained only when its prescribed behavior recurs across multiple repositories; repository-specific variants of the same behavior are then consolidated into transferable rules. For a new issue, XRepoSkill selects relevant rules to guide the agent. We learn skills from publicly released trajectories on the official SWE-bench Verified leaderboard and evaluate them on SWE-bench Pro and DeepSWE using three backbone LLMs from different vendors; none of the evaluation repositories appears in the skill-learning trajectory pool. Against three recent skill learning methods, XRepoSkill achieves the highest issue resolution rate in all six benchmark--LLM combinations. In particular, on the challenging long-horizon DeepSWE benchmark, XRepoSkill improves issue resolution by 10.3 percentage points over the same agent without learned skills and by 5.0 points over the strongest skill-learning baseline.

</details>


### [56] [Cross-Organizational SysML Model Integration: A Survey of Challenges and AI-Supported Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37000)
*Zirui Li,Torsten Brix,Stephan Husung*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper surveys 29 MBSE stakeholders to identify challenges in cross-organizational SysML model integration and evaluate AI-supported solutions. Results show model integration is a multi-dimensional problem requiring alignment across semantics, behavior, traceability, and interoperability. AI is seen as useful for analysis tasks but with strong preference for human-in-the-loop oversight.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cross-organizational collaboration is a key promise of SysML-based MBSE, but practitioners face persistent challenges in model exchange and integration. Additionally, LLMs offer potential AI-assisted solutions, but reliability and human oversight concerns remain. This study aims to understand stakeholder perceptions on integration challenges and AI support opportunities.

Method: Online questionnaire survey with 29 MBSE stakeholders involved in cross-organizational collaboration. Respondents rated 8 predefined integration challenge categories and 6 AI-supported task types on 5-point Likert scales.

Result: Model integration is perceived as a multi-dimensional alignment problem across semantics, behavior, traceability, and exchange interoperability. Perceptions vary by organizational role and frequency of integration involvement. AI is rated highly useful for analysis tasks (semantic structure analysis, inconsistency detection), with respondents preferring human-in-the-loop use with mandatory verification.

Conclusion: AI support should enhance rather than replace engineering responsibility in SysML-based integration. The findings motivate AI tools that assist analysis tasks while maintaining human oversight for verification.

Abstract: Cross-organizational collaboration is widely regarded as a key promise of SysML-based Model-Based Systems Engineering (MBSE), yet practitioners still face persistent challenges when exchanging and integrating system models. In parallel, Large Language Models (LLMs) raise expectations for AI-assisted model understanding and integration, while reliability and required human oversight continue to pose challenges. This paper reports the results of an online questionnaire survey with 29 MBSE stakeholders involved in cross-organizational collaboration. Respondents rated eight predefined integration challenge categories and six AI-supported task types on five-point Likert scales. The results indicate that stakeholders perceive model integration as a multi-dimensional alignment problem across semantics, behavior, traceability, and exchange interoperability. These perceptions vary by organizational role and frequency of integration involvement. AI is rated highly useful for analysis tasks such as semantic structure analysis and inconsistency detection, and respondents predominantly prefer human-in-the-loop use with mandatory verification. These findings motivate AI support that enhances, rather than replaces, engineering responsibility in SysML-based integration.

</details>


### [57] [GitCF: Reducing Incomplete Changes by Exploiting Multiple Similarities Among Commits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37045)
*Yuto Kitabayashi,Takashi Kobayashi*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: GitCF is a change recommendation method that helps developers identify additional locations that should be modified by comparing their current change with past changes in revision history, combining element similarity and textual content (commit messages, code diffs, issue descriptions). It outperforms baseline techniques on 543 incomplete changes from 17 open-source projects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Software developers often struggle to identify all locations affected by their changes, leading to incomplete modifications. Existing co-change rule mining techniques cannot recommend rarely co-changed elements and ignore commit text. Text-based approaches don't use developer current changes as input.

Method: GitCF compares a developer's current change with past changes in revision history using two similarity sources: (1) modified elements and (2) textual content including commit messages, code diffs, and linked issue descriptions. Evaluated on 543 incomplete changes from 17 open-source projects against three co-change-rule-based baselines.

Result: GitCF outperforms the strongest baseline on all five metrics (MAP, Recall@10, Recall@20, Hit@10, Hit@20). Hit@10 improved from 0.322 to 0.403, and the improvement in average precision is statistically significant.

Conclusion: Combining element similarity with textual content from commit history effectively recommends additional change locations and improves upon traditional co-change rule mining approaches.

Abstract: Software developers often struggle to identify all locations that their changes affect, and incomplete changes frequently result from these omissions. To mitigate this problem, several approaches have been proposed to recommend additional locations that should be modified together with a developer's current change. A large class of these techniques mines co-change rules from repository revision histories, but because they rely solely on which elements have been modified together, they cannot recommend elements that have rarely co-changed, and they disregard the textual information that accompanies commits. A second class of techniques does exploit textual information, but it derives it from the source code or from a change request rather than from commit history, and it does not use the developer's current change as its input. To address these limitations, we propose GitCF, a change recommendation method that compares a developer's current change with past changes in the revision history and recommends additional change locations by combining two sources of similarity: the set of modified elements and the textual content of commits, including commit messages, code diffs, and linked issue descriptions. We evaluate GitCF on 543 incomplete changes from 17 open-source projects, where an incomplete change denotes a commit in which an omitted modification is supplied in a later commit, comparing it against three co-change-rule-based techniques. GitCF outperforms the strongest baseline on all five metrics (MAP, Recall@10, Recall@20, Hit@10, and Hit@20); in particular, it raises Hit@10 from 0.322 to 0.403, and the improvement in average precision is statistically significant.

</details>


### [58] [LoLBench: Evaluating Coding Agents with Long-Horizon Proposals on Large Software Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37143)
*Yun Peng,Zihan Wu,Zeyang Zhuang,Xin Zhou,Rui Shu,Xu Han,Chun Yong Chong,Yuan Wang,Jiakun Liu*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: LoLBench is a multilingual benchmark (100 tasks, 29 software systems, 5 domains) evaluating coding agents' both perception and implementation capabilities in the entire proposal-to-implementation process on large software systems. The best agent resolves only 14% of tasks, with incomplete code localization as the major bottleneck.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks evaluate coding agents' implementation capability from detailed specifications, but practical modular development also requires perception capability to ground user intent and high-level design to derive specifications. There was no benchmark evaluating both capabilities through the full proposal-to-implementation process on large software systems.

Method: LoLBench provides human-written enhancement proposals with user intent and high-level design (~5,000 words each), paired with large software systems (~2.4M LoC). Evaluated 28 agents on their ability to complete the entire process from understanding proposals to implementing PRs (~5,500 LoC changes).

Result: Best agent resolves only 14% of tasks with 52.7% F2P pass rate. Incomplete code localization is identified as a major bottleneck. Providing reference-derived file trees alongside API specifications improves resolved rates by 16-22 percentage points (2.4-17x), reaching at most 34%.

Conclusion: Both perception and implementation remain central challenges for coding agents in practical modular development on large software systems. The benchmark and findings are available at https://huggingface.co/datasets/lolbench26/LoLBench.

Abstract: Modern coding agents can deliver increasingly large repository-level changes, and recent benchmarks reflect this by emphasizing long-horizon tasks with large reference implementations. Many benchmarks evaluate coding agents' implementation capability to produce correct code edits from detailed specifications. However, practical modular development tasks also require the perception capability of grounding user intent and high-level design to derive a specification. We introduce LoLBench to evaluate both capabilities through the entire proposal-to-implementation process on large software systems. It is a multilingual benchmark of 100 tasks across 29 software systems in five domains. Each task provides a human-written enhancement proposal with user intent and high-level design. On average, proposals contain about 5,000 words, software systems contain 2.4 million source lines of code (LoC), and implementation pull requests (PRs) change approximately 5,500 LoC. Across 28 agents we evaluated, the best agent resolves only 14% of tasks and achieves a 52.7% Fail-to-Pass (F2P) pass rate. Failure analysis identifies incomplete code localization as a major bottleneck, while providing reference-derived file trees alongside API specifications improves resolved rates by 16--22 percentage points (2.4--17$\times$), reaching at most 34%. These results show that both perception and implementation remain central challenges for coding agents in practical modular development on large software systems. LoLBench is available at https://huggingface.co/datasets/lolbench26/LoLBench.

</details>


### [59] [CRJudgeBench: Can AI Detect Plausible but Invalid Code Reviews?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37216)
*Yue Pan,Jiawei Li,Ziyuan Zhang,Xiangxin Zhao,He Ye*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper introduces CRJudgeBench, a benchmark for evaluating whether code review comments are technically trustworthy, and Sentinel, an agentic judge that uses repository-grounded evidence to verify review comments. The benchmark contains 1199 instances from real pull requests, and Sentinel achieves 76.60% accuracy on the test set, outperforming state-of-the-art LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models can generate plausible code-review comments that contain technically incorrect claims, which can mislead developers. Existing benchmarks focus on review generation or issue discovery, but don't assess whether an agent can determine the technical trustworthiness of individual review comments.

Method: The authors created CRJudgeBench with 1199 instances from real pull requests with expert-verified perturbations covering both trustworthy and plausible but untrustworthy comments. They then developed Sentinel, a repository-grounded agentic judge that actively gathers code evidence to verify review comments before making judgments. Sentinel was trained from Qwen3-Coder-30B-A3B-Instruct using iterative action-level learning from a privileged teacher on the training split.

Result: On the 359-instance CRJudgeBench test set, Sentinel achieves 76.60% accuracy, outperforming GLM-5.3 by 6.13 percentage points and its base model by 19.78 points. The results demonstrate that even state-of-the-art general-purpose LLMs struggle to identify untrustworthy comments, while iterative action-level learning substantially improves the accuracy of repository-grounded trustworthiness judgments.

Conclusion: The paper shows that LLMs have significant difficulty identifying technically untrustworthy code review comments, but iterative action-level learning can substantially improve performance. The CRJudgeBench benchmark and Sentinel agent provide a foundation for further research on technical trustworthiness judgment in code review contexts.

Abstract: Large language models can generate plausible code-review comments, but such comments may contain technically incorrect claims that mislead developers. We study technical trustworthiness judgment: determining whether a review comment's core technical claims are correct and applicable to the reviewed code in its repository context. Existing code-review benchmarks primarily evaluate review generation, issue discovery, or general comment quality, but do not directly assess whether an agent can determine the technical trustworthy of an individual review comment. To fill this gap, we introduce CRJudgeBench, a benchmark of 1199 instances constructed from real pull requests and expert-verified perturbations, covering both trustworthy and plausible but untrustworthy comments. We further present Sentinel, a repository-grounded agentic judge that actively gathers code evidence to verify review comments before making judgments. Starting from Qwen3-Coder-30B-A3B-Instruct, Sentinel is trained on the CRJudgeBench training split through iterative action-level learning from a privileged teacher. On the 359-instance CRJudgeBench test set, Sentinel achieves 76.60\% accuracy, outperforming GLM-5.3 by 6.13 percentage points and its base model by 19.78 points. These results show that even state-of-the-art general-purpose LLMs struggle to identify untrustworthy comments, while iterative action-level learning substantially improves the accuracy of repository-grounded trustworthiness judgments. Our dataset is available at https://huggingface.co/datasets/dcloud347/CRJudgeBenchmark

</details>


### [60] [SafeLLM4SE: Statistical Evaluation and Reporting for LLM-based Software Engineering Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37294)
*Francisco Ortin*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: SafeLLM4SE is a methodology and reporting standard for statistically principled evaluation of LLM-based software engineering systems, addressing the stochastic nature of LLMs that challenges validity and reproducibility of evaluations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM evaluation practices (single output, average score, best-of-N, pass@k) obscure variability and estimation uncertainty, leading to potentially misleading conclusions about system reliability.

Method: SafeLLM4SE treats LLM outputs as realizations of a stochastic process, distinguishing quality, stability, and estimation uncertainty. It combines adaptive sampling with confidence intervals, distribution-aware statistical comparisons, effect sizes, and a minimum reporting standard covering model configuration, reproducibility, evaluation procedures, and resource usage.

Result: SafeLLM4SE provides an open-source software package on PyPI enabling researchers to reproduce and extend the methodology. Demonstrated by comparing two LLMs on HumanEval benchmark.

Conclusion: SafeLLM4SE offers a practical solution for statistically sound evaluation of LLM-based software engineering systems, promoting validity, reproducibility, and comparability in research and practice.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for software engineering tasks, yet their stochastic behavior challenges the validity, reproducibility, and comparability of their evaluations. Conventional practices such as reporting a single output, an average score, best-of-N, or pass@k performance can obscure variability and estimation uncertainty, potentially leading to misleading conclusions about system reliability. This article presents SafeLLM4SE, a practical methodology and reporting standard for statistically principled evaluation of LLM-based software engineering systems. Rather than treating generated outputs as deterministic artifacts, SafeLLM4SE treats them as realizations of a stochastic process and distinguishes quality, stability, and estimation uncertainty. It combines adaptive sampling with confidence intervals, distribution-aware statistical comparisons, effect sizes, and a minimum reporting standard covering model configuration, reproducibility, evaluation procedures, and resource usage. SafeLLM4SE is also provided as an open-source software package available on PyPI, enabling researchers and practitioners to reproduce and extend the methodology. We illustrate its application by comparing two LLMs on HumanEval, a benchmark of programming problems assessed through functional tests.

</details>


### [61] [Do Agent Benchmarks Do What They Say? An Executable-Contract Audit of Tool-Using Agent Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37315)
*Rohith Reddy Bellibatlu,Zichong Wang,Wenbin Zhang*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper introduces a method to audit tool defects in agent benchmarks by treating tool interfaces as executable contracts and tracing score provenance to identify when evaluators reward defective tool behavior. Across 34 audited mutating tools in four benchmarks, the authors found seven tool defects and one evaluator property, with at least 5 of 12 additional tools in AgentDojo diverging from their advertised surfaces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks for tool-using agents grade outputs based on simulated tool calls assuming tools work as advertised, but no audit categories exist for verifying this assumption. Defects beneath scores persist across reruns, meaning benchmarks may be rewarding flawed tool behavior.

Method: The authors treat tool advertised surfaces as executable contracts, check implementations against these contracts, and trace score provenance through task files and evaluator code to verdicts derived from state a defective tool should have written. They use a checker with static and dynamic components across 34 audited mutating tools in four benchmarks, including injected defects and negative controls.

Result: The study confirmed seven tool defects and one evaluator property at pinned commits. On injected defects, the checker raised no false positives in 25 flags, flagged 2 of 5 negative controls, and missed most defects (29 of 33 scored misses had clauses covering the defect but no probe revealed it). The static half alone flags 14 of 17 confirmed sites. Twelve further AgentDojo tools (with six held-out and seven audited) showed at least 5 tools diverging from advertised surfaces. No gold trajectory reached either tau2-bench defect; on 1,120 paths for the telecom defect, the evaluator rewards a refuel of a suspended line and fails the repaired tool. A clinical benchmark's tool reports writes under a no-write design its interface doesn't disclose, and the grader takes this message as evidence of success rather than checking actual record changes.

Conclusion: Tool defects are prevalent in agent benchmarks, and evaluator properties can reward defective tool behavior. The checker confirms and traces rather than discovers defects. The method reveals significant gaps between tool implementations and advertised interfaces, particularly in AgentDojo, tau2-bench, and clinical benchmarks, indicating that current benchmarking practices may not accurately assess agent capabilities.

Abstract: Tool-using agents are entering settings where a wrong action carries real cost, and the benchmarks certifying them grade what each simulated tool call reports having done, assuming the tool did what its interface advertises. The audit taxonomies we survey publish no category for that assumption, and a defect beneath a score is present on every rerun. We treat a tool's advertised surfaces as an executable contract, check the implementation against it, and trace each score's provenance through the task files and evaluator code to the verdicts that derive from state a defective tool should have written. Across 34 audited mutating tools in four benchmarks we confirm seven tool defects and one evaluator property at pinned commits. On injected defects the checker raised no false positive in 25 flags, flagged 2 of 5 negative controls, and missed most: in 29 of 33 scored misses a clause covered the defect but no probe revealed it. The checker's own static half, run alone, flags 14 of 17 confirmed sites, so on these findings the dynamic half confirms and traces rather than discovers. Twelve further AgentDojo tools, with six held-out tools and the seven audited first, complete its 25-tool mutating surface, on which at least 5 tools diverge from their advertised surface as our contracts read it, a rate for AgentDojo alone. No gold trajectory reaches either tau2-bench defect; on 1,120 paths built to isolate the telecom defect, a number fixed by construction, the evaluator rewards a refuel of a suspended line and fails the repaired tool. The clearest case is a clinical benchmark whose tool tells the agent each write executed under a documented no-write design its interface does not disclose; its grader takes that message as evidence, so its action success rate records whether a request carried the expected payload, not whether any record changed.

</details>


### [62] [Complexity-Aware Evaluation of LLM Comprehension](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37405)
*Ali Mohammadi Esfahani,Nafiseh Kahani,Samuel A. Ajila*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper introduces a complexity-aware framework to evaluate LLM code comprehension reliability, revealing that model accuracy substantially degrades as source code becomes structurally more complex—a finding that aggregate benchmarks conceal.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Aggregate benchmark accuracy can mask how LLM reliability changes with code complexity. The authors argue that a more diagnostic evaluation approach is needed to understand LLM limitations in real-world software engineering tasks.

Method: The study uses four complexity metrics (cyclomatic complexity, nesting depth, branching factor, and Halstead volume) to evaluate two LLMs (DeepSeek-Coder-V2 and Llama) through: (1) automatic input-output prediction over 300 Python functions, and (2) manually assessed semantic comprehension over 60 functions. Functions are categorized into Low-, Medium-, and High-complexity bands.

Result: DeepSeek-Coder-V2 achieves 78.33% overall accuracy vs. 70.33% for Llama. However, accuracy drops significantly from Low to High complexity: 93.52%→52.78% for DeepSeek and 87.04%→47.22% for Llama. Incorrect predictions correlate with higher complexity values. Manual semantic comprehension shows similar degradation: 100%→75% for DeepSeek and 90%→60% for Llama.

Conclusion: Complexity-aware evaluation provides a more diagnostic assessment of LLM code-comprehension reliability than aggregate accuracy alone. Model performance degrades substantially on structurally complex code, highlighting the need for complexity-stratified benchmarking.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for software engineering tasks that require understanding existing source code, including behavior prediction, function explanation, debugging, and code review. However, aggregate benchmark accuracy can conceal how model reliability changes as source code becomes structurally more complex. This paper presents a complexity-aware framework for evaluating LLM code comprehension using cyclomatic complexity, nesting depth, branching factor, and Halstead volume. We evaluate DeepSeek-Coder-V2 and Llama through two complementary tasks: automatic input-output prediction over 300 Python functions and manually assessed semantic comprehension over a balanced subset of 60 functions. The functions are grouped into Low-, Medium-, and High-complexity bands. DeepSeek-Coder-V2 achieves an overall automatic accuracy of 78.33%, compared with 70.33% for Llama. However, accuracy decreases substantially from Low to High complexity, from 93.52% to 52.78% for DeepSeek-Coder-V2 and from 87.04% to 47.22% for Llama. Incorrect predictions are consistently associated with higher values of all four complexity metrics, and correlation and logistic-regression analyses confirm broadly comparable negative associations between structural complexity and correctness. Manual semantic comprehension shows the same degradation pattern, with accuracy decreasing from 100.00% to 75.00% for DeepSeek-Coder-V2 and from 90.00% to 60.00% for Llama. These findings demonstrate that complexity-aware evaluation provides a more diagnostic assessment of LLM code-comprehension reliability than aggregate accuracy alone.

</details>


### [63] [Exploring Emotional Intelligence in Software Testing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37526)
*Cristina Martinez Montes,Pushpamalar Rajendran,Grace Karuri,Felix Dobslaw*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This study explores how software testers describe the role of Emotional Intelligence (EI) in their daily work, focusing on communication, conflict management, and responding to requirements volatility. Through semi-structured interviews with 16 Swedish agile testers across diverse industries, researchers identified three key themes: stress regulation under deadline pressure, managing delivery of critical feedback to preserve trust, and responding to requirements changes with frustration leading to various coping strategies. The findings highlight that managing emotions is a constant job requirement for testers, and EI becomes even more important when development processes lack independent testing phases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The research is motivated by a gap in literature: prior studies on emotion in software engineering have focused primarily on developers, while software testers face unique emotional challenges. Testers deliver judgments about colleagues' work under deadlines they don't control, yet this emotional dimension of testing work remains under-explored.

Method: The study employed semi-structured interviews with 16 software testers in Sweden working in agile teams across multiple sectors (aviation, automotive, healthcare, IT services, administration, banking, and pharmaceuticals). Data was analyzed using reflexive thematic analysis informed by Goleman's EI framework, which categorizes EI into self-awareness, self-regulation, motivation, empathy, and social skills.

Result: Three themes emerged: (1) Testers regulate stress under deadline pressure and draw motivation from recognition, clarity, and autonomy; (2) Testers manage the daily delivery of critical findings to colleagues while preserving trust; (3) Testers respond to requirements change with frustration that leads to decisions about what to leave untested, advocacy for process change, or workarounds. The research also identified unique features of the testing role: the work product is a criticism of a colleague's work, success is invisible while failure is attributed, and the tester's window shrinks with upstream delays.

Conclusion: For software testers, managing emotions is a constant job requirement rather than an occasional skill. The importance of EI increases when the development process lacks an independent testing phase. The findings have implications for teams and organizations, suggesting need for better support systems for testers' emotional labor and highlighting directions for future research on emotion management in software testing contexts.

Abstract: Background: Emotional Intelligence (EI) is the ability to recognise, understand, and manage one's own and others' emotions. Software testers deliver judgements about colleagues' work under deadlines they do not control, and prior work on emotion in software engineering has mostly studied developers.
  Aims: To explore how software testers describe the part EI plays in their day-to-day work, in communication and conflict within the team, and in responding to requirements volatility.
  Method: Semi-structured interviews with 16 software testers in Sweden working in teams that use agile practices, across aviation, automotive, healthcare, IT services, administration, banking and pharmaceuticals, analysed with reflexive thematic analysis informed by Goleman's EI framework.
  Results: Three themes. Testers described regulating stress under deadline pressure and drawing motivation from recognition, clarity and autonomy; managing the daily delivery of critical findings to colleagues so that trust survives; and responding to requirements change with frustration that turned into decisions about what to leave untested, into advocacy for process change, or into workarounds. Read against developer-focused studies, the themes point to features of the testing role: the work product is a criticism of a colleague's work, success is invisible while failure is attributed, and the tester's window shrinks with every upstream delay.
  Conclusions: For testers, managing emotions is a constant job requirement. The results highlight that the importance of EI increases when the development process lacks an independent testing phase. The findings also inform implications for teams and, ultimately, for organisations and future research.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [64] [Sage: Formalization with Semantic Correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35790)
*Thomas Hirtz,Farzad Jafarrahmani,Abdelmouksit Sagueni,Xiang Zhou,Wengping Deng,Liang Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Sage is an agentic framework for translating informal natural language mathematical problems into formal Lean 4 code. It uses a four-stage pipeline with a dual-signal semantic correction loop that pairs compiler diagnostics with multi-dimensional semantic feedback to address the "illusion of rigor" problem in formal mathematics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neural theorem provers assume formal Lean 4 statements are provided, but translating natural language to formal languages faces a critical bottleneck: the "illusion of rigor" where type-checkers accept semantically flawed statements (dropped hypotheses, vacuous truths, altered bounds). Additionally, models achieve high formalization rates by "guessing" unverified answers, exhibiting a 70.9% answer leakage rate.

Method: A four-stage decomposed generation pipeline with a dual-signal semantic correction loop. The correction loop combines Lean 4 compiler diagnostics with multi-dimensional semantic feedback to enforce mathematical fidelity alongside syntactic validity. The framework explicitly accounts for the gap between open-ended queries and declarative formal targets.

Result: Sage suppresses answer leakage from 70.9% to 2.7%. It achieves 73.3% pass@4 joint compilation and semantic fidelity on Omni-MATH without proofs, compared to 42.0% for a fine-tuned Goedel-Formalizer-V2 baseline. On IMO-Unformalized (175 unformalized IMO problems), Sage achieves 87.4% pass@4 verified fidelity versus 19.4% for the baseline, winning 79% of blind pairwise evaluations.

Conclusion: The decomposed pipeline with dual-signal semantic correction effectively addresses the illusion of rigor and answer leakage problems in formal mathematics, enabling effective zero-shot generalization to novel mathematical problems while maintaining mathematical fidelity.

Abstract: While neural theorem provers have achieved impressive milestones in formal mathematics, they largely operate on the assumption that faithful Lean 4 formal statements are already provided. Translating informal natural language into a formal language is a critical data bottleneck plagued by an "illusion of rigor": standard type-checkers accept statements that compile but drop hypotheses, introduce vacuous truths, or subtly alter mathematical bounds. To resolve this, we introduce Sage (Semantic Agent-Guided Formalization Engine), an agentic framework that replaces monolithic translation with a four-stage decomposed generation pipeline coupled with a dual-signal semantic correction loop. By pairing Lean 4 compiler diagnostics with multi-dimensional semantic feedback, our correction loop enforces mathematical fidelity alongside syntactic validity. By explicitly accounting for the gap between open-ended queries and declarative formal targets, our pipeline prevents models from achieving high formalization rates by guessing unverified answers (exhibiting a 70.9% answer leakage rate). Consequently, Sage suppresses leakage to 2.7% while achieving 73.3% pass@4 joint compilation and semantic fidelity on the Omni-MATH without proofs (compared to 42.0% for a fine-tuned Goedel-Formalizer-V2 baseline). Finally, on IMO-Unformalized, a novel frontier of 175 unformalized International Mathematical Olympiad problems, Sage demonstrates effective zero-shot generalization with 87.4% pass@4 verified fidelity compared to just 19.4% for the baseline, winning over 79% of blind pairwise evaluations.

</details>


### [65] [Serverless gossip training of LSTM failure detectors: A matched-protocol comparison with federated, local and centralized learning on NASA C-MAPSS](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35792)
*Yusuf Öztürk,Enes Göktekin,Bengisu Atlı,Akın Öztürk,Zhixiang Wang,Ulas Bagci*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper compares serverless gossip learning with Federated Averaging (FedAvg) for predictive maintenance using stacked LSTM on NASA C-MAPSS turbofan benchmark. Gossip achieved 89.6% F1 versus FedAvg's 89.9%, while transmitting the same payload without a coordinator.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Industrial predictive maintenance requires learning from equipment across sites without data pooling. While FedAvg uses a central server and gossip learning removes it, the behavior of gossip for recurrent failure-detection models had not been measured under controlled conditions.

Method: The study compares synchronous ring gossip with FedAvg, isolated local training, and centralized reference using a stacked LSTM for failure detection on NASA C-MAPSS. All methods share identical implementation, architecture, initialization, optimizer, data split, and training budget. Evaluation uses terminal-window F1 per test engine to avoid statistical dependence.

Result: On FD001 (five seeds): gossip achieved 89.6% ± 1.3% F1 vs FedAvg's 89.9% ± 1.1%, local training 83.6% ± 6.7%, and centralized 93.5% ± 2.1%. Node models showed 1.8% pairwise decision disagreement (vs 5.6% without communication). Across FD002-FD004, peer communication improved F1 by 13-28 points over local training; gossip matched FedAvg on FD003/FD004 but was 4.3 points lower on FD002. Simulated message loss, node failure, and server outage showed minimal impact, while larger rings degraded gossip faster.

Conclusion: Ring gossip is a practical serverless alternative to FedAvg when data heterogeneity is moderate, transmitting the same payload without a coordinator. Faster-mixing topologies become important as heterogeneity grows.

Abstract: Industrial predictive maintenance increasingly depends on learning from equipment spread across sites whose sensor data cannot easily be pooled. Federated averaging (FedAvg) solves this with a central aggregation server; gossip learning removes the server, but its behaviour for recurrent failure-detection models has not been measured under controlled conditions. We compare synchronous ring gossip with FedAvg, isolated local training and a centralized reference for a stacked LSTM that detects imminent failure on the NASA C-MAPSS turbofan benchmark. All methods share one open implementation, architecture, initialization, optimizer, data split and training budget, and the primary endpoint uses one terminal window per test engine to avoid the statistical dependence of overlapping windows. On FD001 (five seeds), gossip reached a terminal-window F1 of 89.6 +/- 1.3%, compared with 89.9 +/- 1.1% for FedAvg, 83.6 +/- 6.7% for local training and 93.5 +/- 2.1% for centralized training, while transmitting the same payload as FedAvg without a coordinator. Node models agreed closely but not exactly (1.8% pairwise decision disagreement versus 5.6% without communication). Across FD002-FD004, peer communication improved terminal-window F1 over local training by 13-28 points; gossip matched FedAvg on FD003 and FD004 but was 4.3 points lower on the multi-condition FD002 subset. Simulated message loss, node failure and server outage changed neither method appreciably, whereas larger rings degraded gossip faster.
