Forcing probabilistic models to approximate these deterministic problems wastes accuracy and energy. The authors propose routing queries to the appropriate solver instead of always using the SLM.

Method: The router learns a deterministic finite automaton (DFA) using the L* grammatical inference algorithm. The SLM serves as a membership oracle while labeled data functions as an equivalence oracle. This learned DFA classifies incoming queries: structured tasks go to deterministic engines, while open-ended word problems go to the SLM.

Result: On a Raspberry Pi 4B evaluated with 100 untested prompts from DeepMind Mathematics, GSM8K, and RuleTaker, the learned routing achieved 100% routing accuracy and 98.3% overall accuracy with a 512-token reasoning budget (93.3% on word problems). This significantly outperforms Program-of-Thought (72.0%) and tool-calling agent (58.7%). Formatted queries are answered in 1-11ms, and in its 30-token configuration, runs 8.8x faster and 2.8x more energy-efficient than Program-of-Thought.

Conclusion: The neurosymbolic router successfully combines symbolic and neural approaches by learning the routing logic automatically. It achieves near-perfect accuracy while dramatically improving efficiency on edge devices, demonstrating that many reasoning tasks can be handled more reliably by deterministic solvers when appropriate, rather than always relying on probabilistic language models.

Abstract: Running a language model on edge hardware provides private and low-latency reasoning without a network connection, and yet the small models that fit on such devices are unreliable on the tasks computers are expected to handle well, such as arithmetic, algebra, and formal logic problems. We argue that much of this unreliability is avoidable. Many queries appearing to demand reasoning are in fact structurally deterministic and permit fast and exact symbolic solutions. Therefore, forcing a probabilistic model to approximate them sacrifices accuracy and energy for little benefit. We present a neurosymbolic router that classifies each incoming query and dispatches it to the cheapest correct solver, sending structured tasks to deterministic engines and reserving the small language model (SLM) for open-ended word problems. Instead of hand-coding the routing logic, we learn a deterministic finite automaton (DFA) with the L* grammatical inference algorithm, using the SLM as a membership oracle and labeled data as an equivalence oracle. On a Raspberry Pi 4B (8 GB RAM, no GPU), evaluated on 100 untested prompts from DeepMind Mathematics, GSM8K, and RuleTaker, learned routing attains 100% routing accuracy and 98.3% overall accuracy with a 512-token reasoning budget (93.3% on word problems), compared with 72.0% for the strongest agent baseline, Program-of-Thought, and 58.7% for a tool-calling agent given the same solvers. Since formatted queries never reach the model, the router answers them in 1-11 ms and, in its 30-token configuration, runs 8.8x faster and 2.8x more energy-efficient than Program-of-Thought.

</details>


### [90] [Is Human-Readable Text Necessary for Effective LLM Fine-Tuning?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35868)
*Jinhao Zhang,Zeyu Liu,Zicheng Yan,Yunquan Zhang,Daning Cheng,Song Tang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper investigates whether human-readable text is necessary for fine-tuning LLMs, proposing DASA (Desired-Update-Aligned Synthetic Data) which uses activation-gradient feedback from a frozen reference model to optimize continuous synthetic input embeddings without requiring human-readable textual forms. Experiments on 6 models (1B-32B parameters) across 6 benchmarks show comparable or better performance than natural language data, outperforming GRADMM in most comparisons with 3.6-4.9x speedup.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors question whether human-readable text is truly necessary for effective fine-tuning of large language models. They explore whether model-conditioned training representations can preserve or improve adaptation utility without requiring a human-readable textual form.

Method: DASA uses activation-gradient feedback from a frozen reference model to guide the optimization of continuous synthetic input embeddings. The approach targets useful adaptation updates rather than source-text reconstruction or linguistic fluency. The resulting embeddings are used directly for downstream fine-tuning; discrete token projections are employed only for qualitative inspection.

Result: Under matched LoRA adaptation settings, DASA achieves performance comparable to source natural-language data and surpasses it in multiple configurations, while outperforming GRADMM in most comparisons. DASA provides 3.6-4.9x speedup over GRADMM with comparable peak GPU memory.

Conclusion: Human-readable text is not necessary for effective fine-tuning of LLMs. Model-conditioned training representations (continuous synthetic embeddings) can preserve or even improve adaptation utility without requiring human-readable textual forms.

Abstract: Is human readability necessary for effective fine-tuning of large language models? We investigate whether model-conditioned training representations can preserve or improve adaptation utility without requiring a human-readable textual form. We propose Desired-Update-Aligned Synthetic Data (DASA), which uses activation-gradient feedback from a frozen reference model to guide the optimization of continuous synthetic input embeddings. Inspired by the role of activation gradients in local risk reduction, DASA targets useful adaptation updates rather than source-text reconstruction or linguistic fluency. The resulting embeddings are used directly for downstream fine-tuning; discrete token projections are employed only for qualitative inspection. Experiments on six models from the Llama and Qwen families, ranging from 1B to 32B parameters, cover six benchmarks spanning knowledge, mathematical reasoning, code generation, and commonsense reasoning. Under matched LoRA adaptation settings, DASA achieves performance comparable to the source natural-language data and surpasses it in multiple configurations, while outperforming GRADMM in most comparisons. Further experiments cover general-domain and task-specialized source data. Under the evaluated synthesis settings, DASA provides a $3.6$--$4.9\times$ speedup over GRADMM with comparable peak GPU memory.

</details>


### [91] [The Price of Token Boundaries: Compression Certificates and Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35869)
*Yuhao Du,Shunian Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper quantifies the compression cost of tokenization boundaries (imposed by pre-tokenization) by comparing token counts with and without regular-expression boundary rules. The authors develop methods using nonnegative prices, shortest paths, and linear programming relaxation to bound the minimum token count. They find that boundaries increase optimal token count by 28.3-36.8% on English Wikipedia, and that compression and prediction favor different dictionaries. The paper also introduces "boundary licences" to study intermediate boundary policies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pre-tokenization restricts which text fragments can become tokens, but its compression cost is hidden when tokenizers are compared only under the same boundaries. The authors aim to quantify this cost and separate it from the prediction quality of resulting token units.

Method: The authors use: (1) nonnegative prices on token occurrences to yield a lower bound through shortest paths and vocabulary-budget selection; (2) maximizing over all prices to recover the linear programming relaxation; (3) an independent integer checker to certify reported values. They also introduce "boundary licences" to limit vocabulary entries permitted to cross cuts.

Result: On English Wikipedia, boundaries increase optimal token count by 28.3-36.8%. Byte pair encoding (BPE) is 2.1% above the constrained lower bound but 10.9% above the unrestricted bound. Unrestricted fitting yields higher mean held-out bits per byte in 12/12 languages under a common unrestricted decoder and 11/12 under independent tuning. With 10% vocabulary budget licensed, 85.2% (English) and 100.0% (Chinese) of token-count reduction is recovered.

Conclusion: The compression cost of tokenization boundaries is significant and quantifiable (28-37% increase in token count). Compression and prediction favor different dictionaries, and boundary licences provide a useful framework for studying intermediate boundary policies. The results demonstrate the importance of separating boundary constraints from token unit quality in tokenizer evaluation.

Abstract: Pre-tokenisation restricts which text fragments can become prediction units, but its compression cost is obscured when tokenisers are compared only under the same boundaries. We measure this cost by bounding the minimum token count from both sides, with and without a regular-expression boundary rule. Nonnegative prices on token occurrences yield a lower bound through shortest paths and vocabulary-budget selection; maximising over all prices recovers the linear programming relaxation, and an independent integer checker certifies the reported values. On English Wikipedia, boundaries increase the optimal token count by 28.3--36.8\%. Byte pair encoding lies 2.1\% above the constrained lower bound, but 10.9\% above the unrestricted bound. Compression and prediction favour different dictionaries: at 85M non-embedding parameters and matched training-token budgets, unrestricted fitting yields higher mean held-out bits per byte under a common unrestricted decoder in all 12 languages in the paired study and 11 of 12 under independent tuning and evaluation. To study intermediate boundary policies, we introduce boundary licences, which limit the vocabulary entries permitted to cross cuts and admit the same form of certificate. On separate English and Chinese fitting corpora, licensing 10\% of the vocabulary budget recovers 85.2\% and 100.0\% of the achieved token-count reduction from removing all cuts. These results quantify the compression cost of boundaries while separating it from the prediction quality of the resulting token units.

</details>


### [92] [More Programs or More Rolls? Separating Coverage from Specialization in LLM Harnesses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35873)
*Ziyang Xu,Haitian Zhong,Hao Zhou,Hao Qin,Chenhan Jin,Te Qi,Shengze Xu,Tieyong Zeng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces a controlled evaluation method to separate answer coverage, repeatable task advantages, and gains from pre-execution selection in automated LLM harness generation. On 386 MATH-500 tasks, the study compares eight generated harnesses with nine baseline copies across three executions each, finding that while generated programs show more repeatable score patterns, these primarily reveal persistent weaknesses rather than useful specialization. The study establishes that coverage and repeatability alone cannot justify claims of useful specialization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to address the difficulty in identifying task specialization in LLM harnesses due to answer coverage arising from repeated execution of the same program. The authors seek to establish a rigorous evaluation standard for harness diversity that can properly assess specialization gains.

Method: The study uses a controlled evaluation on 386 MATH-500 tasks, comparing eight generated harnesses plus a baseline with nine byte-identical baseline copies, using three executions per member. The evaluation measures oracle headroom, repeatability of score patterns, persistent wins/losses, and oracle coverage. BIRD traces are used to locate failures in mechanism implementation, activation, and output validity.

Result: Key findings include: (1) Identical programs yield 2.16 percentage points of repeat-averaged oracle headroom; (2) Generated programs exhibit more repeatable score patterns but reveal persistent weaknesses - losses persist across all three repeats on 100 tasks while persistent wins occur on only one task and are sensitive to answer extraction; (3) Frozen selector gains 0.00 percentage points; (4) Both populations reach 98.70% oracle coverage at 27 harness executions; (5) Stable complementarity remains unresolved at three repeats.

Conclusion: The study concludes that coverage and repeatability alone cannot justify claims of useful specialization. It motivates an evaluation standard for harness diversity: task advantages should persist across executions, guide usable decisions, and improve on additional fixed-program executions under matched inference budgets.

Abstract: Automated generation of LLM harnesses promises to improve inference through task specialization. Yet additional answer coverage can arise from repeated execution of the same program, making specialization difficult to identify. We introduce a controlled evaluation that separates answer coverage, repeatable task advantages, and gains from pre-execution selection. On 386 MATH-500 tasks, we compare eight generated harnesses plus a baseline with nine byte-identical baseline copies, using three executions per member. Identical programs yield 2.16 percentage points of repeat-averaged oracle headroom. Generated programs exhibit substantially more repeatable score patterns, but these chiefly reveal persistent weaknesses: losses relative to the baseline persist across all three repeats on 100 tasks, while persistent wins occur on only one task and are sensitive to answer extraction. The frozen selector gains 0.00 percentage points, and both populations reach 98.70% oracle coverage at 27 harness executions. Stable complementarity remains unresolved at three repeats. Supporting BIRD traces locate failures in mechanism implementation, activation, and output validity. Together, these findings establish why coverage and repeatability alone cannot justify claims of useful specialization. They motivate an evaluation standard for harness diversity: task advantages should persist across executions, guide usable decisions, and improve on additional fixed-program executions under matched inference budgets.

</details>


### [93] [Risk-Averse Online POMDP Planning via CVaR of the Immediate Cost with Performance Guarantees](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35874)
*Yaacov Pariente,Vadim Indelman*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a novel risk-averse POMDP planning approach that applies Conditional Value at Risk (CVaR) to the immediate cost over the belief at each step, rather than to the value function. This preserves standard MDP structure, allowing existing expectation-based planners to be made risk-sensitive by only modifying cost computation, while maintaining finite-time guarantees.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Online POMDP planners optimize expected cumulative cost, which can mask dangerous states when the belief places significant mass on high-cost states. Existing risk-averse methods that apply CVaR to the value function have two limitations: they don't address risk within the belief, and they require new tailored algorithms rather than reusing existing planners.

Method: Apply CVaR to the immediate cost over the belief at each step (per-step uncertainty), while retaining the standard expected cumulative return as the objective. This maintains standard MDP structure, allowing any expectation-based POMDP planner to become risk-sensitive by only changing cost computation. The approach inherits finite-time guarantees for policy evaluation and sparse sampling, with estimation error independent of risk level.

Result: Theoretical results include: (1) finite-time guarantees for policy evaluation and sparse sampling, (2) a finite-time bound on the gap between the particle belief MDP surrogate and the original POMDP, (3) end-to-end guarantee from true POMDP value to algorithmic estimate, and (4) recovery of standard expectation-based planning in the risk-neutral limit.

Conclusion: The proposed approach provides a principled way to incorporate risk-sensitivity into POMDP planning while maintaining compatibility with existing algorithms. By targeting per-step uncertainty directly and preserving MDP structure, it bridges the gap between theoretical risk-averse planning and practical implementation.

Abstract: Online POMDP planners optimize the expected cumulative cost, which can mask dangerous states when the belief places significant mass on high-cost states. Existing risk-averse methods apply static or dynamic Conditional Value at Risk (CVaR) to the value function, capturing trajectory-level risk, but share two gaps: (i) by retaining the immediate cost as an expectation of a state-dependent cost over the belief, the risk \emph{within} the belief is left unaddressed; and (ii) by modifying the value function, they require new tailored algorithms rather than reusing existing expectation-based planners. We instead apply CVaR to the immediate cost over the belief at each step, directly targeting per-step uncertainty about the current state. The standard expected cumulative return is retained as the objective, so the resulting problem has a standard MDP structure: any expectation-based POMDP planner can be made risk-sensitive by changing only the cost computation. We inherit finite-time guarantees for policy evaluation and sparse sampling---with estimation error independent of the risk level---and, as our central theoretical result, prove a finite-time bound on the gap between the particle belief MDP surrogate and the original POMDP, which together yield an end-to-end guarantee from the true POMDP value to the algorithmic estimate. In the risk-neutral limit, the formulation recovers standard expectation-based planning.

</details>


### [94] [Beyond Symmetric Agents: Cognitive Diversity and Multi-Agent Debate in Small Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35875)
*Leonardo Ferreira,Gardenia Liu,Kaden Zheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper challenges the common assumption that cognitive diversity among AI agents drives the performance gains in multi-agent debate (MAD) systems. Through extensive experiments across 23 models, 5 tasks, and 5,500+ runs, the authors find that diversity along personas, sampling temperature, and model identity axes does not explain MAD's benefits. Instead, they demonstrate that MAD's gains are largely attributable to ensemble-sampling effects, and that proper budget-matched comparisons reveal MAD often underperforms self-consistency sampling at significantly higher computational costs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The research motivation stems from a critical gap in the multi-agent debate literature: prior work treats agents as symmetric peers without investigating what actually drives the reported reasoning and factuality improvements. The authors seek to determine whether cognitive diversity (through varied personas, temperatures, or model identities) is the actual driver of MAD gains, or if other factors are at play.

Method: The authors conducted a large-scale empirical study across 23 models from eleven vendor families and five tasks, with over 5,500 debate and control runs. They manipulated three diversity axes: personas, sampling temperature, and model identity. Each debate configuration was paired with a generation-budget-matched majority-vote control for fair comparison. They also performed dose-response experiments over combinatorial persona spaces and identified a measurement hazard related to context window overflow.

Result: The hypothesis that cognitive diversity drives MAD gains was rejected on all three axes. Debate beats single-agent inference by 3-7 points but loses to or ties with self-consistency sampling at matched budgets, while costing 1.6x more in wall-clock time and 3.4x in tokens. Persona prompting reduces accuracy (a "persona tax" not diversity tax), with redundant personas hurting most. Mixed-model teams underperform majority votes over their own rosters, and most benefits come from the first exchange. After correcting for context window overflow, the debate-vs-sampling comparison moves from -1.8 points to parity.

Conclusion: The authors conclude that reported MAD gains should be recast as ensemble-sampling effects rather than genuine debate benefits. They provide a budget-matched, contamination-checked baseline that future debate mechanisms must clear. The work establishes a new standard for rigorous evaluation of multi-agent systems, emphasizing the importance of proper baseline comparisons and controlling for computational costs.

Abstract: Multi-agent debate (MAD) reportedly improves reasoning and factuality over single-model inference, but prior work treats agents as symmetric peers, leaving open what drives the gains. We test the hypothesis that cognitive diversity among agents is the driver, in the setting where the question is still measurable: small open-weight models with benchmark headroom. Across 23 models from eleven vendor families, five tasks, and 5,500+ debate and control runs, we vary diversity along three axes - personas, sampling temperature, and model identity - pairing every debate configuration with a generation-budget-matched majority-vote control. The hypothesis is rejected on every axis. Debate beats single-agent inference (3--7 points where tasks have headroom) but at matched budget conditions it ties or even loses to self-consistency sampling at 1.6$\times$ the wall-clock and 3.4$\times$ the token cost. Persona prompting reduces accuracy and a dose-response experiment over each model's full combinatorial persona space shows the cost is a persona tax, not a diversity tax: redundant personas hurt most, while maximally-diverse teams recover part of the loss. Furthermore, mixed-model teams lose to majority votes over their own rosters, with accuracy tracking member capability rather than heterogeneity, and nearly all of debate's benefit comes from the first exchange of answers. We further identify a pervasive measurement hazard in which debate transcripts silently overflow serving context windows, whose correction alone moves our debate-versus-sampling comparison from $-1.8$ points to parity. Our results recast reported MAD gains as an ensemble-sampling effect and provide the budget-matched, contamination-checked baseline bar that future debate mechanisms should be required to clear.

</details>


### [95] [Representational Simplicity and Circuit Size Dissociate in a Threshold-Dependent Way: A Controlled Test via Adversarial Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35890)
*Adam Elimadi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether "clean" neural network representations (sparse-autoencoder decomposability and concentrated feature attribution) actually predict smaller or more tractable causal circuits. Using adversarial training as a controlled instrument on GPT-2 Small, the authors find that while robust models show better SAE decomposability and fewer engaged SAE features, circuit size is regime-dependent: standard models match or exceed robust models at lower faithfulness levels (<85%), but robust models require substantially fewer edges at high faithfulness (90%, 95%).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The field increasingly treats sparse-autoencoder decomposability and concentrated feature attribution as evidence that a model's computation is easier to reverse-engineer. However, whether this representational and attributional cleanliness actually predicts smaller or more tractable causal circuits remains an open question. This is the first controlled empirical test of this hypothesis at the circuit level.

Method: Starting from the same pretrained GPT-2 Small checkpoint, the authors apply matched standard and adversarial continual training. Both conditions must retain competence on indirect object identification (IOI) and pass independent robustness verification. They then compare models along three axes: (1) sparse-autoencoder decomposability, (2) SAE feature engagement in task attribution, and (3) size of faithful circuits recovered from the raw computational graph. Findings were validated across a seven-point sweep and a second corpus.

Result: The robust model is more SAE-decomposable and engages fewer SAE features in task attribution. Circuit size is regime-dependent: on competence-matched IOI, standard leads or ties below 85% faithfulness, but robust needs substantially fewer edges at high faithfulness (90%, 95%). This pattern was established on the primary pair while representational trends generalize across a seven-point sweep and a second corpus.

Conclusion: Representational and attributional simplicity (SAE decomposability, concentrated attribution) do translate into causal simplicity at the circuit level, but only in the high-faithfulness regime. At lower faithfulness requirements, standard models can match or exceed robust models in circuit tractability. This suggests the relationship between representation cleanliness and circuit complexity is non-linear and depends on the fidelity requirements of the reverse-engineering task.

Abstract: Sparse-autoencoder decomposability and concentrated feature attribution are increasingly treated as evidence that a model's computation is easier to reverse-engineer. Whether this representational and attributional cleanliness actually predicts a smaller or more tractable causal circuit remains an open question. We test this directly using adversarial training as a controlled instrument: it reliably reshapes internal representations, but this alone does not constitute a test of circuit size. We investigate this question through reverse-engineering complexity: the causal structure required to recover a model's behavior at a fixed level of faithfulness. To our knowledge, this is the first controlled empirical test of whether representational or attributional simplicity translates into causal simplicity at the circuit level. Starting from the same pretrained GPT-2 Small checkpoint, we apply matched standard and adversarial continual training, requiring both conditions to retain competence on indirect object identification and pass independent robustness verification before comparing mechanisms. We then compare the models along three complementary axes: sparse-autoencoder decomposability, SAE feature engagement in task attribution, and the size of faithful circuits recovered from the raw computational graph. The robust model is more SAE-decomposable and engages fewer SAE features in task attribution. Circuit size is regime-dependent: on competence-matched IOI, standard leads or ties below 85% faithfulness, but robust needs substantially fewer edges at high faithfulness (90%, 95%), a pattern established on the primary pair while representational trends generalize across a seven-point sweep and a second corpus.

</details>


### [96] [Self-discovering RL in the Era of Experience: Is Learning History an Asset or a Burden?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35897)
*Haomin Luo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents the first causal mechanistic audit of a self-discovered RL algorithm (Disco103), examining how recurrent history affects learning dynamics across five pillars of the "Era of Experience" framework. Through surgical manipulation of recurrent states, the study reveals how history can be an asset or burden depending on how it's maintained and updated.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Self-improving AI architectures (RSI) toward general intelligence require underlying optimization engines capable of cumulative adaptation. While algorithm self-discovery has produced Disco103 achieving SOTA performance, its internal update machinery remains a black box. This work aims to inspect this black box through causal mechanistic auditing.

Method: Causal mechanistic audit using surgical techniques: pinning, freezing, and transplanting recurrent states while holding meta-parameters fixed. Tested across three experiments: (1) examining reward scale expansion with history, (2) decoupling historical content from maintenance to isolate clamping effects, (3) controlling replay retention under environmental change to test plasticity.

Result: Three key findings: (1) Recurrent history expands usable reward scales from three to six decades; (2) Mismatched history burden stems from perpetual clamping, but allowing imported state to evolve naturally attenuates this; (3) Controlling replay retention reveals external data turnover can confound internal plasticity, reversing DQN's adaptation advantage. Results validated through capability thresholds and ported to a second rule (OPEN).

Conclusion: This work establishes a foundational audit standard for next-generation self-evolving RL algorithms by grounding macro-RSI ambitions in micro-level learning dynamics. It demonstrates the importance of understanding internal update mechanisms rather than treating them as black boxes.

Abstract: The pursuit of recursive self-improvement (RSI) toward general intelligence is divided between macro-level language model scaling and the interaction-driven principles of "Era of Experience". Yet, any self-improving architecture ultimately rests upon its underlying optimization engine: if general intelligence requires learning from grounded interaction, the reinforcement learning (RL) update rule itself must be capable of cumulative adaptation. While algorithm self-discovery has produced Disco103 that surpassed PPO to achieve SOTA benchmark performance -- its internal update machinery remains an uninspected black box. We present the first causal mechanistic audit of a self-discovered RL rule, structured directly around the five pillars of the Era of Experience: extended horizon, grounded reward scales, continuing streams, within-lifetime change, and exploration depth. By surgically pinning, freezing, and transplanting recurrent states while holding meta-parameters fixed, we test when learning history acts as an asset or a burden. Three findings organize the audit: (1) Recurrent history actively expands usable reward scales, sustaining a six-decade window versus three under zero-pinning. (2) Decoupling historical content from its maintenance reveals that the penalty of mismatched history stems from perpetual clamping; allowing imported state to evolve naturally attenuates this burden. (3) Under environmental change, controlling replay retention reverses the apparent adaptation advantage over DQN, demonstrating that external data turnover can confound internal plasticity. Validated through capability thresholds and ported to a second rule (OPEN), this work grounds macro-RSI ambitions in micro-level learning dynamics, establishing a foundational audit standard for next-generation, self-evolving RL algorithms.

</details>


### [97] [Grab a Coffee: Future-Aware Guidance for Discrete Diffusion with Compiled Objectives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35924)
*Hua,Xu,Dongxin Li,Gwen Yidou-Weng,Guy Van den Broeck,Wei Wang,Anji Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: COFFEE is a plug-and-play framework for guiding discrete diffusion models with sequence-level objectives without retraining. It addresses the exponential complexity problem of enumerating all token completions by separating sequence dependence from the objective using a target-free carrier and a compiled finite-state model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Discrete diffusion models generate sequences by resolving multiple tokens in parallel, but guiding this process with sequence-level objectives is difficult because the value of unresolved tokens depends on combinations with other tokens. Enumerating all completions leads to exponential computational growth.

Method: COFFEE uses two key components: (1) a target-free carrier that absorbs marginal token distributions predicted by the denoiser to construct a joint model over unresolved tokens, and (2) a compiled finite-state model that records how token combinations affect sequence-level preference. By pairing their states, COFFEE transfers global preferences to unresolved positions and samples clean reconstructions without retraining.

Result: COFFEE achieves strong control results with task-dependent quality and diversity trade-offs across multiple symbolic, language, and biological benchmarks.

Conclusion: COFFEE brings joint conditioning, completion-weighted guidance, and optimization-based constraints into pretrained neural generation, demonstrating the potential of neural-symbolic methods in diffusion guidance. It makes objectives available at inference time rather than only evaluation.

Abstract: Discrete diffusion models generate sequences by iteratively resolving multiple tokens in parallel, offering a flexible alternative to left-to-right generation. However, guiding this process with a sequence-level objective is difficult because the value of one unresolved token depends on the other tokens with which it can form a high-reward sequence. Enumerating all such completions makes the whole guidance computation grow exponentially with the number of unresolved positions. We introduce COFFEE, a plug-and-play framework that avoids this enumeration by separating sequence dependence from the objective. At each diffusion step, a target-free carrier absorbs the marginal token distributions predicted by the denoiser to construct a joint model over the unresolved tokens, while a compiled finite-state model records how their combinations affect the sequence-level preference. Pairing their states allows COFFEE to transfer global preferences to unresolved positions and sample a clean reconstruction without retraining the diffusion model. The same framework supports explicit hard constraints and learned soft objectives. We evaluate COFFEE across multiple symbolic, language, and biological benchmarks, where it achieves strong control results with task-dependent quality and diversity trade-offs. By making objectives available to inference rather than only evaluation, COFFEE brings joint conditioning, completion-weighted guidance, and optimization-based constraints into pretrained neural generation, showing the potential of neural-symbolic methods in diffusion guidance.

</details>


### [98] [Right Words, Wrong Moment: A Clinician-Grounded Analysis of Distress in 19,930 Conversations between Young People and ChatGPT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35953)
*Marx Wang,Ella Zhang,Cameron Tan,Andrea Mock,Songling Ngo,Zijing Wang,Robert Wolfe,Shirin Amouei,Rachel A. Hanebutt,Desmond C. Ong,Caroline Figueroa,Katie Davis,Anind K. Dey,Alexis Hiniker*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This study examines how young adults (18-25) use ChatGPT in moments of distress, analyzing 19,930 conversations and clinician feedback to identify 7 process failures and propose 3-stage design guidelines for more effective crisis support.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Young people increasingly use AI chatbots like ChatGPT during emotional distress, but these tools may provide inadequate or potentially harmful responses in crisis situations.

Method: Mixed-methods approach: (1) Survey data from 158 young adults with 19,930 ChatGPT conversations; (2) Qualitative analysis of 5 example distress conversations; (3) Expert review by 10 clinicians to identify failures and propose guidelines.

Result: Distressed users showed greater emotional engagement and behavioral change. ChatGPT responses were overly dramatic with excessive action-oriented suggestions. Clinicians identified 7 process failures including premature solution-jumping.

Conclusion: Three-stage design guidelines proposed: (1) ask about safety, (2) de-escalate intensity to restore emotional regulation, (3) explore concerns without agreeing. These aim to improve GPCAs' crisis response capabilities.

Abstract: Young people increasingly turn to General-Purpose Conversational Agents (GPCAs), such as ChatGPT, in moments of distress. We examine young adults' (ages 18-25) experiences using ChatGPT. We first collected 19,930 ChatGPT conversations and survey data from 158 young adults. We then selected five example conversations reflecting user distress. Finally, we asked ten clinicians to review those five conversations. We found distressed participants reported greater emotional engagement with ChatGPT and greater behavioral change from using it than their peers. When they turned to ChatGPT in moments of acute distress, ChatGPT was quick to give overly dramatic responses and excessive action-oriented suggestions. Clinicians endorsed ChatGPT's availability and much of its wording, but identified seven process failures, such as prematurely jumping to solutions. We translated clinicians' feedback into design guidelines following three stages: 1) asking about safety, 2) de-escalating intensity to restore emotional regulation, and 3) exploring concerns without agreeing with them.

</details>


### [99] [SAGE: A Statistical Acceptance Gate for Self-Evolving Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36043)
*Yihao Wang,Linhan Xia,Rui Liu,Zhaofeng Zhang,Hongyu Wu,Yang Yang,Jinglu He,Yu Guo,Kai Lei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SAGE is a statistical acceptance gate for self-evolving LLM-based agents that addresses two flaws in the naive rule-based gate: permanent regressions and Optimizer's Curse. It uses per-item paired comparison and one-sided paired testing to filter unreliable edits, achieving 0% regression rate in 19/20 settings across benchmarks and LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work on self-evolving LLM agents focused on the optimizer but neglected the gate mechanism. The naive gate (accepting any edit that improves aggregate validation score) has two critical flaws: (1) it admits permanent regressions by allowing edits that raise averages but break previously solved items, and (2) it suffers from Optimizer's Curse where the best observed score on finite/noisy validation sets is upward biased.

Method: SAGE introduces two key mechanisms: (1) Per-item paired comparison that evaluates current and edited skills on identical validation items to expose regressions hidden by aggregate scores, with asymmetric penalty; (2) One-sided paired test that commits an edit only when wins are statistically reliable against losses, abstaining otherwise. This is a conservative refinement that recovers baseline at boundary settings.

Result: Across five benchmarks and four backbone LLMs under equal-budget protocol: SAGE lowers regression rate in 19 of 20 settings and matches baseline in the remaining one. Examples: regression reduced from 36.5% to 0% on LiveMath and from 42.8% to 0% on OfficeQA with DeepSeek-V4. SAGE achieves highest final score in all 20 settings, raising LiveMath from 34.15 to 48.78.

Conclusion: SAGE successfully addresses the limitations of naive rule-based gates in self-evolving LLM agents by using statistical testing and per-item comparison. It filters out unreliable edits and those that break already-solved items, providing a more conservative and reliable evolution mechanism that prevents regression while maintaining or improving performance.

Abstract: Large Language Model (LLM)-based agents increasingly self-evolve by editing a persistent skill document that encodes their workflow, tool-use rules, and decision logic. This loop has two steps, an optimizer that proposes a candidate edit and a gate that accepts or rejects it. Prior work has concentrated on the optimizer, while the gate still follows a naive rule that keeps any edit which improves an aggregate validation score. We show that this rule fails in two ways. First, it admits permanent regressions, since an edit can raise the average while breaking items the skill already solves. Second, it is vulnerable to the Optimizer's Curse, since the best observed score on a finite and noisy validation set is upward biased. To solve the above two limitations, we propose a statistical acceptance gate for self-evolving agents (SAGE). Compared with previous work, SAGE has two contributions. First, SAGE proposes a per-item paired comparison that evaluates the current skill and the edited skill on identical validation items, which exposes regressions that an aggregate score hides and penalizes them asymmetrically. Second, SAGE also employs a one-sided paired test that commits an edit only when its wins are statistically reliable against its losses, and it abstains otherwise. SAGE is a conservative refinement of the standard gate that recovers the baseline exactly at a boundary setting. It commits only a subset of the baseline's edits, filtering out those whose gains are unreliable or purchased by breaking already-solved items. Across five benchmarks and four backbone LLMs under an equal-budget protocol, SAGE lowers the regression rate in 19 of 20 settings and matches the baseline in the remaining one, for example from 36.5% to 0% on LiveMath and from 42.8% to 0% on OfficeQA with DeepSeek-V4. SAGE also attains the highest final score in all 20 settings, raising LiveMath from 34.15 to 48.78.

</details>


### [100] [PowerZooJax: A JAX-based Power System Benchmark for Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36052)
*Zhanhua Pan,Xiao Liu,Zhilong Cao,Jianhong Wang,Dawei Qiu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PowerZooJax is a JAX-based GPU-accelerated benchmark suite for reinforcement learning in power system operation, providing five constrained MDP tasks spanning generation, transmission, distribution, distributed energy resources, and data center microgrid.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RL environments for power systems are narrow in scope and computationally limited by CPU-based simulation workflows, making large-scale evaluation difficult. There is a need for a comprehensive, GPU-accelerated benchmark suite.

Method: The authors rewrite power flow, economic dispatch, market clearing, and device dynamics as JAX computation graphs, keeping the entire training and evaluation loop on the GPU.

Result: Experiments demonstrate substantial speedups over CPU-based simulations and enable standardized evaluation of policy returns, safety violations, and out-of-distribution stress conditions.

Conclusion: PowerZooJax provides an open-source benchmark for standardized evaluation of RL agents in power system operation, with significant computational advantages through JAX-based GPU acceleration.

Abstract: Power system operation is a safety-critical sequential decision-making problem, making it a natural testbed for reinforcement learning (RL). However, existing RL environments for power systems are often narrow in scope and computationally limited by CPU-based simulation workflows, making large-scale evaluation difficult. We introduce PowerZooJax, a JAX-based benchmark suite for RL in power system operation. It provides five constrained Markov decision process tasks spanning generation, transmission, distribution, distributed energy resources, and data center microgrid. By rewriting power flow, economic dispatch, market clearing, and device dynamics as JAX computation graphs, PowerZooJax keeps the entire training and evaluation loop on the GPU. Experiments show substantial speedups over CPU-based simulations and demonstrate standardized evaluation of policy returns, safety violations, and out-of-distribution stress conditions. Our open-source benchmark is available at: https://github.com/powerzoojax/PowerZooJax.

</details>


### [101] [GeoWind2Plan: Mission-Time 3D Urban Wind Prediction for Energy-Efficient UAV Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36056)
*Shaoxiang Qin,Yucheng Zhao,Fuyuan Lyu,Di Zhou,Jiachen Yao,Xue Liu,Anima Anandkumar,Liangzhu Leon Wang,Xiongye Xiao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GeoWind2Plan is a framework for fast 3D urban wind prediction and energy-efficient UAV mission planning. It uses a geometry-conditioned neural operator to predict mission-relevant wind patches in ~3 seconds (vs. 8 hours for CFD), enabling real-time wind-aware path planning that reduces energy consumption by 4.5-12.7% compared to wind-agnostic planning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Urban low-altitude flight involves spatially varying 3D wind flows reshaped by buildings, making UAV energy consumption depend on local wind exposure. However, building-resolved wind information is rarely available at mission planning time, and CFD simulations are too computationally expensive (hours to days) for practical use in missions lasting minutes.

Method: GeoWind2Plan transforms building geometry into a reference-wind frame, predicts 3D wind patches using a localized geometry-conditioned neural operator, stitches them into a queryable local wind field, and optimizes feasible 3D paths and speed profiles using a physically grounded UAV energy model. The approach targets "decision-useful" wind prediction rather than CFD-perfect reconstruction.

Result: GeoWind2Plan performs corridor-localized wind inference in ~3 seconds (vs. ~8 hours for CFD). Under CFD evaluation, trajectories planned with GeoWind2Plan reduce energy by 6.9% (tailwind), 12.7% (headwind), and 4.5% (crosswind) compared to wind-agnostic planning, recovering 87.9%, 85.7%, and 75.0% of CFD-reference savings respectively.

Conclusion: Fast, corridor-localized 3D urban wind prediction can make wind-aware UAV energy planning practical at mission time. The approach balances computational efficiency with decision-useful accuracy, enabling energy-efficient UAV operations in complex urban environments.

Abstract: In urban low-altitude flight, buildings reshape ambient wind into spatially varying 3D flow, making unmanned aerial vehicle (UAV) energy depend on local wind exposure as well as path length. However, building-resolved wind information is rarely available when a mission must be planned. Computational fluid dynamics (CFD) can produce high-fidelity urban flow fields, but each simulation is tied to a fixed inflow boundary condition and can take hours to days, which is incompatible with urban UAV missions that typically last minutes to tens of minutes. We present GeoWind2Plan, a geometry-to-wind-to-planning framework for mission-time 3D urban wind prediction and energy-efficient UAV planning. Given only a background wind vector, 3D building geometry, and a start-goal pair, GeoWind2Plan transforms the building geometry into a reference-wind frame, predicts mission-relevant 3D wind patches with a localized geometry-conditioned neural operator, stitches them into a queryable local wind field, and optimizes a feasible 3D path and speed profile using a physically grounded UAV energy model. Rather than pursuing CFD-perfect reconstruction, GeoWind2Plan targets decision-useful wind prediction: trajectories are planned with predicted wind and evaluated under high-fidelity CFD wind. Across held-out urban domains, wind speeds, and mission wind-angle regimes, GeoWind2Plan performs corridor-localized wind inference in about 3 seconds, compared with roughly 8 hours for CFD. Under CFD evaluation, trajectories planned with GeoWind2Plan reduce energy by 6.9%, 12.7%, and 4.5% in tailwind, headwind, and crosswind missions relative to wind-agnostic planning, recovering 87.9%, 85.7%, and 75.0% of CFD-reference savings. These results show that fast, corridor-localized 3D urban wind prediction can make wind-aware UAV energy planning practical at mission time.

</details>


### [102] [Mirror-Score: Calibrated, Inference-only Scoring Exposes the Limits of Sequence-compatibility Ranking in D-peptide Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36057)
*Jiada Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Mirror-Score is a calibrated scoring framework for predicting binding affinities of D-peptide/L-protein complexes. The study reveals that raw ProteinMPNN NLL is not a valid affinity ranker, while Boltz-2 pLDDT shows strong correlation (rho=0.90) within the viral-entry family. A benchmark of 31 crystal complexes across 4 target families is presented, along with a design protocol for antimicrobial resistance targets LasR and LecB.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous computational design of D-peptide binders (Mirror-Peptidizer) had limitations: raw ProteinMPNN NLL was not validated against measured affinities, and only 4/9 MDM2 designs showed detectable binding. There was a need for a validated scoring framework for heterochiral peptide-protein complexes.

Method: 1) Developed Mirror-Score, an inference-only scoring framework for D-peptide/L-protein complexes; 2) Created a benchmark of 31 crystal complexes across 4 target families (18 with verified affinities); 3) Evaluated ProteinMPNN NLL and Boltz-2 mirror-space cofolding confidence (pLDDT) as ranking methods; 4) Tested cross-family vs family-matched calibration.

Result: Raw ProteinMPNN NLL shows poor overall correlation with affinity (Spearman rho=0.19) with inconsistent direction across families (MDM2/CHIP: +0.62, gp41: -0.70). For the viral-entry family (7 structures, 3 peptides), interface pLDDT achieves leave-one-out Spearman rho=0.90 (p=0.006) and correctly ranks all three peptides by affinity. Cross-family calibration fails at current sample sizes, supporting family-matched calibration.

Conclusion: Boltz-2 pLDDT is a promising structure-based predictor within protein families, but cross-family transferability remains limited. Family-matched calibration is recommended for practical deployment. A prospective design protocol for LasR and LecB targets is provided. All resources are openly available on GitHub.

Abstract: D-peptides combine protease resistance with high target specificity, but computational design of D-peptide binders remains immature. Mirror-Peptidizer introduced an in silico mirror-image screening pipeline using target reflection, backbone generation, and ProteinMPNN sequence design, but its raw ProteinMPNN negative log-likelihood (NLL) ranking was not validated against measured affinities, and only 4 of 9 tested MDM2 designs bound detectably. We introduce Mirror-Score, a calibrated, inference-only scoring framework for heterochiral D-peptide/L-protein complexes, and a public benchmark of 31 crystal complexes across four target families, including 18 with literature-verified affinities. Raw ProteinMPNN NLL is not a valid affinity ranker: its pooled Spearman correlation with affinity is 0.19, and correlations reverse between MDM2/CHIP (+0.62) and gp41 (-0.70). We therefore evaluate Boltz-2 mirror-space cofolding confidence. For the complete viral-entry family (7 structures representing 3 peptides), interface predicted local distance difference test (pLDDT) achieves structure-level leave-one-out Spearman rho = 0.90 (p = 0.006) and correctly orders all three peptides by affinity, whereas NLL fails (structure-level rho = 0.18). Because only three independent chemotypes are represented, this result indicates directional consistency rather than a statistically validated predictor. Cross-family calibration does not transfer at current sample sizes, supporting family-matched calibration as the practical deployment mode. We also specify a prospective design protocol for the antimicrobial-resistance targets LasR and LecB from Pseudomonas aeruginosa, including mirrored structures, ligand-derived hotspot maps, diffusion-model-ready inputs, and Mirror-Score ranking. Code, benchmark data, structures, and analysis scripts are openly available at https://github.com/Jiadalee/Mirror-Score.

</details>


### [103] [SMat-Attention: Structured Long-Context Sequence Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36062)
*Emile Anand,Abdullah Ateyeh,Archer Wang,Marin Soljačić*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces Structured Matrix Attention (SMat-Attention), which uses VC-dimension as a tunable parameter to bridge the gap between quadratic-cost softmax attention and subquadratic linear attention by controlling routing complexity in attention masks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-context sequence models face a fundamental tradeoff: softmax attention has quadratic cost but flexible token interactions, while linear attention is efficient but compresses history into a fixed-size state. This work seeks to connect these regimes through a tunable notion of structure.

Method: The authors introduce SMat-Attention using a family of causal masks with structured long-range routing whose row supports have VC-dimension d. They develop chunkwise forward and backward algorithms for hardware efficiency. The approach allows explicit control over access-pattern complexity, prefill cost, and decoding memory through the VC-dimension parameter.

Result: For sequences of length T, the hard-routing construction achieves O(T^{2-3/d}+T) prefill cost despite a dense mask. In fixed-horizon streaming, decoding takes constant time per token using O(T^{1-1/d}) cached states. Empirically, subset-routing and rule-assisted multi-key retrieval experiments demonstrate the masks' routing expressiveness. Extensions to Mamba-2 and Gated DeltaNet with learned routing retain subquadratic prefill, improve recall accuracy over backbones, and achieve comparable small-scale language modeling performance.

Conclusion: SMat-Attention makes VC-dimension an explicit knob governing access-pattern complexity, prefill cost, and decoding memory. This provides a principled way to balance computational efficiency with routing expressiveness in long-context models, with promising results on learned routing extensions to state-space models.

Abstract: Long-context sequence models face a fundamental tradeoff: softmax attention uses flexible token-level interactions at quadratic cost, whereas linear attention obtains linear-time training and constant-time decoding by compressing history into a fixed-size state. In this work, we ask whether we can connect these regimes through a tunable notion of structure. To this end, we introduce Structured Matrix Attention (SMat-Attention) via a family of causal masks with structured long-range routing whose row supports have VC-dimension $d$. In our construction, $d=1$ recovers the standard causal mask, and increasing $d$ permits richer subset-routing patterns. We give chunkwise forward and backward algorithms to enable hardware-efficiency. For sequences of length $T$, the hard-routing construction takes $O(T^{2-3/d}+T)$ work, despite the mask being dense, for our prescribed family. In fixed-horizon streaming, decoding after the distant prefix takes constant time per token using $O(T^{1-1/d})$ cached states. SMat-Attention therefore makes VC-dimension an explicit knob governing access-pattern complexity, prefill cost, and decoding memory. Empirically, subset-routing and rule-assisted multi-key retrieval experiments illustrate the masks' routing expressiveness. Extensions to Mamba-2 and Gated DeltaNet using learned routing with top-$k$ query reads retain subquadratic prefill, improve recall accuracy over the backbones in several settings, and achieve comparable small-scale language-modeling performance.

</details>


### [104] [LongCat-DeepResearch Technical Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36071)
*Meituan LongCat Team,He Zhu,Yue Xu,Wanli Wu,Haolin Ren,Yuxin Bian,Jiarui Zhao,Rongzhi Zhang,Quanchi Weng,Jinghao Cui,Yu Fan,Yuhan Liu,Yunhu Ye,Jiyuan Ren,Fengcheng Yuan,Zhao Yang,Jiacheng Zhang,Yuchuan Dai,Ruixuan Xiao,Haozhe Sun,Xiangyuan Liu,Cheng Sun,Yao Du,Yiming Hao,Hongbo Guo,Shuo He,Lei Wang,Xunliang Cai,Yan Chen,Fan Yang,Lingchuan Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LongCat-DeepResearch is a multi-agent AI research system that combines an enhanced LongCat model with a workflow separating global planning from detailed investigation, achieving strong performance on research benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to create a deep research system that can produce comprehensive, evidence-grounded reports through coordinated multi-agent workflows, with the ability to support mid-training and post-training of general-purpose models.

Method: The system uses a multi-agent workflow with: (1) multiple planning agents that explore external sources and refine a ResearchSpec research plan, (2) parallel research agents that investigate and draft assigned sections in separate contexts, and (3) global review that guides targeted local revisions at the section level, reducing repeated full-report rewriting.

Result: The system achieves 55.25 on DeepResearchBench, 51.35 on DeepResearchBench II, 79.83 on ResearchRubrics, and 76.04 on an in-house benchmark (ranking second among four compared systems).

Conclusion: Combining planning perspectives provides benefits, while further planning refinement shows mixed effects. Additional editing improves readability with different trends across benchmarks.

Abstract: We present LongCat-DeepResearch, a deep research system that combines an enhanced LongCat model with a multi-agent workflow for producing comprehensive, evidence-grounded reports. The workflow separates global planning from detailed investigation and coordinates revision at the section level. Multiple planning agents first explore external sources and refine an actionable research plan, termed ResearchSpec. Research agents then investigate and draft their assigned sections in parallel, gathering additional evidence in separate contexts as their analyses develop. Once the sections are assembled, global review guides targeted local revisions, reducing reliance on repeated full-report rewriting. This workflow also supports the construction of research tasks and trajectories for the mid-training and post-training of LongCat's general-purpose models. LongCat-DeepResearch achieves 55.25 on DeepResearchBench, 51.35 on DeepResearchBench II, and 79.83 on ResearchRubrics. On an in-house benchmark, it scores 76.04, ranking second among four compared systems. Development-set analyses show benefits from combining planning perspectives, while further planning refinement has mixed effects. Additional editing improves average automatic readability preference across two benchmarks, with different trends on each.

</details>


### [105] [A Polyphonic Conception of AI Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36079)
*Matthieu Queloz,Pierre Beckmann*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper challenges the prevailing "monophonic" view of AI understanding—the idea that a cognitive system's understanding must be localized to a single mechanism. The authors argue that Large Language Models (LLMs) are instead "pervasively polyphonic": their outputs emerge from coalitions of parallel mechanisms of uneven reliability that complement, duplicate, or compete with each other. This polyphonic nature makes traditional attributions of understanding hazardous. The authors propose a new conception of understanding centered on "sound circuitry"—reliable mechanisms correctly recruited and in control of outputs—which makes attributions of understanding tractable claims about internal organization capable of guiding trust in AI systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation stems from a practical problem: professionals (doctors, judges, engineers) must decide whether to trust AI outputs, which requires understanding what the model "understands." Current mathematical/statistical descriptions fail to distinguish trustworthy from untrustworthy outputs without re-introducing the question of understanding. The inherited concept of understanding is problematic because it assumes a monophonic paradigm—where understanding is localized to a single mechanism—which the authors argue is fundamentally ill-framed for modern AI systems.

Method: The authors draw on a wide range of mechanistic evidence to demonstrate polyphony in LLMs. They analyze how outputs emerge from coalitions of parallel mechanisms with varying reliability, showing how multiple mechanisms can complement, duplicate, or interfere with each other. They also examine how several coalitions can suffice for a task without any single mechanism being indispensable.

Result: The key result is the identification and characterization of "polyphony" in LLMs—where multiple parallel mechanisms of uneven reliability jointly produce outputs. This polyphonic organization complicates traditional attributions of understanding and renders monophonic inference patterns (assuming a single underlying mechanism) hazardous. The authors develop an alternative conception of understanding fit for polyphonic AI, centered on "sound circuitry" that is reliably and correctly recruited and in control of outputs.

Conclusion: The conclusion is that attributions of understanding in AI systems should become tractable claims about internal organization rather than binary judgments about whether understanding exists. By focusing on sound circuitry—mechanisms that are reliably and correctly recruited—understanding attributions can do the practical work of guiding trust in AI systems. This polyphonic conception provides a more adequate framework for evaluating AI understanding in real-world applications where multiple mechanisms contribute to task performance.

Abstract: When a doctor, a judge, or an engineer must decide whether to trust an AI model's output, they cannot avoid asking what the model understands. Purely mathematical or statistical descriptions struggle to distinguish trustworthy from untrustworthy outputs without reintroducing the question of AI understanding in all but name. Yet the question is ill-framed as it stands, because the inherited concept operates within a monophonic paradigm: the idea that a cognitive system's understanding of something must be localised to a single mechanism underpinning all the capacities conferred by such understanding. Drawing on a wide range of mechanistic evidence, we show that LLMs are pervasively polyphonic: outputs emerge from coalitions of parallel mechanisms of uneven reliability, which variously complement, duplicate, or drown out one another, with several coalitions sufficing for a task without any one being indispensable. Polyphony not only complicates attributions of understanding, but renders monophonic inference patterns hazardous. In response, we develop a conception of understanding fit for polyphonic AI. It centres on sound circuitry that is reliably and correctly recruited and in control of outputs. Attributions of understanding thereby become tractable claims about internal organisation, and can do the work of guiding trust in AI.

</details>


### [106] [GeoOutageBench: Benchmarking Ambiguity-aware, Ontology-grounded Geospatiotemporal KGQA for Multimodal Power Outage and Resilience Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36082)
*Ethan D. Frakes,Amy Kvien,Rishabh Kundu,Redad Mehdi,Van D. Tran,Vibha S. Mandayam,Kristopher O. Davis,Erika I. Barcelos,Roger H. French,Yinghui Wu,Mengjie Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GeoOutageBench is a benchmark for evaluating LLM-based geospatiotemporal Knowledge Graph Question Answering (KGQA) systems focused on multimodal outage and resilience analysis for infrastructure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing KGQA benchmarks are designed for Web knowledge and don't address the unique challenges of spatiotemporal knowledge graphs that integrate visual, textual, and structured data from diverse sources like outage records, remote sensing, weather observations, storm events, and geographic entities. There is a need to assess LLM-KG systems for real-world infrastructure resilience analysis.

Method: The benchmark provides: (1) A spatiotemporal KG integrating multimodal data sources including outage records, remote sensing, weather observations, storm/power events, geographic entities, and domain ontologies; (2) A competency query taxonomy with varying difficulty levels covering spatiotemporal containment, proximity, co-occurrence analysis, multimodal evidence, and hypothetical evaluation; (3) User-configurable evaluation for three tasks: NL to SPARQL interpretation for ambiguous geospatiotemporal questions, ontology utility assessment, and multimodal KGQA retrieval accuracy.

Result: GeoOutageBench provides a comprehensive design principle and foundation for assessing LLM-KG systems that support real-world infrastructure resilience analysis. The benchmark, source code, data, and results are publicly available.

Conclusion: This benchmark addresses a critical gap in evaluating LLM-based KGQA systems for geospatiotemporal and multimodal analysis in the domain of infrastructure resilience, offering a standardized evaluation framework for future research in this area.

Abstract: We introduce GeoOutageBench, a benchmark for assessing LLM-based geospatiotemporal KGQA for multimodal outage and resilience analysis. Unlike existing KGQA benchmarks for Web knowledge, GeoOutageBench considers a spatiotemporal KG that integrates visual, textual, and structured data from outage records, remote sensing, weather observations, storm and power events, geographic entities, and domain ontologies. It provides a competency query taxonomy at different difficulty levels from spatiotemporal containment and proximity, spatiotemporal co-occurrence analysis, multimodal evidence, to hypothetical evaluation. Over multimodal KG and query classes, GeoOutageBench provides user-configurable evaluation of three important, highly coherent yet less studied tasks: (1) LLMs' understanding for ambiguous geospatiotemporal questions in terms of NL to SPARQL interpretation, (2) query-driven assessment of ontology utility, and (3) answer accuracy of multimodal KGQA retrieval. GeoOutageBench provides a design principle and foundation for assessing LLM-KG systems that support real-world infrastructure resilience analysis. Our benchmark, source code, data, results, and other documentation are available at https://github.com/UCF-SAGE/GeoOutageBench.

</details>


### [107] [An Exact Generate - Transform Decomposition of Small-LLM Team Scaling Across Orchestration Architectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36104)
*Blaz Bertalanic,Carolina Fortuna*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether scaling LLM agent teams improves accuracy across different tasks. Testing 8 architectures across 5 models and 6 benchmarks, the authors find that team scaling benefits are highly task-dependent: arithmetic tasks see up to 17-point accuracy gains while other benchmarks show at most 4-point improvements. The Proposer-Critic architecture scales best on arithmetic but underperforms on other tasks, with no architecture winning across all tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to understand whether replacing a single LLM agent with a collaborating team improves accuracy, and more importantly, whether scaling the team size continues to provide benefits. The key question is which orchestration architecture scales best and on which tasks.

Method: The study conducted large-scale experiments sweeping eight agent orchestration architectures across five instruction-tuned 7-9B models (ranging from 7B to 9B parameters), five short-answer benchmarks (GSM8K, GSMHard, ARC, GPQA, MMLU), and an executable-code benchmark, with up to 30 calls per task. They also introduced an "exact generate-transform decomposition" method to explain accuracy trajectories by partitioning workflows into proposal coverage and downstream transformation components.

Result: Key findings: (1) Team scaling returns are sharply task-dependent - arithmetic benchmarks (GSM8K, GSMHard) show up to 17-point accuracy gains from 3 to 30 calls, while ARC, GPQA, and MMLU show at most 4-point gains across all architectures. (2) Proposer-Critic architecture captures the arithmetic gains with steepest scaling, surpassing other architectures at the largest budget (with statistically significant results using item-clustered intervals). (3) However, Proposer-Critic ranks among the weakest on non-arithmetic tasks. (4) No single architecture wins across all tasks. (5) At equal call budgets, token cost varies by 2.1x. (6) The generate-transform decomposition reveals that arithmetic tasks have coverage headroom that critic-guided transforms can convert, while multiple-choice benchmarks either saturate in coverage or fail to convert it.

Conclusion: Team scaling is a task- and architecture-specific bet, not a uniform lever. Extra calls create candidate opportunity, but only some architectures on some tasks can convert this into accuracy gains. The study advises practitioners to consider task characteristics and architecture properties when deciding on team scaling strategies.

Abstract: Replacing one LLM agent with a collaborating team can raise accuracy, but whether scaling the team helps, and which architecture to scale, is unclear. Sweeping eight agent orchestration architectures across five instruction-tuned 7-9B models, five short-answer benchmarks, and an executable-code benchmark up to 30 calls, we find that the returns to team scaling are sharply task-dependent: from three to thirty calls accuracy rises by up to 17 points on the two arithmetic word-problem benchmarks (GSM8K, GSMHard) but by at most four on ARC, GPQA, and MMLU, for every architecture, a split the usual task-averaged number conceals. Proposer-Critic captures the arithmetic gains, scaling steepest and, in aggregate, surpassing every other architecture at the largest budget (item-clustered intervals exclude zero), though it ranks among the weakest elsewhere, and no architecture wins across tasks.
  We explain these trajectories with an exact generate-transform decomposition. Partitioning any workflow into proposal coverage and a downstream transform, any accuracy change splits exactly into an extensive coverage dividend and an intensive transformation change. The decomposition diagnoses each task: arithmetic offers coverage headroom that a critic-guided transform converts, whereas the multiple-choice benchmarks either saturate in coverage or fail to convert it, and on open-ended code generative recovery nearly vanishes so accuracy tracks coverage. At equal call budgets token cost still varies 2.1x. Extra calls therefore create candidate opportunity that only some architectures, on some tasks, convert. Team scaling is a task- and architecture-specific bet, not a uniform lever.

</details>


### [108] [The Layer Mystery of VLA: An Information-Theoretical Analysis of VLA Latent Interface](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36118)
*Yuxiang Liu,Lizhi Yang,Fengze Xie,Aaron Ames,Yisong Yue*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates layer selection and fusion strategies for connecting pretrained vision-language backbones to action heads in VLA policies. Across 54 configurations, fusion generally underperforms single-layer selection, but the optimal layer varies significantly across models and benchmarks. The authors propose using InfoNCE as a proxy for layer quality, which reduces GPU compute by 9-33x and selection regret from 17.89 to 3.71 percentage points compared to exhaustive sweeps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Determine which backbone layers should be exposed to the action head in VLA policies, and whether multi-layer fusion provides benefits over single-layer selection. The challenge is that optimal layer selection varies across models and benchmarks, making exhaustive sweeps expensive.

Method: Empirical study across three pretrained models (vision-language backbones) and two manipulation benchmarks (LIBERO and CALVIN), with three policy-training seeds per configuration. Tested three fusion mechanisms and three layer-subset strategies. Also derived theoretical equivalence between information-bottleneck objectives for action-conditioned InfoNCE and action prediction. Evaluated four proxies for layer quality, with InfoNCE being the most consistent.

Result: 47 out of 54 fusion configurations underperform the best single-layer policy. InfoNCE provides the most consistent positive association with policy success among four evaluated proxies. InfoNCE-based selection requires 9-33x less GPU compute and reduces mean selection regret from 17.89 to 3.71 percentage points, comparable to retrospectively optimized fixed-layer heuristics.

Conclusion: Fusion provides very limited advantage over single-layer selection. Layer selection is consequential but optimal layer varies by backbone and benchmark. InfoNCE serves as an effective proxy for layer quality, enabling efficient layer selection without closed-loop evaluations.

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) policies connect a pretrained vision-language backbone to an action head through a latent interface, but which backbone layers this interface should expose remains unclear. We study single-layer selection and multi-layer fusion for frozen backbones across three pretrained models and two manipulation benchmarks, LIBERO and CALVIN, with three policy-training seeds per configuration. Across three fusion mechanisms and three layer-subset strategies, 47 of 54 configurations underperform the best observed single-layer policy. Our stastical analysis further confirms that fusion's advantage is very limited. However, the best layer varies substantially across backbones and benchmarks, making layer selection consequential and exhaustive policy sweeps expensive. We further derive a reweighting equivalence between the proposed information-bottleneck objectives for action-conditioned InfoNCE and action prediction, motivating InfoNCE as a proxy for layer quality. Empirically, InfoNCE provides the most consistent positive association with policy success among four evaluated proxies. Selecting the layer with the highest InfoNCE score requires 9-33 times less GPU compute than exhaustive policy sweeps and reduces mean selection regret from 17.89 percentage points for deepest-layer selection to 3.71 points across six settings. Its mean regret is close to the 3.28-3.50 points achieved by fixed-layer heuristics optimized retrospectively using all six oracle sweeps, without requiring closed-loop evaluations during selection.

</details>


### [109] [AdaST: Adaptive Coupling for Spatial-Temporal Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36119)
*Zhenyu Lei,Chenghao Liu,Yushun Dong,Qi R. Wang,Jundong Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AdaST is an adaptive spatial-temporal forecasting framework that dynamically handles varying coupling regimes (temporal-dominated, spatial-dominated, strongly coupled) in real-world data through a decompose-recompose paradigm with heterogeneity-aware experts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current ST forecasting methods assume strong spatiotemporal coupling, but real-world data exhibits distinct coupling regimes. This mismatch causes spurious dependencies and degraded performance when one correlation type dominates.

Method: AdaST uses a decompose-recompose paradigm: (1) Factorizes inputs into components capturing different coupling patterns via heterogeneity-aware experts, (2) Processes each component with role-aligned modules, (3) Integrates components using a correlation-informed adaptive recomposer for final prediction.

Result: Extensive experiments show AdaST significantly outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, validating the necessity of an adaptive approach for handling varying ST coupling structures.

Conclusion: The adaptive approach effectively addresses the challenge of heterogeneous coupling dynamics in spatial-temporal forecasting, providing a more flexible and robust solution than existing methods that assume uniform coupling.

Abstract: Spatial-temporal (ST) forecasting underpins many real-world systems such as traffic, climate, and energy networks. While existing methods implicitly assume strong spatiotemporal coupling, we observe that real-world ST data exhibits distinct coupling regimes, ranging from temporal-dominated and spatial-dominated to strongly coupled patterns. This mismatch causes current models to suffer from spurious dependencies and degraded performance when one correlation dominates. To overcome this limitation, we aim to dynamically modulate spatial and temporal modeling based on the data's inherent coupling structure. However, three key challenges exist: unknown coupling structure, heterogeneous coupling dynamics, and suboptimal spatial modeling. We propose AdaST, an adaptive ST forecasting framework that tackles these challenges through a decompose-recompose paradigm. AdaST factorizes inputs into components capturing different coupling patterns using heterogeneity-aware experts. Each component is processed by role-aligned modules, and a correlation-informed adaptive recomposer integrates them for final prediction. Extensive experiments confirm that AdaST significantly outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, validating the necessity of an adaptive approach.

</details>


### [110] [Memory Is a Derivation: The Distributed-Evidence Paradox in Long-Term Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36130)
*Hongjun Liu,Chen Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper addresses a critical problem in long-running LLM agents: whether compressed memories are actually supported by the interaction history. The authors identify three key requirements (evidence scope, compositional validity, and admission reliability) and propose DerivAudit, a framework to audit memory support. Experiments show that broader history recovers support for ~60% of apparently unsupported memories, but 17-21% remain unsupported, and admission reliability remains problematic even with expanded evidence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-running LLM agents compress past interactions into persistent memories for reuse in later tasks. However, this creates a derivation problem: whether memories actually follow from the interaction history. Compression can introduce unsupported relations or event status, and relevant evidence may be scattered across earlier interactions while citations may omit key evidence.

Method: The authors introduce DerivAudit, a framework that audits whether a memory is supported by the history available at write time. The audit addresses three questions: (1) whether supporting evidence lies beyond writer-provided citations, (2) whether the composed memory introduces unsupported meaning, and (3) how write-time admission decisions affect later memory use. They conducted audits across two natural memory corpora using broader pre-write history.

Result: Audits using broader pre-write history recover support for nearly 60% of memories that appear unsupported from citations alone. However, 17-21% of memories remain unsupported after evidence expansion. Additionally, broader evidence alone does not improve admission reliability: unsupported memories are still frequently admitted across verification models, and evidence expansion alone actually worsens admission reliability on two backbones.

Conclusion: Memory compression in LLM agents can introduce unsupported derivations. While expanding evidence scope helps recover support for many apparently unsupported memories, a significant portion (17-21%) remain unsupported. Critically, evidence expansion alone is insufficient to ensure admission reliability, suggesting that better admission mechanisms are needed in addition to evidence expansion.

Abstract: Long-running LLM agents compress past interactions into persistent memories that may be reused as premises for later tasks. This creates a distinct derivation problem: whether the memory actually follows from what the interaction history supports.
