Relevant evidence may be scattered across earlier interactions, while compression can introduce relations or event status that the history never established. A valid memory may therefore appear unsupported because its citations omit relevant evidence, while individually supported facts may be composed into a stronger statement the history never established. We characterize this problem through three coupled requirements: (1) Evidence scope; (2) Compositional validity; (3) Admission reliability. We therefore ask whether the interaction history available at write time supports what enters persistent memory. We introduce DerivAudit, a framework for auditing whether a memory is actually supported by the history available when it was written. The audit separates three questions: whether supporting evidence lies beyond writer-provided citations, whether the composed memory introduces unsupported meaning, and how write-time admission decisions affect later memory use. Across two natural memory corpora, audits using broader pre-write history recover support for nearly 60% of memories that appear unsupported from citations alone, while 17-21% remain unsupported after expansion. Yet broader evidence does not by itself make admission reliable: unsupported memories are still frequently admitted across verification models, and evidence expansion alone worsens it on two backbones.

</details>


### [111] [More Features Are Not More Evidence: Limits of Training-Free Human Activity Recognition with Jev](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36154)
*Orhan Konak*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether general-purpose models can replace task-specific classifiers in Human Activity Recognition (HAR) without any training. Using Jev (a fixed probabilistic decision model) on 1,800 accelerometer windows from three datasets, the study finds that Jev significantly underperforms supervised models, achieving macro-F1 scores of 0.038-0.118 versus 0.686-0.907 for supervised approaches. More numerical features actually hurt performance, while semantic rendering partially helps. The authors conclude that the sensor-to-model interface is critical and should be treated as part of model evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether general-purpose models can make sensor-based decisions without task-specific training, potentially reducing dependence on labeled data in Human Activity Recognition (HAR) applications.

Method: Tested Jev (a fixed general-purpose probabilistic decision model) on 1,800 class-balanced accelerometer windows from three datasets (WISDM, UCI341, PAMAP2). Jev received no labeled examples, retrieval context, or HAR-specific parameter updates. Evaluated three deterministic sensor representations and compared 5,400 Jev decisions with a generative baseline and three supervised HAR models. Also performed post-hoc fusion analysis.

Result: Jev significantly underperformed supervised models across all datasets. Jev's strongest representation achieved macro-F1 of 0.038 (WISDM), 0.118 (UCI341), and 0.089 (PAMAP2), compared to 0.686-0.907 for supervised models. Adding more numerical features reduced recognition performance on all datasets. Augmenting numerical evidence with deterministic semantic rendering partially recovered performance. Post-hoc fusion showed small improvement only on WISDM that did not replicate on other datasets. Jev's probabilities were not reliably calibrated for recognition.

Conclusion: Training-free sensor decisions depend not only on information available in the signal but also on whether the model can effectively use the representation through which that information is exposed. The sensor-to-model interface should be treated as part of model evaluation rather than as neutral preprocessing. General-purpose models like Jev cannot currently replace or complement trained HAR models for deterministic sensor signal interpretation.

Abstract: General-purpose models promise sensor-based decisions without training a task-specific classifier, which could reduce the dependence of Human Activity Recognition (HAR) on labeled data. Yet it remains unclear whether such models can directly interpret deterministic descriptions of physical sensor signals well enough to replace or complement trained HAR models. We study this question using Jev, a fixed general-purpose probabilistic decision model, on 1,800 class-balanced accelerometer windows from WISDM, UCI341, and PAMAP2. Jev receives no labeled examples, retrieval context, or HAR-specific parameter updates. We evaluate three deterministic sensor representations and compare 5,400 Jev decisions with a generative baseline and three supervised HAR models. Jev remains far below supervised recognition, with its strongest representation reaching macro-F1 of 0.038, 0.118, and 0.089 across the three datasets, compared with 0.686 to 0.907 for the supervised models. More numerical features do not improve Jev. Instead, they reduce recognition on all three datasets, while augmenting the same numerical evidence with a deterministic semantic rendering partially recovers performance, although the experiment does not isolate semantics from the accompanying serialization and redundancy changes. Jev is fast and inexpensive to query, but its probabilities are not reliably calibrated for recognition. A post-hoc fusion analysis finds a small improvement on WISDM that does not replicate on UCI341 or PAMAP2. These results show that training-free sensor decisions depend not only on the information available in the signal, but also on whether the model can use the representation through which that information is exposed. The sensor-to-model interface should therefore be treated as part of the model evaluation rather than as a neutral preprocessing step.

</details>

### [112] [Toward Generative Video Communication: A Dual-Stream Digital Transmission Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36565)
*Bingyan Xie,Longyu Zhou,Tianhao Liang,Yongpeng Wu,Zehui Xiong,Wenjun Zhang,Tony Q. S. Quek*

Main category: cs.MM

TL;DR: This paper proposes a dual-stream digital generative video communication (DGVC) framework that combines traditional digital transmission with generative AI to enable bandwidth-efficient, personalized video delivery over wireless networks. The framework uses a traditional link for visual references and a generative link for semantic/perceptual information, with case studies demonstrating quality benefits under wireless fading conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to address bandwidth constraints in wireless transmission while enabling personalized content delivery. Generative video communication offers a solution by reducing bandwidth requirements while maintaining perceptual quality.

Method: The proposed DGVC framework uses a dual-stream approach: (1) Traditional digital link for source-grounded visual references, and (2) Generative link for compact semantic and perceptual information. The paper discusses three bandwidth-dependent operating regimes and key technologies including coordination, synchronization, reliability, and latency control mechanisms. A practical case study was conducted under wireless fading channels.

Result: The case study demonstrates perceptual and temporal-quality benefits of DGVC under wireless fading channels, showing the framework's effectiveness in challenging wireless environments.

Conclusion: The paper presents a novel DGVC framework that combines traditional digital transmission with generative AI to enable bandwidth-efficient video communication. It addresses practical challenges of wireless fading channels and outlines future research directions for generative video communication. The framework shows promise for personalized content delivery in bandwidth-constrained environments.

Abstract: Generative video communication has shown promise for bandwidth-constrained wireless transmission and has the potential to support personalized content delivery. In this article, we propose a dual-stream digital generative video communication (DGVC) framework that integrates a traditional digital link with a generative link. The traditional link provides source-grounded visual references, while the generative link conveys compact semantic and perceptual information for receiver-side generation. We further discuss three bandwidth-dependent operating regimes and key technologies for dual-stream coordination, synchronization, reliability, and latency control. A practical case study demonstrates the perceptual and temporal-quality benefits of DGVC under wireless fading channels. Finally, we discuss open challenges and future research directions for generative video communication.

</details>


<div id='cs.SD'></div>

# cs.SD [[Back]](#toc)

### [113] [Estimation of Room Impulse Responses from Handclaps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35839)
*Shih-Yu Lai,Kyung Yun Lee,Nils Meyer-Kahlen,Eloi Moliner,Bing-Yu Chen,Vesa Välimäki*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: A deep neural network is trained to estimate room impulse responses (RIRs) directly from handclap recordings without needing to know the excitation signal, using an anechoic handclap dataset of 2,540 claps from 17 participants.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Handclaps provide equipment-free excitation for room acoustics measurement, but their unknown and variable source waveform makes RIR estimation challenging. Existing methods like windowing the direct sound are insufficient for accurate RIR estimation.

Method: 1) Created an anechoic handclap dataset with 2,540 claps from 17 participants to capture natural variability. 2) Established baseline performance using regularized deconvolution with known excitation. 3) Proposed a deep neural network trained with supervised regression to estimate RIRs directly from handclaps without knowing the excitation signal.

Result: The neural regressor significantly outperforms windowing-based baselines on a controlled synthetic benchmark across all instrumental metrics. On real acoustic spaces, inferred RIR spectra are consistent across different handclap measurements taken in the same room location.

Conclusion: It is feasible to directly estimate room impulse responses from natural handclaps without requiring knowledge of the excitation signal, enabling equipment-free room acoustics measurement.

Abstract: Handclaps provide an equipment-free excitation for room acoustics, but their unknown and variable source waveform makes room impulse response (RIR) estimation challenging. In this work, we investigate whether RIRs can be estimated directly from handclaps. To this end, we introduce an anechoic handclap dataset containing 2,540 claps from 17 participants, designed to capture variability across natural claps and different hand configurations. We first establish the performance attainable when the excitation clap is known using regularized deconvolution, and show that approximating the unknown excitation by windowing the direct sound from the reverberant recording is insufficient. To estimate the RIR without a known excitation, we propose using the anechoic handclap recordings to train a deep neural network with a supervised regression objective. Evaluated on a controlled synthetic benchmark, the proposed neural regressor significantly outperforms windowing-based baselines across all instrumental metrics. Furthermore, we test the proposed method on handclap recordings measured in real acoustic spaces, showing that the inferred RIR spectra are consistent across different handclap measurements taken in the same room location. These results showcase the feasibility of directly estimating RIRs from natural handclaps without requiring knowledge of the excitation signal.

</details>


### [114] [HEAR: Real Voices, Real Bias: A Large-Scale Human-Recorded, Demographically Diverse Benchmark for Audio Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35952)
*Shen Yan,Duc Le,Irina-Elena Veliche*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: HEAR is a large-scale benchmark with 87k human audio samples from 843 participants for evaluating bias in Audio-LLMs. It is the first voice benchmark grounded in authentic human speech, supporting both MCQA and open-ended long-form evaluation tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: This research addresses the lack of ecologically valid benchmarks for evaluating voice-conditioned bias in Audio-LLMs. Existing benchmarks likely lack real human speech data, and there is a need to understand how voice characteristics affect model behavior across different demographic groups.

Method: The researchers created HEAR with 87k audio samples from 843 demographically diverse participants. They evaluated model behavior using both Multiple Choice Question Answering (MCQA) and open-ended long-form tasks across real-time speech-to-speech and speech-to-text architectures.

Result: Voice-conditioned bias is a model-specific property, meaning different models exhibit different biases. Personalization instructions consistently exacerbate demographic disparities. Voice bias is found to be a controllable model characteristic.

Conclusion: The study establishes that voice bias can be measured and controlled, providing a foundational framework for future bias mitigation and evaluation in Audio-LLM development.

Abstract: We introduce HEAR (Human-recorded Evaluation of Audio-LLM bias by Real speakers), a large-scale, ecologically valid benchmark comprising 87k real human audio samples from 843 demographically diverse participants. HEAR enables comprehensive evaluation through Multiple Choice Question Answering (MCQA) and open-ended long-form tasks. To our knowledge, this is the first large-scale voice benchmark grounded entirely in authentic human speech.
  We evaluate model behavior across both real-time speech-to-speech and speech-to-text architectures. Our results reveal that voice-conditioned bias is a model-specific property. Furthermore, we demonstrate that personalization instructions consistently exacerbate demographic disparities. Our findings establish that voice bias is a controllable model characteristic, providing a foundational framework for future bias mitigation and evaluation in Audio-LLM development.

</details>


### [115] [Enabling Immersive Audio-Visual Experience from Any Video](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36295)
*Zitong Lan,Mutian Tong,Jiatao Gu,Mingmin Zhao*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: OmniDream is a training-free framework that transforms silent monocular videos into immersive 360° audiovisual experiences with spatially consistent audio, enabling viewers to freely look around while sounds remain aligned with the visual scene.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current videos have narrow field of view and lack spatial audio, limiting immersion. While video generation models can expand perspective videos to panoramic ones, they fail to provide corresponding spatial soundscapes, leaving visual worlds incomplete without spatially consistent audio.

Method: OmniDream uses an object-centric audio representation that disentangles each sound source's intrinsic audio content from its scene-dependent acoustic effects. This enables independent audio generation, physics-based simulation of propagation effects, and flexible spatial audio rendering.

Result: Experiments demonstrate improved audio-visual alignment, spatial correctness, and perceptual immersiveness over baseline methods.

Conclusion: OmniDream successfully transforms silent monocular videos into immersive audiovisual experiences where viewers can freely look around while sounds remain spatially aligned with the visual scene, without requiring any training.

Abstract: Most videos capture only a narrow field of view and provide no spatial audio, limiting the sense of immersion they can provide. Recent video generation models can expand perspective videos into panoramic ones, but do not provide the corresponding spatial soundscape. Without spatially consistent audio, these expanded visual worlds remain incomplete. This paper presents OmniDream, a training-free framework that transforms a silent monocular video into an immersive audiovisual experience, where viewers can freely look around while sounds remain spatially aligned with the visual scene. At the core of OmniDream is an object-centric audio representation that disentangles each sound source's intrinsic audio content from its scene-dependent acoustic effects, enabling independent audio generation, physics-based simulation of propagation effects, and flexible spatial audio rendering. Experiments show improved audio-visual alignment, spatial correctness, and perceptual immersiveness over baselines. Examples are available on https://huggingface.co/spaces/CuriousAlien000/spatial-audio-360-demo

</details>


### [116] [Distill Locally, Schedule Globally: Flow Maps for Few-Step Text-to-Speech](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36324)
*Yentl Collin,Evan Dufraisse,Amr Mohamed,Amine Khelif Khelif,Dani Bouch,Guokan Shang*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: Local Flow-Map Distillation (LFMD) enables high-quality few-step text-to-speech synthesis by avoiding teacher trajectory integration during training and using derived sampling schedules. The method achieves 1.80% WER with 1-NFE, nearly matching the 32-NFE teacher's 1.76% WER on Seed-TTS.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Flow-matching TTS models achieve high synthesis quality but require many neural function evaluations (NFEs) to integrate their generative trajectories. Existing few-step flow-map distillation approaches construct targets from numerically integrated teacher trajectories, creating a trade-off between target accuracy and training cost. The goal is to reduce NFEs while maintaining synthesis quality.

Method: Local Flow-Map Distillation (LFMD) adapts Eulerian Map Distillation to conditional TTS by avoiding teacher trajectory integration during target construction. For inference, TD-DP sampling schedule is derived from teacher dynamics and consistency of learned maps, supporting multiple NFE budgets from a single cost graph. For the 1-NFE regime, alignment-aware temporal self-distillation using soft-DTW refines the model.

Result: On Seed-TTS and LibriSpeech-PC datasets, LFMD improves low-NFE synthesis over matched integral-distillation baselines. On Seed-TTS, the refined student achieves 1.80% WER with 1-NFE, compared to 1.76% for its 32-NFE teacher—nearly matching quality with 32x fewer NFEs.

Conclusion: LFMD successfully enables high-quality few-step TTS synthesis. The 1-NFE student achieves 1.80% WER, nearly matching the 32-NFE teacher's 1.76% WER, representing a 32x reduction in computational cost with minimal quality degradation. The approach demonstrates that avoiding teacher trajectory integration during training while using derived sampling schedules can effectively bridge the gap between few-step generation and full-step flow matching.

Abstract: Flow-matching text-to-speech (TTS) models achieve high synthesis quality but require many neural function evaluations (NFEs) to integrate their generative trajectories. Recent few-step flow-map distillation approaches for TTS construct targets from numerically integrated teacher trajectories, creating a trade-off between target accuracy and training cost. We propose Local Flow-Map Distillation (LFMD), which adapts Eulerian Map Distillation to conditional TTS and avoids teacher trajectory integration during target construction. For inference, we derive a sampling schedule (TD-DP) from teacher dynamics and consistency of the learned maps, with a single cost graph supporting multiple NFE budgets without external audio-metric evaluation. Because scheduling offers no flexibility at one NFE, we refine this regime with alignment-aware temporal self-distillation using soft-DTW. Across Seed-TTS and LibriSpeech-PC, LFMD improves low-NFE synthesis over a matched integral-distillation baseline. On Seed-TTS, the refined student reaches 1.80% WER with 1-NFE, compared with 1.76% for its 32-NFE teacher.

</details>


### [117] [Prediction-Layer Branch Calibration for Multimodal Sentiment Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37100)
*Yulin Sun,Kele Xu,Yong Dou*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: BC-MLF introduces explicit prediction-layer branch allocation for multimodal sentiment analysis using two novel components: Branch-Calibrated Task Head (BCHead) for sample-adaptive fusion of predictions, and Fusion Token Contrastive Learning (FTCL) for sentiment-aware regularization of fusion tokens. The method improves over baseline without modifying the fusion backbone.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current language-model-based fusion methods in multimodal sentiment analysis leave prediction-layer branch allocation implicit, which limits model performance and interpretability.

Method: BC-MLF uses two components: (1) FTCL organizes mean-pooled fusion-token representations by continuous sentiment affinity using contrastive learning; (2) BCHead combines fusion, text, and audiovisual predictions through a lightweight sample-adaptive constrained mixture. The approach does not modify the fusion backbone.

Result: BC-MLF consistently improves the reproduced DeepMLF baseline and achieves state-of-the-art results on CMU-MOSEI and CH-SIMS datasets across both classification and regression metrics. Ablations confirm that sample-adaptive prediction-layer branch allocation outperforms static branch aggregation.

Conclusion: Explicitly modeling prediction-layer branch allocation with sample-adaptive mechanisms significantly improves multimodal sentiment analysis. The method is effective across multiple datasets and evaluation metrics while maintaining compatibility with existing fusion backbones.

Abstract: Multimodal sentiment analysis integrates textual, acoustic and visual cues, yet current language-model-based fusion methods typically leave prediction-layer branch allocation implicit. We introduce Branch-Calibrated Multimodal Language Fusion (BC-MLF), which explicitly models prediction-layer branch allocation through a Branch-Calibrated Task Head (BCHead), complemented by Fusion Token Contrastive Learning (FTCL) for sentiment-aware fusion-token regularization. FTCL organizes mean-pooled fusion-token representations according to continuous sentiment affinity, while BCHead combines fusion, text and audiovisual predictions through a lightweight sample-adaptive constrained mixture. Without modifying the fusion backbone, BC-MLF consistently improves the reproduced DeepMLF baseline and achieves the strongest results among the compared methods on CMU-MOSEI and CH-SIMS across classification and regression metrics. The controlled ablations show that sample-adaptive prediction-layer branch allocation consistently outperforms static branch aggregation. Code is available at https://github.com/sunyulin0421/BC-MLF.

</details>


### [118] [Trigger Sound Suppression for Misophonia](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36351)
*Vaishnavi Vidyasagar,Jasmine Zhang,Mahima Uliyar,Seunghyun Oh,Emily Catherine Gates,Mark Zachary Rosenthal,Shyamnath Gollakota*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper presents a neural trigger sound suppression system for misophonia, selectively removing specific trigger sounds while preserving other audio content. The system uses streaming dual-path networks processing 6ms audio chunks and was validated in a listening study with 30 misophonia sufferers, showing significant reductions in distress and arousal.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Misophonia affects 5-20% of the population but current treatments (therapy, earplugs, noise cancellation) are inadequate - therapy helps only a minority, and existing solutions silence all sounds rather than selectively removing triggers.

Method: Curated dataset covering 10 most common trigger classes; streaming dual-path networks operating on 6 ms audio chunks; explored both one-hot and multi-hot-conditioned models to suppress 1-3 triggers from the acoustic scene; validated with listening study involving 30 adults with clinically elevated misophonia impairment.

Result: Participants reported significantly lower distress and arousal, and improved valence, for suppressed audio compared to unsuppressed audio.

Conclusion: Neural trigger sound suppression shows promise as an effective treatment for misophonia, offering selective removal of trigger sounds while preserving other audio, potentially improving quality of life for sufferers.

Abstract: Misophonia, a disorder of decreased tolerance to specific sounds, affects 5-20% of the population, yet sufferers have no good options: therapy helps a minority, and earplugs or noise cancellation silence everything. We present a study for neural trigger sound suppression for misophonia, selectively removing trigger sounds. We curate a dataset covering the 10 most common trigger classes. Using streaming dual-path networks operating on 6 ms audio chunks, we explore both one-hot and multi-hot-conditioned models that suppress 1-3 triggers from the acoustic scene. We validate our model outputs in a listening study with 30 adults with clinically elevated misophonia impairment. Participants reported significantly lower distress and arousal, and improved valence, for suppressed audio.

</details>


### [119] [UDSS-BWE: Uncertainty- and Decision-Science Inspired Swin BandWidth Extension](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36379)
*Tarikul Islam Tamiti,Sajid Fardin Dipto,David Vergano,Luke Baja-Ricketts,Anomadarshi Barua*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: UDSS-BWE introduces decision-science and uncertainty-aware discriminators for bandwidth extension, addressing the limitation of standard objectives that smooth away rare high-frequency transients. The framework uses Swin-based generators with a dual-stream architecture and achieves 3.89x parameter reduction while improving perceptual quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard risk-neutral objectives in bandwidth extension tend to smooth away rare high-frequency (HF) transients, which are the most perceptual distortions. The authors seek solutions in risk-sensitive and uncertainty-aware decision science to address this gap.

Method: UDSS-BWE uses five decision-science and uncertainty-aware discriminators: CVaRD (tail pooling to amplify HF artifacts), CCD (primal-dual augmented Lagrangian to prevent HF overboost), MCUD (learnable utility over spectral flatness/centroid/rolloff), EDD (epistemic uncertainty), and DROD (entropic KL-DRO aggregation). The generator uses a Swin-based architecture with a lightweight dual-stream shifted-window backbone and learnable lattice coupling for controlled cross-stream exchange.

Result: UDSS-BWE achieves better perceptual quality with 3.89x fewer parameters (72M vs. 18.5M) over two English and French datasets under clean and noisy conditions.

Conclusion: This work demonstrates that multi-disciplinary decision-science-inspired and uncertainty theories can be successfully used to design efficient discriminators for producing more nuanced audios, establishing a new baseline in the BWE task.

Abstract: Bandwidth extension (BWE) is fundamentally localized: the most perceptual distortions are not average-case distortions, but rare high-frequency (HF) transients that standard, risk-neutral objectives tend to smooth away. To close this gap, we seek solutions in the risk-sensitive and uncertainty-aware decision science rules and present UDSS-BWE, which introduces five decision-science and uncertainty-aware discriminators: CVaRD (does tail pooling to amplify HF artifacts), CCD (a primal-dual augmented Lagrangian to prevent HF overboost), MCUD (a learnable utility over spectral flatness/ centroid/ rolloff), EDD (captures epistemic uncertainty), and DROD (captures entropic KL-DRO aggregation). UDSS-BWE is also designed as a complex valued adversarial BWE framework that uses Swin-based generators, a lightweight dual-stream shifted-window backbone, to capture local and long-range structure efficiently, while learnable lattice coupling provides controlled cross-stream exchange. UDSS-BWE is optimized extensively and achieves better perceptual quality with 3.89x fewer parameters (72M vs.18.5M) over two English and French datasets under clean and noisy conditions. To the best of our knowledge, this work shows how multi disciplinary decision-science-inspired and uncertainty theories can be successfully used to design efficient discriminators for producing more nuanced audios, establishing a new baseline in the BWE task.

</details>


### [120] [Emergent Tonal Structure in Learned Chord Embeddings and Its Relation to Tonal Tension](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36460)
*Maral Ebrahimzadeh,Gilberto Bernardes,Sebastian Stober*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether skip-gram chord embeddings can recover tonal structure and serve as a basis for analyzing tonal tension in Western tonal music. Using chord sequences with and without transposition-based augmentation, the study evaluates learned spaces from geometric, functional, and tension-related perspectives.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While several tonal pitch spaces and computational models exist for analyzing tonal structure in Western tonal music, data-driven methods like skip-gram have been used to learn chord embeddings but their ability to recover tonal structure and its relation to tonal tension remains underexplored.

Method: Using chord sequences with and without transposition-based augmentation, the researchers evaluate the learned spaces from geometric, functional, and tension-related perspectives. They derive embedding-based measures from chord-to-key distance and contextual chord-distance relations.

Result: Augmented embeddings exhibit strong transposition equivariance, recover a clear circle-of-fifths structure, and support interpretable shifts between key-related regions. Embedding-based measures capture meaningful aspects of tonal tension through correspondence with matched tonal measures and moderate alignment with human tension profiles. Transposition-based augmentation generally improves stability, tonal coherence, and interpretability.

Conclusion: Skip-gram chord embeddings, particularly with transposition-based augmentation, can effectively recover tonal structure and provide a useful basis for analyzing structural aspects of tonal tension in Western tonal music.

Abstract: Several tonal pitch spaces and computational models have been proposed to analyze tonal structure in Western tonal music, many of them grounded in principles from music theory and used to support tonal analysis with important implications for tonal tension. In parallel, data-driven methods such as skip-gram have been used to learn chord embeddings from symbolic corpora, but their ability to recover tonal structure and its relation to tonal tension remains underexplored. In this work, we investigate how skip-gram chord embeddings reflect tonal structure and whether they provide a useful basis for analyzing structural aspects of tonal tension. Using chord sequences with and without transposition-based augmentation, we evaluate the learned spaces from geometric, functional, and tension-related perspectives. We show that augmented embeddings exhibit strong transposition equivariance, recover a clear circle-of-fifths structure, and support interpretable shifts between key-related regions of the learned space. We then derive embedding-based measures from chord-to-key distance and contextual chord-distance relations, and show that they capture meaningful aspects of tonal tension structure through correspondence with matched tonal measures and moderate alignment with human tension profiles. Across analyses, transposition-based augmentation generally improves the stability, tonal coherence, and interpretability of the learned space.

</details>


### [121] [InterBias-SV: Compound Conditions in Speaker Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36500)
*Kamel Kamel,Hridoy Sankar Dutta,Keshav Sood,Sunil Aryal*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: InterBias-SV is a benchmark for evaluating speaker verification systems under compound adverse conditions (noise, channel distortion, speech changes). It provides 4,068 scored records across 17 experiments, 12 encoder labels, and 6 corpora (12 million trial evaluations). Mean EER contrasts for speaker-trained encoders are +0.0026, +0.0088, and +0.0024, but additivity is not established. The paper also addresses interpretation problems: near-chance EER difficulty and demographic gap ratio instability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current speaker verification evaluation examines noise, channel distortion, and speech changes separately, but real-world systems face these in combination. This paper addresses whether their effects add up, which is critical for understanding system performance in realistic deployment scenarios.

Method: Four-term comparison framework: joint error, two marginal errors, and common reference. Analyzed 12 million trial evaluations across 17 experiments, 12 encoder labels, and 6 speech corpora. Three experiment families contained terms for computing additive contrasts.

Result: Mean EER contrasts for speaker-trained encoders: +0.0026, +0.0088, +0.0024. Descriptive averages show small effects but do not establish equivalence to additivity. Limitations include unverified trial matching, checkpoint identity, and condition metadata. Near-chance EER makes predictions difficult to interpret, and demographic gap ratios are unstable when clean reference is near zero.

Conclusion: The benchmark provides condition definitions, analysis scripts, and requirements for interpretable compound-condition comparisons. However, additivity is not confirmed—trial matching and metadata remain unverified. Interpretation warnings: chance performance is not a hard EER ceiling, correlation doesn't identify saturation mechanism, and absolute gaps are more reliable than ratios when clean reference approaches zero.

Abstract: Speaker verification systems encounter combinations of noise, channel distortion, and changes in speech. Evaluating each condition separately does not establish whether their effects add. InterBias-SV organises this question around a four-term comparison: joint error, two marginal errors, and a common reference. Its results artefact contains 4,068 scored records across 17 experiments, 12 encoder labels, and six speech corpora, totalling 12 million trial evaluations. Three experiment families contain the same-corpus terms needed to compute additive contrasts. For labels assigned to speaker-trained encoders, their mean contrasts are +0.0026, +0.0088, and +0.0024 in equal error rate (EER), with larger variation across settings. These descriptive averages do not establish equivalence to additivity: trial matching, checkpoint identity, and parts of the condition metadata remain unverified. We also examine two interpretation problems. Near-chance EER can make additive predictions difficult to interpret, but chance performance is not a hard EER ceiling, and correlation with the prediction does not identify a saturation mechanism. Ratios of demographic gaps are unstable when their clean reference is near zero; absolute gaps provide a more direct summary. The benchmark provides condition definitions, analysis scripts, and explicit requirements for interpretable compound-condition comparisons, while separating recomputable summaries from claims that require further experimental validation.

</details>


### [122] [Long-Term Memory-Guided Enhancement for Target Perception in Audio-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36577)
*Zhenhong Zhou,Xuanyue Zhao,Youji Liu,Yuanhe Zhang,Xiaoyu Ma,Lianyu Hu,Yang Liu*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper proposes Long-Term Memory-Guided Audio Enhancement (LTM-AE), a training-free method that improves Audio Large Language Models (ALLMs) performance in noisy real-world environments by using clean reference recordings as long-term memory to guide audio token enhancement through interpolation with original tokens.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Audio large language models can reason about audio content but their capabilities degrade significantly in real-world environments with background noise and competing sound sources. The authors draw inspiration from human long-term memory in selective listening to address this problem.

Method: LTM-AE works by: (1) extracting representations from hidden states of clean reference recordings as long-term memory for each category; (2) reconstructing incoming audio tokens in the selected category's long-term memory; (3) interpolating reconstructions with original tokens before language backbone decoding. All ALLM parameters remain fixed. For speech, an additional learned token-level gate is used.

Result: Across 20 sound categories and 3 ALLMs, LTM-AE strengthens responses to specified targets amid 3 interfering sources. Accuracy gains range from 29.53 to 46.15 percentage points over raw mixtures. For speech recovery, Qwen2-Audio's word error rate drops from 23.07% to 14.77%.

Conclusion: This work demonstrates that principles of human long-term memory can be applied to enhance ALLMs for real-world listening scenarios without requiring model retraining. The method provides substantial improvements in selective target perception across various sound categories.

Abstract: Audio large language models (ALLMs) can reason about the content of audio recordings to perform complex tasks. However, these capabilities usually collapse in real-world environments when background noise and competing sources mix the target sound. Inspired by long-term memory in human listening, we propose Long-Term Memory-Guided Audio Enhancement (LTM-AE) to improve selective target perception by refining the audio representations of ALLMs without training. LTM-AE extracts representations in hidden states from separate clean reference recordings as long-term memory for each category, guiding enhancement toward a user-specified listening target. We reconstruct incoming audio tokens in the selected category long-term memory and interpolate the reconstructions with the original tokens before language backbone decoding. This interpolation controls the influence of stored auditory experience while keeping all ALLM parameters fixed. Diagnostic readouts across twenty sound categories and three ALLMs show that LTM-AE strengthens responses to a specified target amid three interfering sources. Averaged over constrained and free-form classification, accuracy gains over raw mixtures range from 29.53 to 46.15 percentage points across multiple open source models. For speech content recovery, LTM-AE with an additional learned token-level gate reduces Qwen2-Audio's word error rate from 23.07% to 14.77%. This work takes an initial step toward using principles of human long-term memory to enhance ALLMs for real-world listening. Our code is available at https://github.com/aynlp/ltm-audio-code

</details>


### [123] [Reconstructing the Vocal Tract with Differentiable Acoustic Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36737)
*Eric Ming Chen,Jin Woo Lee,Vincent Sitzmann*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: A differentiable and GPU-accelerated acoustic simulator for the vocal tract that can solve the inverse problem of reconstructing vocal tract geometry from speech, with applications in linguistics and medical imaging.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The vocal tract filters voice to create speech, but reconstructing the shape of the vocal tract from sound alone is challenging due to the non-convex inverse mapping between geometry and sound.

Method: Three technical contributions: (1) frequency domain formulation of fluid dynamics that is 70x more GPU parallelizable than finite differences, (2) differentiable turbulence model for synthesizing consonants, (3) neural network parameterization of geometry (similar to INRs/neural fields) to accelerate convergence and escape local minima.

Result: The simulator successfully: (1) performs self-supervised autoencoding of vocal tract shapes across 11 languages, (2) reconstructs moving vocal tracts from speech alone by coupling with a generative MRI model without paired data.

Conclusion: The differentiable simulator enables novel linguistics and medical imaging applications by integrating with deep learning pipelines, demonstrating that gradient descent can solve the non-convex inverse problem with appropriate technical contributions.

Abstract: The vocal tract is the region of the human body responsible for filtering one's voice to create speech. In this paper, we present a differentiable and GPU accelerated acoustic simulator for the vocal tract. The differentiable simulator synthesizes speech by propagating sound along an acoustic tube model of the vocal tract, and via its gradients, can solve the inverse problem: reconstructing the shape of the vocal tract solely from the sound it produces. Although the inverse mapping between geometry and sound is notoriously non-convex, we discover that gradient descent succeeds with three technical contributions: (1) we design a frequency domain formulation of the vocal tract's fluid dynamics that is 70x more GPU parallelizable than finite differences in time, (2) we integrate a differentiable model for turbulence to synthesize consonants, and (3) similar to prior work in implicit neural representations (INRs) and neural fields, we find that parameterizing the geometry with a neural network accelerates convergence and escapes local minima that trap discrete representations. Because the simulator is differentiable, it is readily integrated with other deep learning pipelines to enable novel linguistics and medical imaging applications. (1) We demonstrate self-supervised autoencoding of vocal tract shapes across 11 languages, and (2) we couple our simulator with a generative model of MRI (magnetic resonance imaging) images to reconstruct one's moving vocal tract from only their speech without paired data.

</details>


### [124] [When Capabilities Fail to Compose: Diagnosing the Compositionality Gap in Large Audio-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36921)
*Chien-Feng Liu,Chih-Kai Yang,Bo-Han Feng,Yu-Hsuan Li Liang,Hung-yi Lee,Cheng-Fu Chou*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether large audio-language models (LALMs) can reliably compose multiple capabilities (audio-attribute recognition, segment selection, and downstream tasks like ASR/QA). Through controlled tests with two-utterance inputs across four open-source models, the study finds severe compositional failures: QA accuracy drops in 39/40 settings (avg 26.7 pp) and ASR WER increases in 39/40 settings (avg 28.5 pp), revealing a systematic gap between individual capabilities and their reliable composition.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large audio-language models perform strongly on individual audio tasks, but whether these capabilities can be reliably composed remains underexplored. This study aims to diagnose and quantify the capability composition gap in LALMs.

Method: Controlled diagnostic study requiring models to integrate audio-attribute recognition, cue-conditioned segment selection, and downstream ASR or question answering. Uses two-utterance inputs with distinct acoustic cues (environmental sound, gender, emotion) across downstream tasks (ASR, Math QA, Factual QA). Probes failures through output format, positional preference, and chain-of-thought analyses. Four open-source LALMs evaluated.

Result: Compositional QA accuracy decreases in 39 of 40 model-task-cue settings, with an average drop of 26.7 percentage points. ASR exhibits similar degradation with WER increasing in 39 of 40 settings by an average of 28.5 percentage points. Degradation magnitude varies across models, cue types, and cue salience.

Conclusion: There exists a systematic gap between possessing individual audio capabilities and reliably composing them in LALMs. The models fail to reliably integrate multiple capabilities even when they demonstrate strong performance on individual tasks.

Abstract: Large audio-language models (LALMs) perform strongly on individual audio tasks, but whether these capabilities can be reliably composed remains underexplored. We conduct a controlled diagnostic study of capability composition in LALMs, requiring models to integrate audio-attribute recognition, cue-conditioned segment selection, and downstream ASR or question answering. We construct two-utterance inputs with distinct acoustic cues to evaluate composition across environmental sound, gender, and emotion cues, with ASR, Math QA, and Factual QA as downstream tasks. Across four open-source LALMs, compositional QA accuracy decreases in 39 of 40 model-task-cue settings, by an average of 26.7 percentage points. ASR exhibits a similarly consistent degradation, with WER increasing in 39 of 40 settings by an average of 28.5 percentage points, while the magnitude of degradation varies across models, cue types, and cue salience. We further probe these failures through output format, positional preference, and chain-of-thought (CoT) analyses. Our study reveals a systematic gap between possessing individual audio capabilities and reliably composing them.

</details>


### [125] [Interpreting and Evaluating Dynamic-Rate Speech Codec Boundaries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36951)
*Han Wang,Jiaqi Li,Yingda Shen,Yuxiang Wang,Zhizheng Wu*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper investigates what boundaries encode in dynamic-frame-rate neural speech codecs and whether interpretable boundaries improve speech reconstruction. The study finds that boundary meaning depends on the speech representation: for ASR-oriented encoders, shallow layers capture phonetic/voicing features while deeper layers capture syllable/subword structure. Syllable-based boundaries yield better reconstruction than phoneme-based or uniform approaches for semantically rich representations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dynamic-frame-rate neural speech codecs use variable-duration tokens with boundaries being part of the representation itself. However, it was unclear what these boundaries encode and whether interpretable boundaries have practical utility for neural speech reconstruction. This work addresses this gap by combining boundary interpretation with controlled reconstruction analysis.

Method: The study compared predicted boundaries with linguistic and acoustic references using two approaches: 1) Analysis of SenseVoice and Whisper encoders across different layers; 2) Comparison of six dynamic-frame-rate algorithms against a uniform (fixed-frame-rate) baseline, including frame-rate-matched boundary tests comparing syllable-derived vs phoneme-derived partitions.

Result: For ASR-oriented encoders (SenseVoice, Whisper), shallow layers emphasize phonetic, voicing, and acoustic transitions while deeper layers shift toward syllable and subword structure. Higher-level linguistic alignment correlates with lower pooling distortion and better reconstruction from semantic tokens. Syllable-derived partition improves over both Uniform and Similarity methods, while phoneme-derived partition shows no improvement over Uniform.

Conclusion: For semantically rich speech representations, useful codec boundaries are best understood as allocation decisions organized around the syllable scale. This suggests that syllable-level segmentation is optimal for dynamic-frame-rate speech coding.

Abstract: Dynamic-frame-rate neural speech codecs replace a uniform frame grid with variable-duration tokens, making boundary placement part of the representation itself. Yet it is unclear what these boundaries encode and whether interpretable boundaries are also useful for neural speech reconstruction. This work combines boundary interpretation and controlled reconstruction analysis by comparing predicted boundaries with linguistic and acoustic references. We find that boundary meaning depends on the underlying speech representation. For the ASR-oriented SenseVoice and Whisper encoders, shallow layers emphasize phonetic, voicing, and acoustic transitions, whereas deeper layers shift toward syllable and subword structure. In a comparison of six dynamic-frame-rate algorithms and a uniform (fixed-frame-rate) baseline, higher-level linguistic alignment is associated with lower pooling distortion and better reconstruction from semantic tokens. Frame-rate-matched boundary tests make the distinction concrete: a syllable-derived partition improves over both Uniform and Similarity, while a phoneme-derived partition does not improve over Uniform. We infer that for semantically rich speech representations, useful codec boundaries are best understood as allocation decisions organized around the syllable scale.

</details>


### [126] [RVQ Position Aware Speculative Decoding for On Device Text to Speech](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37007)
*Berkin Durmus,Eduardo Pacheco,Zach Nagengast,Atila Orhon*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: A speculative decoding technique (RVQ position aware) that accelerates autoregressive decoding in Qwen3-TTS by predicting multiple RVQ tokens per model call, reducing inference latency from 200 to 88 sequential model calls per second while maintaining WER parity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autoregressive decoding with Transformer models is memory bandwidth bound at single stream inference, which is the typical deployment regime for on-device TTS. Qwen3-TTS requires over 200 sequential model calls per second for real-time streaming, dominated by the MultiCodeDecoder inner loop that emits 15 RVQ codes per 80ms audio frame.

Method: Proposed RVQ position aware speculative decoding for the MultiCodeDecoder, which adds only 5×10^-4 percent parameters and incurs 10-20% per round speculation/verification overhead. The scheme is distributionally lossless under deployed top-k sampling.

Result: Achieved 2.47 accepted tokens per model call, reducing real-time synthesis from 200 to 88 sequential model calls per second. Delivered 2 to 2.2x speedup for RVQ token generation on iPhone and Apple Silicon Mac devices.

Conclusion: RVQ position aware speculative decoding provides significant speedup (2-2.2x) for on-device TTS inference while maintaining WER (Word Error Rate) parity with the original system, making real-time streaming feasible on resource-constrained devices.

Abstract: Autoregressive decoding (AR) with Transformer models is memory bandwidth bound at single stream inference, the typical deployment regime for on device text to speech (TTS). Real time streaming with Qwen3-TTS requires more than 200 sequential model calls per second, dominated by the inner loop MultiCodeDecoder that emits the 15 residual vector quantization (RVQ) codes per 80 ms audio frame. We propose RVQ position aware speculative decoding for the MultiCodeDecoder, attaining 2.47 accepted tokens per model call at $5\times10^{-4}$ percent added parameters and 10 to 20 percent per round speculation/verification overhead, reducing real time synthesis from 200 to 88 sequential model calls per second. The scheme is distributionally lossless under the deployed top-k sampling, and WER parity with the original system is consistent with this guarantee. We deliver 2 to 2.2x speedup for RVQ token generation with Qwen3-TTS 0.6B on recent iPhone and Apple Silicon Mac devices.

</details>


### [127] [ReDimNet2+: Multi-Corpus Data Scaling for Robust Speaker Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37014)
*Kirill Borodin,Vasilii Kudryavtsev,Maxim Maslov,Grach Mkrtchian*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: ReDimNet2+ is a speaker verification system that scales training of a compact backbone across seven public corpora (63,934 speakers, ~8,675 hours). It uses multi-corpus training, large-margin fine-tuning (LMFT), and graph-based retrieval reranking to achieve state-of-the-art performance, reducing VoxCeleb1 EER from 2.42% to 0.82% and achieving 0.35% EER on VoxCeleb1-O.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automatic speaker verification must remain reliable across different devices, rooms, and compression pipelines (real-world variability). The analysis of VoxBlink2 subset revealed a shift in predicted spectral coloration, highlighting the need for codec and waveform augmentation to handle real-world conditions.

Method: 1) Multi-corpus training across seven public corpora (63,934 speakers, ~8,675 hours)
2) Large-margin fine-tuning (LMFT) for improved discrimination
3) Graph-based retrieval reranking for better ranking performance
4) Codec and waveform augmentation to address spectral coloration shifts
5) Random 4-second evaluation windows for all models

Result: 1) Pooled VoxCeleb1 EER: 2.42% → 0.82% (with LMFT)
2) 26-condition robustness stress-test EER: 7.21% → 1.99% (with LMFT)
3) VoxCeleb1-O: 0.35% EER (vs 0.787% for best WeSpeaker checkpoint)
4) VoxBlink2 retrieval subset: Pr@k improved from 0.7413 to 0.7687 with reranking

Conclusion: ReDimNet2+ with LMFT significantly outperforms prior methods, achieving 0.35% EER on VoxCeleb1-O and substantially improving robustness across 26 conditions. Graph-based reranking further improves retrieval performance. The combination of multi-corpus training, large-margin fine-tuning, and augmentation techniques effectively addresses device, room, and compression pipeline variability in speaker verification.

Abstract: Automatic speaker verification must remain reliable across devices, rooms, and compression pipelines. We present ReDimNet2+, which scales training of the compact ReDimNet2 backbone across seven public corpora (63,934 speakers, about 8,675 hours). Analysis of a VoxBlink2 subset reveals a shift in predicted spectral coloration, motivating codec and waveform augmentation alongside this multi-corpus training, large-margin fine-tuning (LMFT), and graph-based retrieval reranking. With random 4-second evaluation windows for all models, ReDimNet2+ LMFT reduces pooled VoxCeleb1 EER from 2.42% to 0.82% and a 26-condition robustness stress-test EER from 7.21% to 1.99%. Under this shared local protocol, it reaches 0.35% EER on VoxCeleb1-O versus 0.787% for the best evaluated WeSpeaker checkpoint. On a VoxBlink2 retrieval subset, reranking improves the final model's Pr@k from 0.7413 to 0.7687.

</details>


### [128] [RAWD-TTS: Ratio-Free Reward Alignment for Discrete-Diffusion Voice Cloning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37028)
*Maxim Maslov,Kirill Borodin,Vasilii Kudryavtsev,Nikita Vasiliev,Grach Mkrtchian*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: RAWD-TTS introduces a reward-based post-training method for zero-shot voice cloning that uses group-relative advantages to weight masked-token reconstruction, without requiring reverse-trajectory likelihoods or target audio. It achieves 24% relative WER reduction and improved speaker similarity on Russian voice cloning benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Zero-shot text-to-speech voice cloning requires accurate content preservation and speaker identity retention. However, supervised acoustic-token prediction doesn't directly optimize these waveform-level properties. While reward-based post-training addresses this mismatch, discrete diffusion poses challenges because token choices and reveal positions jointly define the sampling trajectory, complicating alignment between rewards and generation.

Method: RAWD-TTS (Ratio-free Advantage-Weighted Denoising) scores decoded samples with recognition rewards (ASR-based) and speaker rewards (WavLM embeddings), then uses group-relative advantages to weight masked-token reconstruction of those samples. This approach avoids needing reverse-trajectory likelihoods or target audio, unlike traditional reinforcement learning methods.

Result: On 500 Russian CV3-Eval voice-cloning prompts: Word error rate reduced from 3.18% to 2.42% (24.0% relative reduction) at the reward-selected checkpoint and to 2.58% (19.0% relative reduction) at the final checkpoint. WavLM speaker cosine similarity increased from 0.733 to 0.748 and 0.755 respectively. Controlled experiments characterized recognition-identity trade-offs and effects of corruption count, group composition, and weighting.

Conclusion: Joint alignment via reward-based post-training with group-relative advantages effectively improves both content accuracy and speaker similarity in zero-shot voice cloning. The method successfully balances recognition-identity trade-offs through controlled experimentation on key hyperparameters.

Abstract: Zero-shot text-to-speech synthesizes new utterances in a speaker's voice from a short reference recording. Voice cloning requires accurate content and preserved speaker identity, but supervised acoustic-token prediction does not directly optimize these waveform-level properties. Reward-based post-training addresses this mismatch, but in discrete diffusion, token choices and reveal positions jointly define the sampling trajectory, complicating alignment. We introduce RAWD-TTS (Ratio-free Advantage-Weighted Denoising), which scores decoded samples with recognition and speaker rewards and uses group-relative advantages to weight masked-token reconstruction of those samples, without reverse-trajectory likelihoods or target audio. On 500 Russian CV3-Eval voice-cloning prompts, joint alignment reduces word error rate from 3.18% to 2.42% at the reward-selected checkpoint (24.0% relative) and to 2.58% at the final checkpoint (19.0%), while WavLM speaker cosine rises from 0.733 to 0.748 and 0.755. Controlled experiments characterize recognition-identity trade-offs and the effects of corruption count, group composition, and weighting.

</details>


### [129] [Multichannel Audio Quality Assessment: Extending Pretrained Perceptual Models to Spatial Audio](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37116)
*Gouthaman KV,Shiv Gehlot,Vishnu Raj,Lars Villemoes,Arijit Biswas*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper addresses perceptual quality assessment for multichannel spatial audio (5.1-channel). The authors propose Feature-Band Group Attention (FGAtt), which adaptively fuses spatial groups at the feature level before perceptual processing. This approach effectively reuses pretrained perceptual knowledge for higher-channel-count audio, achieving state-of-the-art performance across five test sets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Subjective evaluation of spatial audio quality is costly and time-consuming. Existing perceptual models are typically trained for limited channel configurations and cannot be directly applied to higher-channel-count audio. There is a need to effectively reuse pretrained perceptual knowledge for multichannel spatial audio quality assessment.

Method: The authors study four levels of multichannel integration: (1) signal-level, (2) prediction-level, (3) latent-level, and (4) feature-level. They propose two learned approaches: (1) latent-level aggregation of spatial-group representations, and (2) feature-level Feature-Band Group Attention (FGAtt), which adaptively fuses spatial groups at the feature level before perceptual processing.

Result: Across five 5.1-channel test sets, FGAtt achieves the strongest overall performance, demonstrating the effectiveness of feature-level adaptation for reusing pretrained perceptual knowledge.

Conclusion: Feature-level adaptation (FGAtt) is the most effective approach for reusing pretrained perceptual knowledge in multichannel spatial audio quality assessment. This method successfully bridges the gap between models trained on limited channel configurations and higher-channel-count audio.

Abstract: Accurate perceptual quality assessment is essential for evaluating and optimizing spatial audio, where perceived quality depends on both signal fidelity and inter-channel spatial relationships. However, subjective evaluation is costly, while existing perceptual models are often trained for limited channel configurations and cannot be directly applied to higher-channel-count audio. This raises the question: how can pretrained perceptual knowledge be effectively reused for multichannel spatial audio? Using 5.1-channel audio, we study four levels of multichannel integration: signal, prediction, latent, and feature and propose two learned approaches: latent-level aggregation of spatial-group representations and the feature-level Feature-Band Group Attention (FGAtt), which adaptively fuses spatial groups at the feature level before perceptual processing. Across five 5.1-channel test sets, FGAtt achieves the strongest over- all performance, demonstrating the effectiveness of feature-level adaptation for reusing pretrained perceptual knowledge

</details>


### [130] [Bad: Taming the Bioacoustic Data Deluge with a Bat Acticity Detector](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37518)
*Stefano Ciapponi,Santiago Martinez Balvanera,Andrea Cesaretti,Elisabetta Farella,Kate E. Jones*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: A hardware-aware Bat Activity Detector (BAD) designed for microcontroller deployment (Silicon Labs EFM32PG26) that discriminates bat calls from acoustic confusers using a 14-layer deep learning model optimized for 8-bit integer precision, achieving 100% hardware offload with 17.2 KB Flash and 73.1 KB RAM.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Passive Acoustic Monitoring of bats generates massive ultrasonic datasets (>27 GB/night per node), straining edge storage and battery life. Legacy triggers fail against acoustic confusers, while deep learning models exceed microcontroller resource limits.

Method: Hardware-aware model design tailored for Silicon Labs EFM32PG22 (MVP) in 8-bit integer precision. The 14-layer model achieves 100% hardware offload with optimized preprocessing (74.00 ms for 76 frames) and inference (30.00 ms) for 100 ms audio clips at 192 kHz sampling rate.

Result: On spatially out-of-domain recordings under realistic low-prevalence regime (r_pos = 0.05), BAD achieves AUC-ROC of 0.9748, suppresses 99.4% of non-target noise frames while retaining 65.3% of bat calls - delivering >33x precision gain over classical Goertzel baselines.

Conclusion: The proposed BAD system successfully enables deep learning-based bat detection on resource-constrained microcontrollers, providing a practical solution for edge-based passive acoustic monitoring that balances accuracy and computational efficiency.

Abstract: Passive Acoustic Monitoring of bats generates massive ultrasonic datasets (>27 GB/night per node), straining edge storage and battery life. Legacy triggers fail against acoustic confusers, while deep models exceed microcontroller limits. We present a hardware-aware Bat Activity Detector (BAD) specifically designed to discriminate bat calls from hard biological and environmental confusers across variable sampling rates (192-384 kHz). Tailored for the Silicon Labs EFM32PG26 (MVP) in 8-bit integer precision, our model achieves 100 percent hardware offload across all 14 layers (17.2 KB Flash, 73.1 KB RAM). End-to-end preprocessing (74.00 ms for 76 frames) and inference (30.00 ms) of 100 ms clips at 192 kHz require 104.00 ms per clip. On spatially out-of-domain recordings under a realistic low-prevalence regime (r_pos = 0.05), BAD achieves an AUC-ROC of 0.9748 and suppresses 99.4% of non-target noise frames while retaining 65.3% of bat calls - delivering a >33x precision gain over classical Goertzel baselines.

</details>


### [131] [Rate-Agnostic Bioacoustics: Heterogeneous Multi-Taxa Classification with Continuous Filterbanks and Fourier Neural Operators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37540)
*Stefano Ciapponi,Francesco Ardan Dal Rı,Nicola Conci,Elisabetta Farella*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper introduces a Sampling-Frequency-Independent (SFI) frontend combined with a Fourier Neural Operator (FNO) backbone for bioacoustic classification, eliminating the need for fixed-rate resampling and preserving high-frequency information across heterogeneous sampling rates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional bioacoustic classification models rely on fixed-rate spectral representations, requiring recordings at heterogeneous sampling rates to be resampled before analysis, which can cause information loss.

Method: The proposed SFI frontend processes each recording at its native sampling rate, coupled with an FNO backbone featuring progressive temporal-scale fusion. The framework uses mild training-time sampling-rate augmentation to improve robustness to unseen rate variations.

Result: Evaluated on a multi-taxa corpus of 84 classes and 60 sampling rates, the SFI-FNO configuration achieves .906 accuracy, .921 balanced accuracy, and .899 Macro-F1 score, outperforming fixed-rate and corpus-maximum-rate baselines.

Conclusion: The SFI-FNO framework successfully handles variable sampling rates without resampling, preserves high-frequency information, and produces fixed-size representations, demonstrating superior performance across diverse taxa and sampling rates.

Abstract: Conventional bioacoustic classification models rely on fixed-rate spectral representations, requiring recordings acquired at heterogeneous sampling rates to be resampled before analysis. We propose a Sampling-Frequency-Independent (SFI) frontend that processes each recording directly at its native sampling rate, coupled with a Fourier Neural Operator (FNO) backbone featuring progressive temporal-scale fusion. This framework avoids fixed-rate resampling and high-frequency information loss while producing fixed-size representations across sampling rates. Mild training-time sampling-rate (\textit{sr}) augmentation further improves robustness to unseen rate variations. Evaluated on a multi-taxa corpus comprising 84 classes and 60 sampling rates, the proposed SFI-FNO configuration outperforms fixed-rate and corpus-maximum-rate baselines, achieving .906 accuracy, .921 balanced accuracy, and a Macro-F1 score of .899.

</details>


### [132] [Learning as Deepfakes Evolve: RF-Prompt for Continual Audio Deepfake Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37586)
*Yuankun Xie,Xiaoxuan Guo,Xiaopeng Wang,Siqing Qin,Shaole Li,Kong Aik Lee*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper addresses continual audio deepfake detection, where models must learn newly emerging deepfake methods while retaining discrimination of previously encountered speech. The authors propose RAMI protocol for controlled evaluation and RF-Prompt, an asymmetric prompt-learning method that preserves real-speech knowledge through shared prompts and expands fake expert knowledge through orthogonal residuals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing dataset-incremental evaluation changes both real-speech domains and deepfake mechanisms simultaneously, making it difficult to distinguish their individual effects. There was a need for a controlled evaluation protocol that separates these factors and reflects practical deployment scenarios where real speech provides recurring reference while new deepfake mechanisms arrive incrementally.

Method: The authors proposed: 1) RAMI (Real-Anchored Mechanism-Incremental) protocol - a controlled evaluation framework with five task organizations over identical training/development/evaluation pools to study real-speech domains and deepfake mechanisms separately under controlled sample budget. 2) RF-Prompt - an asymmetric continual prompt-learning method that preserves reusable real-speech knowledge through a shared real prompt and expands mechanism-specific knowledge through inherited fake experts with orthogonal residuals. Input-adaptive soft fusion combines accumulated experts into fixed injected tokens without requiring task identity at inference.

Result: RF-Prompt achieves 10.110% average EER and 10.370% pooled EER on RAMI, outperforming all evaluated continual-learning baselines. RAMI yields the lowest common-average and pooled EER across the five controlled protocols. Component ablations, limited-data experiments, and cross-backbone evaluations further validate the proposed design.

Conclusion: The proposed RF-Prompt method with RAMI protocol provides an effective solution for continual audio deepfake detection. The controlled evaluation approach enables better understanding of how real-speech domains and deepfake mechanisms affect detection performance. The method successfully balances learning new deepfake types while retaining knowledge of previously encountered speech.

Abstract: Continual audio deepfake detection requires learning newly emerging deepfake methods while retaining discrimination of previously encountered speech. Existing dataset-incremental evaluation changes both real-speech domains and deepfake mechanisms, making their effects difficult to distinguish. We construct five task organizations over identical training, development, and evaluation pools to study these factors under a controlled sample budget. Our proposed Real-Anchored Mechanism-Incremental (RAMI) protocol reflects the practical setting in which available real speech provides a recurring mixed-domain reference while new deepfake mechanisms arrive incrementally. We further propose RF-Prompt, an asymmetric continual prompt-learning method that preserves reusable real-speech knowledge through a shared real prompt and expands mechanism-specific knowledge through inherited fake experts with orthogonal residuals. Input-adaptive soft fusion combines the accumulated experts into a fixed number of injected tokens without requiring task identity at inference. On RAMI, RF-Prompt achieves 10.110% average EER and 10.370% pooled EER, outperforming all evaluated continual-learning baselines. Across the five controlled protocols, RAMI yields the lowest common-average and pooled EER. Component ablations, limited-data experiments, and cross-backbone evaluations further validate the proposed design.

</details>


### [133] [AS$^2$D: Accelerating On-Demand Audio Understanding on Mobile Devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37617)
*Yunzhe Li,Kyoungjun Park,Hongzi Zhu,Lili Qiu*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: AS²D (Audio Speculative Speculative Decoding) is a new speculative decoding method for audio language models that decouples the drafter from the target model's evolving prefix, enabling parallel drafting and verification by using audio input as the conditioning signal instead of the text prefix.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional speculative decoding couples drafting to the target's evolving verified prefix, which serializes drafting and verification. The authors question whether this dependency is necessary for source-conditioned generation, particularly for audio language models where the input audio can provide useful speculative candidates.

Method: AS²D enables target-decoupled drafting: an audio-conditioned drafter follows its own generation history while the target independently verifies and corrects ready candidates. When no usable candidates exist, the target advances alone. This allows concurrent drafting and verification while retaining target-side verification and correction.

Result: Implemented in MNN for Android, evaluated on two target models across four phones, seven datasets, and three tasks (12.2 hours of audio). AS²D improves pooled ASR throughput by 42-76% over target-only decoding. Only 5.7% of evaluation windows are slower than target-only (vs 58.1-63.0% for speculative baselines). Achieves 97.33-98.20% of oracle's pooled throughput. Native on-demand execution with 7B target achieves up to 78% higher throughput than target-only.

Conclusion: Source-conditioned audio generation can relax the conventional dependence of speculative drafting on the target's evolving output prefix, exposing substantial parallelism for efficient inference.

Abstract: Speculative decoding accelerates autoregressive generation by using a smaller drafter to propose tokens for batched verification by a larger target. However, conventional speculative decoding couples drafting to the target's evolving verified prefix, serializing drafting and verification. We ask whether this dependency is necessary for source-conditioned generation. Our key observation is that, for audio language models, the input audio and user request can provide useful speculative candidates without following the target's evolving text prefix. We propose AS$^2$D (Audio Speculative Speculative Decoding), which enables target-decoupled drafting: an audio-conditioned drafter follows its own generation history while the target independently verifies and corrects ready candidates. Without usable candidates, the target advances alone. Thus, target feedback determines which candidates are committed but no longer determines when the drafter can make progress, enabling drafting and verification to proceed concurrently while retaining target-side verification and correction. We implement AS$^2$D in MNN for Android and evaluate two target models across four phones, seven datasets, and three tasks covering 12.2 hours of audio. Across four phones, AS$^2$D improves pooled ASR throughput by 42-76% over target-only decoding, while only 5.7% of evaluation windows are slower than target-only, compared with 58.1-63.0% for speculative baselines. For ASR, AS$^2$D reaches 97.33-98.20% of a hindsight per-window oracle's pooled throughput over the evaluated drafter/budget catalog. Native on-demand execution with a 7B target achieves up to 78% higher throughput than target-only. These results show that source-conditioned audio generation can relax the conventional dependence of speculative drafting on the target's evolving output prefix, exposing substantial parallelism for efficient inference.

</details>


### [134] [Do Music Generative Models Understand Musical Qualities? Automatic Music Evaluation with Model-Intrinsic Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37710)
*Xiaosha Li,Chun Liu,Ziyu Wang*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates that intrinsic signals from music generation models (MusicGen) — including prediction loss, prediction entropy, and concepts extracted via sparse autoencoder (SAE) — strongly correlate with human music quality ratings, with SAE latents carrying the most predictive power.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous approaches using likelihood for music evaluation failed, necessitating supervised evaluation models. This work investigates whether a model's internal signals can inherently predict human perception of music quality, addressing whether generative models "understand" their outputs.

Method: The authors use MusicGen and extract three feature types: (1) prediction loss, (2) prediction entropy, and (3) SAE-extracted concepts from hidden representations. They train a lightweight prediction model using these features (individually and combined) to estimate subjective ratings from human evaluations on five benchmarks spanning continuous ratings and pairwise preferences.

Result: All three intrinsic signals correlated with human ratings. SAE latents carried the strongest predictive signal. The hypothesis that these signals parallel human listening processes (loss/entropy reflecting expectation and surprise; SAE gradients predicting perceived quality) was confirmed.

Conclusion: Music generative models possess intrinsic signals that align with human music quality evaluation, suggesting these models have some understanding of musical qualities. SAE-extracted concepts are the most effective predictor of human judgments.

Abstract: Current music generative models can produce high-quality music, but does this ability imply that they ``understand'' the musical qualities of their outputs, and is that understanding aligned with human evaluation? Previous attempts to use the likelihood of a generative model to evaluate music, an approach commonly used in text, have proven unsuccessful, leading researchers to rely on standalone supervised music evaluation models. In this paper, we answer this question affirmatively: we show that a model's intrinsic signals---derived from its hidden representations and predictions---are strongly correlated with human ratings. In particular, we study MusicGen and consider three types of features: (1) prediction loss, (2) prediction entropy, and (3) concepts extracted from the model using a sparse autoencoder (SAE). Using these features, we train a lightweight prediction model to estimate subjective ratings. We evaluate these features both individually and in combination. We hypothesize that these signals parallel the listening process: the temporal and frequency-domain structure of loss and entropy reflects listeners' expectation and surprise, while gradient directions in SAE latent space predict perceived quality. Experiments on five human-evaluation benchmarks spanning continuous ratings and pairwise preferences confirm this hypothesis, with SAE latents carrying most of the predictive signal.

</details>


### [135] [2-Dimensional spectral gating for denoising bioacoustics recordings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37910)
*Julien Boussard,Mélisande Teng,Sulagna Saha,Mario Gallego-Abenza*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper proposes an improved noise reduction method for bioacoustics recordings, extending the popular Noisereduce tool by leveraging the multi-frequency structure characteristic of animal sounds. The method was tested on bird and marine mammal recordings, showing improved denoising performance without sacrificing processing speed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bioacoustics recordings in open environments face variable vocalizations, noise, and signal-to-noise ratios across individuals, species, environments, and recording conditions. Existing methods like Noisereduce use spectral gating but may not fully exploit the structured nature of animal sounds across multiple frequencies, limiting their robustness and generalizability for ecological analyses.

Method: The authors extended Noisereduce by incorporating the fact that most animal sounds have structure across multiple frequencies. The improved method was applied to bird and marine mammal recordings for evaluation.

Result: The proposed extension of Noisereduce achieved improved denoising performance on both terrestrial (bird) and underwater (marine mammal) acoustic recordings, while maintaining comparable preprocessing speed to the original method.

Conclusion: The multi-frequency structure of animal vocalizations can be leveraged to enhance noise reduction in bioacoustics recordings. This improvement maintains computational efficiency while providing better denoising across different recording environments.

Abstract: Isolating vocalizations from noise in bioacoustics recordings is a prerequisite to many ecological analyses, including species identification, animal communication understanding, and individual or population-level variability studies. However, when recordings are acquired in open environments, vocalizations, noise, or signal-to-noise ratio can vary widely across individuals, species, environment, and recording conditions, making it hard to develop robust and generalizable methods for ecological analyses. To account for these challenges, noise reduction techniques are used to remove noise before downstream analyses. Popular methods such as Noisereduce rely on spectral gating, which estimates a noise threshold for each frequency channel.
